Spectral Networks and Betti Lagrangians
Roger Casals, Yoon Jae Nho

Abstract
. We introduce and develop the theory of spectral networks in real contact and symplectic topology. First, we establish the existence and pseudoholomorphic characterization of spectral networks for Lagrangian fillings in the cotangent bundle of a smooth surface. These are proven via analytic results on the adiabatic degeneration of Floer trajectories and the explicit computation of continuation strips. Second, we construct a Family Floer functor for Lagrangian fillings endowed with a spectral network and prove its equivalence to the non-abelianization functor. In particular, this implies that both the framed 2d-4d BPS states and the Gaiotto-Moore-Neitzke non-abelianized parallel transport are realized as part of the A ∞ A_{\infty} -operations of the associated 4d partially wrapped Fukaya categories. To conclude, we present a new construction relating spectral networks and Lagrangian fillings using Demazure weaves, and show the precise relation between spectral networks and augmentations of the Legendrian contact dg-algebra.



1.
Introduction

The object of this article is to develop the theory of spectral networks within the context of real contact and symplectic topology. First, we provide foundational results on the existence and characterization of spectral networks for Lagrangian fillings in the cotangent bundle of a smooth surface. In particular, we characterize the walls of spectral networks in terms of pseudoholomorphic strips. These are proven via new results on the adiabatic degeneration of Floer trajectories, the behavior of the associated flowlines, and explicit computations of continuation strips. Second, we construct a Family Floer functor for Betti Lagrangians endowed with a spectral network and show that the non-abelian parallel transport in Gaiotto-Moore-Neitzke’s supersymmetric
N=4
context is both generalized and made rigorous by this Floer-theoretic parallel transport. Third, the article brings forth new constructions and applications relating spectral networks and Lagrangian fillings, including the study of spectral networks from Demazure weaves, their relation to the Legendrian contact dg-algebra, and an interpretation of framed 2d-4d BPS states and the non-abelianization maps as part of the
A_{\infty}
-operations and module categories of partially wrapped Fukaya categories.

A central theme of this work is that spectral networks can be studied and characterized in terms of the Floer theory of real Lagrangians in the real cotangent bundle of a smooth surface. This allows for a key generalization of the standard physical theory of spectral networks, which itself focuses on meromorphic spectral curves in the holomorphic cotangent bundle of a Riemann surface. This generalization is needed to define and study the Betti moduli space in the wild case for smooth asymptotic data, not necessarily meromorphic Stokes data. In particular, it allows us to tackle asymptotics associated to any positive braid, including and generalizing all braid varieties, and perform computations on their associated cluster structures, BPS monodromy and Donaldson-Thomas theory. Indeed, the Floer-theoretic framework and results we establish provide a precise bridge to apply the new developments on cluster algebras in 4-dimensional symplectic topology to the study of spectral networks.

The introduction of spectral networks in mathematical physics has lead to an outstanding number of breakthroughs in string theory. The present work also serves the purpose of establishing a rigorous mathematical foundation for many beautiful insights and claims from the physics community, in our case within the context of contact and symplectic topology. A number of such conjectured properties and examples are discussed and proven throughout this manuscript, including the characterization of spectral networks in terms of pseudo-holomorphic disks and the study of higher-order Stokes phenomena, including the Berk-Nevins-Roberts example and beyond.

1.1.
Scientific context

In 2012, within the context of supersymmetric gauge theories in mathematical physics, D. Gaiotto, G. Moore and A. Neitzke introduced and developed the study of spectral networks in
[
GMN13
,
GMN13a
,
GMN14
]
. These contributions to physical theories of class
S
allowed for the computation of different BPS degeneracies in the associated 4-dimensional
N=2
theories (e.g. coupling to surface defects) and a better understanding of how the wall-crossing phenomenon in coupled 2d-4d systems
[
GMN12
,
GMN13b
]
related to the BPS spectrum. Since then, an abundance of exciting work illustrates that spectral networks are part of a fertile and productive ground for mathematical physics. To wit, further explorations of different types of BPS states were achieved in
[
Gab+17
,
GLM15
,
LP16
,
LP17
,
Lon18
]
and
[
DMN20
,
Erg+21
,
HN17
,
HHN20
]
, non-abelianization and quantum holonomies were studied in
[
Gab17
,
HN16
,
HN20
,
KLS23
,
NY20
]
and see also the surveys
[
Nei14
,
Nei21
]
.

In pure mathematics, spectral networks have started to be used in a variety of areas, including exact WKB analysis
[
Ale+24
,
GHN23
,
IK22
,
IK23
]
(see also the preceding works
[
AKT01
,
HLO04
]
), the study of moduli of Higgs bundles and their Hitchin fibrations
[
Dum+21
,
FN21
,
Kat+15
,
Wil16
]
, and that of spaces of stability conditions
[
BS15
,
HKS24
,
Smi15
]
; see also
[
FN24
,
Kuw24
,
Nho24
,
Sue24
]
. Throughout these contributions, there is a focus towards the case
G=\mbox{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, where the spectral curve is a 2-fold branch cover of the base. This case is rather well-understood by now, in part due to its direct relation to the theory of quadratic differentials on Riemann surfaces and the combinatorics of their associated foliations. The higher rank case, for
G=\mbox{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, remains an interesting subject of exploration, cf.
[
Kat+15
,
Smi21
]
, and a main focus of this article.

In the
\mbox{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
case, a first connection between the Floer theory of real Lagrangian surfaces and framed 2d-4d BPS states was hinted by the first author in
[
CM18
]
, being further developed in
[
Nho24
]
by the second author. Subsequently, the introduction of Legendrian weaves
[
CZ22
]
has provided key insights to establish the general connection between Floer theory and spectral networks in all higher ranks, significantly beyond the theory of quadratic differentials. Equipped with new tools in symplectic topology
[
CL22
,
CZ22
,
GPS20
,
GPS24a
]
, the present manuscript now greatly generalizes the results in
[
CM18
,
Nho24
]
to arbitrary rank, and crystallizes a number of further insights relating spectral networks to both partially wrapped Fukaya categories
[
GPS20
,
GPS24a
,
Syl19
]
and the construction of cluster structures on braid varieties
[
Cas+24
,
Cas+25
,
CW24
]
. From the Betti perspective on wild character varieties, a key difference between rank
n=2
and higher rank spectral networks is that, in the latter case, asymptotics are not constrained to be meromorphic. Namely, the putative spectral curves can have asymptotics dictated by non-algebraic links, and thus they are not necessarily associated to meromorphic curves. Thus far, all higher rank explorations in the literature focus on the algebraic case, on the so-called WKB spectral networks, where the spectral curve is a holomorphic subvariety of the holomorphic cotangent bundle of a Riemann surface. The results we present go much beyond such algebraic constraints.

A key conceptual advance of this article is that the technique of spectral networks also works for real Lagrangian fillings of Legendrian links, regardless of whether the Lagrangian surface and their asymptotic Legendrian links have algebraic origin or not. For this, we introduce a more general definition of spectral network than
[
GMN13a
]
, where only an almost complex structure is used and the necessary analytical properties of pseudo-holomorphic strips and their adiabatic limits can be proven. An advantage of intertwining spectral networks with some of the current tools from real contact and symplectic topology is that structural results known within the latter translate into theorems for spectral networks. An instance of this is the invariance of BPS soliton classes under real Hamiltonian isotopies, and not just holomorphic deformations. Another example is the functorial behavior of Floer-theoretic invariants for Legendrian links under Lagrangian cobordisms, which readily explains how to glue and compute spectral networks associated to Demazure weaves. These new classes of spectral networks, built from Demazure weaves, include and generalize many guiding examples in the literature and allow for direct computations of the BPS monodromy generator by using explicit reddening sequences from their associated cluster algebras.

1.2.
Main results

Let
{\bf S}
be a smooth closed oriented surface and
\boldsymbol{m}\subset{\bf S}
a finite collection of marked points. A triple
({\bf S},\boldsymbol{m};{\bf\Lambda})
is said to be a Betti surface of rank
n
if
{\bf\Lambda}
is a collection of Legendrian links in the ideal contact boundary
(T^{\infty}S,\xi_{\text{st}})
, each of which is associated to an
n
-stranded positive braid over the Legendrian fiber of a marked point in
\boldsymbol{m}
. Let us denote by
S\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}={\bf S}\setminus\boldsymbol{m}
the complement of the marked points. By depicting the wavefronts of the links in
{\bf\Lambda}
onto
S\subset{\bf S}
, a Betti surface is pictorially drawn as in Figure
1
.

Figure 1
.
A Betti surface
{\bf S}
of genus
3
and rank
3
, with 9 marked points
\boldsymbol{m}=\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{9}\}
, in orange, and their associated Legendrian links in
{\bf\Lambda}
, in blue. The Legendrian links associated to marked points
m_{1},m_{5},m_{8}
have concentric circles as their fronts, which corresponds to a regular singularity. A non-concentric Legendrian isotopy class corresponds to an irregular singularity.

The Legendrian links in
\Lambda
are the contact topological incarnation of the Stokes data from the theory of irregular singularities of meromorphic connections, cf.
[
KKP08
, Remark 2.17]
. In particular, any meromorphic spectral curve in the sense of
[
GMN13a
]
yields a well-defined Betti surface of the corresponding rank, where the fronts of the Legendrians in
\Lambda
are the associated Stokes diagrams. Details are provided in Section
2
, cf. also
[
Boa21
,
She+19
]
and
[
Su25
, Appendix B]
. At core, a Betti Lagrangian
L\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
is a Lagrangian submanifold whose projection onto
S
is an
n
-fold branched cover and whose asymptotics are dictated by the Legendrian links in
{\bf\Lambda}
; this is detailed in Definition
2.6
. There are two important classes of such Betti Lagrangians, both discussed in
Section

2.3
:

(i)

Exact Betti Lagrangians, where the restriction of the Liouville form
\lambda_{\text{st}}
to
L
is an exact 1-form, i.e. all real periods of
\lambda_{\text{st}}
on
L
vanish. This class is defined with
S
a smooth punctured surface and it yields Lagrangian fillings
L\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
of the Legendrian link given by the union of all Legendrian links
\Lambda
, after cylindrization near the contact boundary; cf.
Section

6.3
and
[
CG22
,
CG24
,
CZ22
,
EHK16
]
.

(ii)

If
S
is endowed with a Riemann surface structure
\mathbb{S}
, we can consider meromorphic Betti Lagrangians. These are the Betti Lagrangians that appear as the real part of a holomorphic Lagrangian curve
\Sigma\subset(\mathbb{T}^{*}\mathbb{S},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
in the holomorphic cotangent bundle
(\mathbb{T}^{*}\mathbb{S},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
with asymptotics dictated by the irregular classes at
\boldsymbol{m}
. These are the spectral curves studied in the literature, including those in
[
GMN13
,
GMN13a
,
GMN14
]
and those featuring in the irregular Riemann-Hilbert correspondence, see e.g.
[
BB04
,
Boa21
]
.

The theory of spectral networks in
[
GMN13
,
GMN13a
,
GMN14
]
is devoted to Betti Lagrangians of type
(ii)
, in Riemann surfaces
\mathbb{S}
. The resulting spectral networks satisfy a number of additional properties, implied by the underlying holomorphicity: we refer to those spectral networks in
[
GMN13
,
GMN13a
,
GMN14
]
as WKB spectral networks. In order to develop the theory of spectral networks in the real setting of
(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
, so as to include the important class
(i)
above, Section
3
introduces a generalization of those WKB spectral networks, which we refer to as a Morse spectral networks. In a nutshell, a Morse spectral network
\EuScript{W}\subset S
compatible with
L
is a set of gradient flow trajectories in
S
given by the difference functions of the sheets of
L\subset T^{*}S
above
S
satisfying a set of non-trivial
interaction and asymptotic constraints
. These constraints exclude a number of pathological behaviors. For instance, it is proven in
Section

3
that Morse spectral networks do not allow for ill-behaved asymptotics at infinity, such as trajectories reaching
\boldsymbol{m}
in finite time, nor trajectories getting arbitrarily close to
\boldsymbol{m}
and then steering away from
\boldsymbol{m}
. In fact, trajectories should converge to Reeb chords of
\Lambda
, which generalize the points of maximal decay in Stokes diagrams. In the interior, Morse spectral networks also disallow for high-valence vertex interactions and tangencies between trajectories and sink points inside of
S
, among others. In general, these are all possible behaviors for such difference-function gradient trajectories that occur without a priori constraints on the geometry of
L
. In addition, a Morse spectral network must have a compatible counting function
\mu
on such trajectories, generalizing the count of framed 2d-4d BPS states.
1
1

1

This is in line with the 4d BPS invariants of
[
Bri19
, Definition 2.1]
. Nevertheless, we do not consider a central charge in this general real setting; that might be the focus of future work.
These interaction constraints and the necessary properties of
\mu
, both crucial to the concept of a Morse spectral network, are specified in Section
3.2
: a depiction of the key allowed local models for a Morse spectral network
\EuScript{W}
is in Figure
2
.

Figure 2
.
The local models for vertices in spectral networks: initial, interaction and non-interaction. There are two types of interaction vertices: creation and 6-valent type. The inconsistent vertex type is allowed for pre-spectral networks, but is
not
allowed for spectral networks.

A.
The first natural question that arises is the existence of such Morse spectral networks compatible with an exact Betti Lagrangian
L\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
. A main difficulty is that the gradient flow trajectories given by the sheets of
L
over
S
might not satisfy the necessary asymptotic and interaction constraints. Also, it might not be clear how to generally assign the counting function on trajectories given an arbitrary such Lagrangian
L\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
. Similarly, in the holomorphic setting, it has not been established that a finite-energy WKB spectral network compatible with a given a meromorphic Betti Lagrangian
\Sigma\subset(\mathbb{T}^{*}\mathbb{S},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
actually does exist. In practice, it is also important to guarantee that there are finitely many trajectories in a spectral network, given an energy cut-off (or equivalently, a mass cut-off in physics literature). In fact, due to its conceptual importance, we include this condition into the definition of the WKB spectral network. The first result in this manuscript resolves the matter of existence, including the (energy)-finiteness constraint:

Theorem 1

(
Existence of Spectral Networks
)
.

Let
S
be a Betti surface.

(i)

Let
L\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
be an exact Betti Lagrangian and
(S,g)
a metric adapted to
L
.

Then there exists a finite Morse spectral network compatible with
L
and
(S,g)
.

(ii)

Let
\mathbb{S}
a Riemann surface structure for
S
,
(\mathbb{T}^{*}\mathbb{S},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
its holomorphic cotangent bundle, and
\Sigma\subset(\mathbb{T}^{*}\mathbb{S},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
a meromorphic spectral curve with
O(-1)
ends. Then, for dense subset of phases
\theta\in S^{1}
,
there exists a generic WKB spectral network compatible with the real Lagrangian
e^{i\theta}\Sigma\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
.

In the statement of Theorem
1
, the dense set of phases in
S^{1}
is a countable intersection of open dense sets. The notation
e^{i\theta}\Sigma
stands for the real part of the holomorphic subvariety
\Sigma
after being acted by a rotation of phase
\theta
, see Section
2
for details. In the real case
(i)
, our proof of Theorem
1
actually yields a compatible Morse spectral network whose only interaction vertices are creation vertices, which is in itself an interesting difference with the WKB case. For the proof of Theorem
1
, presented in
Section

3
, both the trapping lemma in Section
2.5
and the preliminary transversality established in Section
3.3.1
are important technical aspects, controlling the behavior of flow trajectories and ensuring necessary asymptotics and interaction constraints. The proof of Theorem
1
in the WKB case
(ii)
nicely intertwines with the heuristic construction proposed in
[
GMN13a
]
: indeed, we prove that their proposed procedure gives an algorithm that both terminates and outputs an object which is indeed a WKB spectral network, satisfying the energy-finiteness condition, or rather, a stronger condition that we call
gapped
(c.f
Definition

3.12
). In this latter WKB case, see also
[
Kuw24
, Theorem 1.2]
, which discusses the construction of certain WKB spectral networks via sheaf quantization. Note that in the WKB case
(ii)
, the real Lagrangian
e^{i\theta}\Sigma\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
is typically not an exact Lagrangian, as the periods of
\Sigma
might not all have the same phase
\theta+\pi/2
.

B.
Floer theory, broadly understood as the study of symplectic topology through pseudo-holomorphic methods, infinite-dimensional variational analysis on path spaces and their homological incarnations, is a pillar of contact and symplectic topology. See for instance
[
AS19
,
EGH00
,
Flo88
,
Flo89
,
Gro85
,
PSS96
,
Vit99
]
and the monographs
[
Fuk+09
,
Fuk+09a
,
MS04
,
Sei08a
]
, to name some key references. The context for Theorem
1
certainly belongs to real symplectic topology: a real Lagrangian submanifold
L
inside the cotangent bundle
(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
. Yet, spectral networks
\EuScript{W}\subset S
are defined in terms of gradient trajectories on
S
: even if they are trajectories of functions describing
L
, there are no immediate Floer-theoretic aspects to the notion of a spectral network. Based on examples, e.g. when certain 4d BPS states correspond to holomorphic disks
[
GMN13a
, Section 6.2]
, it has been hoped by the mathematical physics community that spectral networks
\EuScript{W}\subset S
can in fact be given a characterization entirely in Floer-theoretic terms. Given the effectiveness of Floer theory and all its known functorial properties, establishing a rigorous connection between Floer theory and spectral networks seems particularly desirable from the viewpoint of pure mathematics as well. Our second result proves such characterization:

Theorem 2

(
Floer-theoretic characterization of Spectral Networks
)
.

Let
S
be a Betti surface,
\Sigma\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
a Betti Lagrangian and
\EuScript{W}\subset S
a spectral network compatible with
\Sigma
. Then the following characterization holds:

A point
p\in S
belongs to an active wall of
\EuScript{W}

\Longleftrightarrow

\exists
broken pseudo-holomorphic strip in
(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})

between the fiber
T^{*}_{p}S
and
\Sigma
in the adiabatic limit.

Furthermore, the following two properties are satisfied:

(i)

If
p\in\EuScript{W}
, then the relative homology class in
\Sigma
associated any such pseudo-holomorphic strip is uniquely determined by
\EuScript{W}
, and such strip must adiabatically degenerate to a subset of
\EuScript{W}
.

(ii)

For
\Sigma
an exact Betti Lagrangian, the characterization holds for (genuine) pseudoholomorphic strips, without being potentially broken.

When
\Sigma
is non-exact, we demand
\Sigma
to be meromorphic outside some compact subset of
T^{\ast}S
, for some technical reasons. All known interesting examples of non-exact Betti Lagrangians fall into such a category.
A first important aspect of Theorem
2
is the presence of an adiabatic limit: it refers to the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
when the Betti Lagrangian
\Sigma\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
is rescaled by a factor of
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
under the fiberwise radial scaling action of the cotangent bundle.
2
2

2

In the literature in geometric analysis, adiabatic limit refers to the process of degenerating a Riemannian metric in certain directions. In symplectic topology, one typically starts with the symplectic 2-form and a compatible almost complex structure, from which a Riemannian metric is produced. The fiberwise rescaling action can be seen as a rescaling of the almost complex structure, which itself is then seen as rescaling the metric. In this case, the limit metrics when
\varepsilon\to 0
degenerate to be supported in the zero section of
T^{*}S
.
Since even in the simplest cases pseudo-holomorphic strips are in bijective correspondence with flow-trees only in the adiabatic limit, the adiabatic hypothesis in
Theorem

2
is needed in any such characterization. A second relevant fact is that, for exact Betti Lagrangians, all the walls of the spectral networks produced in
Theorem

1
are active. Therefore
Theorem

2
gives a complete characterization of points in a spectral network, without even considering framed 2d-4d BPS indices.

Appropriately understood, Theorem
2
generalizes the analysis relating flow-trees and pseudo-holomorphic strips from
[
Ekh07
]
. The result in ibid. are nevertheless far from concluding
Theorem

2
: a key new ingredient is establishing the convergence from pseudo-holomorphic strips to trees while satisfying the initial edge constraints, which is crucial to control the behavior of
D_{4}^{-}
-trees near branch points. This convergence result is proven in
Section

4
. In addition, the flowtree results in the literature do not apply to non-exact Lagrangians (and so would not be able to deal with any of
[
GMN13a
,
GMN13
,
GMN14
]
) and also require generic swallowtails front singularities. Since
D_{4}^{-}
-singularities are necessarily present in a branch cover and the study of spectral curves, we also further develop the machinery of Morse flowtrees for this context.

Theorem
2
is proven at the end of
Section

5
, after developing the necessary analytical results in the bulk of Sections
4
and
5
. At core, it is the combination of two different of results. First, we establish no-go theorems in Section
4
, cf. Theorem
4.2
, using adiabatic degeneration. We are able to use the adiabatic limit to show that certain pseudoholomorphic strips do
not
exist, as stated in Theorem
2
: it allows us to prove that points in the complement of the spectral networks cannot support such a pseudoholomorphic strip and that, for any point in the spectral network
\EuScript{W}
, the only possible homology class for a potential pseudoholomorphic strip through that point must be the soliton class determined by
\EuScript{W}
and the point. Second, we prove that there exist such pseudo-holomorphic strips for points in
\EuScript{W}
, as stated in Theorem
2
, through the explicit computations established in Section
5
.

C.
In Theorems
1
and
2
, the more foundational aspects of existence and characterizations are addressed. A central aspect of spectral networks in the supersymmetric theories of
[
GMN13a
]
is the non-abelianization functor, cf.
[
GMN12
, Section 10]
and
[
GMN14
, Section 5]
. In the supersymmetric
N=4
context, it is a construction from the infrared to the ultraviolet that carries
\mbox{GL}_{1}(\mathbb{C})
-local systems on the Seiberg-Witten curve to
\mbox{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
-local system on the space of ultraviolet parameters. In topological terms, each spectral network
\EuScript{W}\subset S
partially defines a functor
\Phi{W}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(L)\dashrightarrow\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(S)

from rank-
n
twisted local systems on
L
to rank-
1
twisted local systems in
S
,
i.e. from a Betti Lagrangian
L\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
to the zero section
S
. The functor
\Phi{W}
is constructed combinatorially from
\EuScript{W}
, introducing detour paths that act as instanton corrections and abide by formulas similar to the Hori-Vafa wall-crossing formulas. Technically, given
V\in\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(L)
, the physics framework is such that
\Phi{W}(V)
has parallel transport only defined for paths transverse to
\EuScript{W}
and endpoints in the complement
\EuScript{W}^{c}
, thus our use of the dashed arrows in
\Phi{W}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(L)\dashrightarrow\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(S)
, cf.
Section

5.1
.

In this paper we develop Floer theory for exact Betti Lagrangians in the presence of a spectral network
\EuScript{W}
, in
Section

5
, constructing Floer-type cochain complexes with differentials recording certain counts of (actual non-adiabatic) pseudo-holomorphic disks. As a consequence, we are able to construct an actual functor
\mathbb{F}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(L)\longrightarrow\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(S)
using Family Floer techniques. The functor
\mathbb{F}
is defined for all paths and built as a direct limit of functors
\mathbb{F}_{\varepsilon}
depending on the adiabatic parameter. This is presented in
Section

5.2
; here
\mathbb{F}
stands both for Family and for Floer. Then we establish the comparison between these two functors in
Section

5.3
. The results can be summarized as follows:

Theorem 3

(
Family Floer and Non-abelianization
)
.

Let
S
be a Betti surface,
L\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
an exact Betti Lagrangian and
\EuScript{W}\subset S
a spectral network compatible with
\Sigma
. Then:

(1)

Family Floer with respect to
L
can be defined and yields a functor

\mathbb{F}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(L)\longrightarrow\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(S),

invariant under compactly supported Hamiltonian isotopies of
L
. Specifically, it is built from a type of Floer complexes
CF^{\ast}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L)
between cotangent fibers and
L
, which can be constructed and shown to be well-defined.

(2)

In the adiabatic limit
\varepsilon\to 0
, the two functors

\mathbb{F},\Phi{W}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(\varepsilon L)\longrightarrow\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(S),

are equivalent.

As said above, the Floer-theoretic functor
\mathbb{F}
in
Theorem

3
.(1) is defined as a direct limit of Family Floer functors
\mathbb{F}_{\varepsilon}
that depends on the adiabatic parameter
\varepsilon
: at core,
\mathbb{F}
uses the spectral network
\EuScript{W}
in order to correct the technical issues that often obstruct when applying Family Floer methods, including caustics. The comparison in
Theorem

3
.(2) does require scaling the Betti Lagrangian and thus the comparison
\mathbb{F}=\Phi{W}
only makes sense in the adiabatic limit.
Theorem

3
is proven in
Section

5.2
for Part (1) and
Section

5.3
for Part (2). Note that the case of quadratic differentials, with
\mbox{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, can be argued from
[
Nho24
]
, whereas
Theorem

3
works for any higher rank exact Betti Lagrangians. We emphasize that the functor
\mathbb{F}
is well-defined whereas, technically,
\Phi{W}
cannot parallel transport starting at
\EuScript{W}
:
Theorem

3
.(2) is thus an equality where
\Phi{W}
is defined. More conceptually, it shows how the framework of Floer theory not only captures but generalizes that of spectral networks: the former being able to be used to define and extend the latter. An interesting question is whether
Theorem

3
could be extended to general Betti Lagrangians. For such Lagrangians, we are currently only able to explicitly compute parallel transport maps for very short line segments. Such a computation is the key component behind the proof of
Theorem

2
. We leave this question for the future research.

A corollary of
Theorem

3
is that we can extract a symplectic invariant of the exact Betti Lagrangians
L\subset T^{*}S
from
\EuScript{W}
, up to compactly supported Hamiltonian isotopies. Indeed,
Theorem

3
implies that the set of soliton classes in
\EuScript{W}
associated to Reeb chords of
\partial_{\infty}L
is an invariant of
L
. This is proven in
Theorem

5.8
.
3
3

3

It is an effective invariant, as it at least matches the information contained in the augmentation induced by
L
for the Legendrian contact dg-algebra of
\partial_{\infty}L
, when the latter is defined. Thus it already distinguishes Lagrangians before and after a surgery.
Note that the cluster algebra structures built in
[
Cas+25
]
, corresponding to a Betti surface
S=S^{2}
with one irregular singularity, have cluster variables defined by Floer parallel transport along relative cycles. In combination with that work,
Theorem

3
then formalizes the cluster-like coordinates sketched in the supersymmetric context in
[
GMN13a
, Section 10]
and
[
Nei14
]
.

D.
Fukaya
A_{\infty}
-categories, in their many forms, are a central object in Floer theory and the study of symplectic topology via pseudo-holomorphic methods, see e.g.
[
Fuk+09
,
GPS20
,
Sei08a
]
. The Floer complexes we constructed for
Theorem

3
and the results developed in Sections
4
and
5
can be enhanced to an
A_{\infty}
-categorical level. This is achieved in
Section

6
, as follows:

(i)

First, due to its asymptotic ends, an exact Betti Lagrangian
L\subset T^{*}S
does not define an object of
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
or any partially wrapped version thereof. That said, we construct in
Section

6.1
an
A_{\infty}
-module
\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}
of
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
that behaves as if it was the Yoneda module for such an object. More crucially, we then prove in
Section

6.2
that its
\mu_{1|1}

A_{\infty}
-operation precisely captures the non-abelian parallel transport.

(ii)

Second, in
Section

6.3
we geometrically modify
L
via cylindrization so as to obtain a Lagrangian
L_{\circ}
which defines an object in the partially wrapped Fukaya category
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
. We also establish that its associated
\mu_{2}

A_{\infty}
-map recovers non-abelian parallel transport, now in the partially wrapped case.

(iii)

Third, we compare the
A_{\infty}
-modules presented in (i) and (ii) above under the inclusion functor
i_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)\to\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
and completely describe the Yoneda
A_{\infty}
-module of
L_{\circ}
. This description of this
A_{\infty}
-module of
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
associated to the cylindrized Betti Lagrangian
L_{\circ}
is achieved in
Section

6.4
by proving a generation result for
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
, cf.
Proposition

6.19
, showing that its generated by internal and infinity fibers, without linking disks being in the generating set.

These are all established in detail in
Section

6
. See
Proposition

6.1
,
Theorem

6.3
and
Corollary

6.4
for (i),
Section

6.3.1
and
Theorem

6.12
for (ii), and
Corollary

6.13
and
Proposition

6.19
for (iii). We summarize some of the key outcomes in the following result:

Theorem 4

(
Spectral curves and 4d Partially Wrapped Fukaya Categories
)
.

Let
L\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
be an exact Betti Lagrangian endowed with a local system
V\in\mbox{Loc}(L)
and
\EuScript{W}\subset S
a compatible Morse spectral network. Then:

(i)

There exists an
A_{\infty}
-module

\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)\longrightarrow\mbox{mod}_{k}
with cohomologies

H^{\ast}(\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}(P))\cong HF^{\ast}(P,(L,V)),

where
P\subset T^{*}S
is any exact cylindrical Lagrangian with compact horizontal support. Furthermore, the minimal geodesic generator
[\alpha_{zw}]\in\mbox{Hom}_{\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,T^{\ast}_{w}S)
of an
\EuScript{W}
-adapted pair
z,w\in S
satisfies

\mu_{1|1}([\alpha_{zw}],\cdot)=\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}(V)(\alpha_{zw}).

In particular, there exists an
A_{\infty}
-quasi-equivalence
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)\mbox{-mod}\cong C_{-\ast}(\Omega_{z}S)
-mod,

\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}\simeq\Phi{W}(V),

as
A_{\infty}
-modules over
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
.

(ii)

Consider the Yoneda
A_{\infty}
-module
\EuScript{Y}_{(L_{\circ},V)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)\longrightarrow\mbox{mod}_{k}
of the cylindrized Betti Lagrangian
L_{\circ}\subset T^{*}S
and its
\mu_{2}

A_{\infty}
-operation. Then the minimal geodesic
\alpha_{zw}\in\Omega_{z,w}
satisfies

\mu_{2}(i_{\ast}[\alpha_{zw}],\cdot)=\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}(V)(\alpha_{zw})

and there is a homotopy

\EuScript{Y}_{(L_{\circ},V)}\circ i_{\ast}\simeq\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}

of
A_{\infty}
-modules over
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
.

At core,
Theorem

4
establishes that the non-abelian parallel transport of Gaiotto-Moore-Neitzke studied in
[
GMN12
,
GMN13a
,
GMN14
]
, and the associated counts of framed 2d-4d BPS states, can be realized as parts of the
A_{\infty}
-operations in either wrapped or partially wrapped 4-dimensional Fukaya categories.
Theorem

4
.(i) does so in the wrapped category
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
and
Theorem

4
.(ii) in the partially wrapped category
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
, also establishing the comparison. At a technical level, we use in
Section

6
the modern construction of partially wrapped categories in
[
GPS20
]
and establish our results by indeed using their definitions in terms of homotopy colimits, carefully defining the class associated to a minimal geodesic
\alpha_{zw}
by studying the necessary continuation maps, cf. Sections
6.2
and
6.3.2
.

E.
Theorems
1
and
2
establish existence and characterizations of spectral networks, and Theorems
3
and
4
develop the Floer-theoretic results needed to rigorously define and establish non-abelianization in spectral networks, first analytically and then categorically, respectively. The last result we present is a new construction of spectral networks, providing both novel examples and crystallizing connections to cluster algebras.

The input of the construction is a Legendrian weave
\mathfrak{w}\subset S
, as introduced in
[
CZ22
]
, specifically a Demazure weave, cf.
Section

7.1
,
[
Cas+24
, Section 4.1]
or
[
Cas+25
, Section 4.1]
. Such a weave
\mathfrak{w}\subset S
defines an exact Betti Lagrangian
L_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
with asymptotics determined by a Legendrian
\Lambda_{\partial\mathfrak{w}}\subset(T^{\infty}S,\xi_{\text{st}})
, depending only on the boundary
\partial\mathfrak{w}
of the weave. In
Section

7.2
we define a certain object
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset S
, associated to a weave
\mathfrak{w}
, entirely in combinatorial terms: we refer to it as the
augmentation forest
of
\mathfrak{w}
. These
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
are built combinatorially from
\mathfrak{w}
, in contrast to the intrinsically analytic nature of any spectral network associated to
L_{\mathfrak{w}}
and its associated augmentation
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{A}(\Lambda_{\partial\mathfrak{w}})\longrightarrow k[H_{1}(L_{\mathfrak{w}})]
of the Legendrian contact dg-algebra
\mathcal{A}(\Lambda_{\partial\mathfrak{w}})
. Figure
3
illustrates two examples of weaves and their associated spectral networks.

Figure 3
.
(1) A weave
\mathfrak{w}
and the spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}
constructed in
Section

7.2
. (2) The spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}
in (1), drawn on its own, without the weave
\mathfrak{w}
. (3) Another weave with its associated spectral network. In this case, the spectral network recovers precisely the Berk-Nevins-Roberts network from
[
BNR82
]
by adiabatically degenerating the rigid pseudoholomorphic strips contributing to the augmentation associated to the Lagrangian filling of the weave.

For simplicity, we focus on
S=\mathbb{R}^{2}
and their
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, as this already yields many interesting new examples; see
[
CN22
]
for specifics on the Legendrian dg-algebra
\mathcal{A}(\Lambda_{\partial\mathfrak{w}})
. In
Section

7
, after providing the construction of the augmentation forest
\Theta_{\mathfrak{w}}
, we conclude the following result and use it to explicitly compute several examples, cf.
Section

7.3
:

Theorem 5

(
Augmentations, weaves and Spectral Networks
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a Demazure weave and
L_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset(T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{2},\lambda_{\text{st}})
its associated exact Betti Lagrangian. Then:

(1)

The augmentation forest
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a Morse spectral network compatible with
L_{\mathfrak{w}}
.

In addition, it is a finite and creative spectral network.

(2)

The flowlines of the spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}
are exactly given by the union of
D_{4}^{-}
-trees obtained by adiabatically degenerating the rigid pseudoholomorphic strips contributing to the augmentation
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}
induced by
L_{\mathfrak{w}}
.

Theorem
5
has two advantages. First, there are many weaves with the same boundary
\partial\mathfrak{w}
, e.g.
[
CG22
, Section 2]
and
[
CZ22
, Section 7.1]
, and thus Theorem
5
provides many examples of spectral networks with the
same
asymptotic conditions: the spectral curves
L_{\mathfrak{w}}
for different such weaves
\mathfrak{w}
are typically
not
Hamiltonian isotopic, but they are all exact Lagrangian fillings of the same
\Lambda_{\partial\mathfrak{w}}
. The associated spectral networks
\Theta_{\mathfrak{w}}
are themselves also different: in fact, the notion of (Lagrangian disk) mutation for Betti Lagrangians aligns with the notion of mutation of spectral networks, corresponding to the appearance of a 4d BPS state in a degenerate spectral curve, cf.
Section

7.3.2
. Second, since the construction of
\Theta_{\mathfrak{w}}
is combinatorial, not requiring to solve any differential equation, Theorem
5
is an effective tool for understanding which spectral networks are associated to irregular data. In particular, given a higher-order linear differential equation – e.g. with new Stokes curves as in
[
BNR82
]
– one can qualitatively draw spectral networks associated to it once the Legendrian associated to the Stokes data is computed, which only requires the asymptotic range.
Section

7.3
gives several such examples and computations.

: We thank Merlin Christ, Ian Le, Andrew Neitzke and Daping Weng for useful discussions. Specifically, the construction in
Section

7.2
was first discussed by R.C. with I. Le and D. Weng in relation to another project and we are thankful for their input and generosity. R.C. is also particularly grateful to M. Aganagić and P. Zhou for their interest and encouragement on this work during his visit to their String Theory-Math Seminar at UC Berkeley. Y.J.N. is grateful to Yong-Geun Oh for his interest and hospitality at the Institute for Basic Science (IBS). R.C. is supported by the NSF CAREER DMS-1942363, a Sloan Research Fellowship of the
Alfred P. Sloan Foundation
and a UC Davis College of L&S Dean’s Fellowship.
\Box

2.
Betti Lagrangians and Flowline Trajectories

This section introduces Betti surfaces in Section
2.2
and Betti Lagrangians in
2.3
, where exact Betti Lagrangians and meromorphic spectral curves are also discussed. Real and holomorphic flowlines are discussed in Sections
2.4
and
2.6
respectively. Accordingly, the trapping lemma is proved in Section
2.5
and the asymptotics of WKB trajectories are studied in Section
2.7
. We start with a brief discussion on real and holomorphic cotangent bundles in
Section

2.1
.

2.1.
Real and holomorphic cotangent bundles

Let
S
be a smooth real surface, we denote by
(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
its real cotangent bundle endowed with the canonical Liouville 1-form
\lambda_{\text{st}}\in\Omega^{1}(T^{*}S)
. It is the unique 1-form such that
\eta^{*}\lambda_{\text{st}}=\eta
for any
\eta\in\Omega^{1}(S)
, and its differential is denoted by
\omega_{\text{st}}=d\lambda_{\text{st}}
. The ideal contact boundary
[
Gir20
, Prop. 2]
of this Liouville domain is denoted
(T^{\infty}S,\xi_{\text{st}})
. In this article, we focus on real Lagrangian surfaces
L\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
with boundary on a specified Legendrian
\Lambda\subset(T^{\infty}S,\xi_{\text{st}})
. The Legendrian boundary
\Lambda
will always be smooth, and
L
will typically be embedded, though we also consider immersed, and more singular, Lagrangian subsets in certain parts. We refer to
[
AG01
,
Arn90
]
or
[
Gei08
,
MS98
]
for initiating matters on contact and symplectic topology.

Let
S
be endowed with a Riemann surface structure
\mathbb{S}
, so that
\mathbb{S}
is a complex 1-dimensional manifold and
J_{\mathbb{S}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}TS\longrightarrow TS
is its defining (almost) complex structure. The holomorphic cotangent bundle will be denoted by
(\mathbb{T}^{*}\mathbb{S},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
, and its canonical (complex-valued) holomorphic symplectic form by
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{*}\mathbb{S})=\Omega^{2,0}(T^{*}S,J_{\mathbb{S}})
. Here the splitting of
\Omega^{\bullet}(T^{*}S)
into its
\Omega^{i,j}(T^{*}S,J_{\mathbb{S}})
parts is determined by the eigenspaces of
J_{\mathbb{S}}
. As in the real case, this 2-form can be written as
d\omega_{\mathbb{C}}=\lambda_{\mathbb{C}}
, where the primitive
\lambda_{\mathbb{C}}\in\Omega_{\mathbb{C}}^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{*}\mathbb{S})=\Omega^{1,0}(T^{*}S,J_{\mathbb{S}})
is the canonical holomorphic Liouville form. In this holomorphic context, holomorphic Lagrangian submanifolds will be denoted by
\Sigma\subset(\mathbb{T}^{*}\mathbb{S},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
. Given
(\mathbb{T}^{*}\mathbb{S},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
, we can consider its underlying real rank-2 bundle
T^{*}S
and the real-valued smooth symplectic 2-form
\text{Re}{(e^{i\theta}{\omega_{\mathbb{C}}})}
, for a choice of
\theta\in S^{1}
. We refer to this pair
(T^{*}S,\text{Re}{(e^{i\theta}\omega_{\mathbb{C}})})
over
S
as the
\theta
-part
(\mathbb{T}^{*}\mathbb{S},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
; when
\theta=0
, this is also said to be the real part of
(\mathbb{T}^{*}\mathbb{S},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
.

Remark 2.1
.

(i). Let
\theta\in S^{1}
and
\Sigma\subset(\mathbb{T}^{*}\mathbb{S},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
be a holomorphic Lagrangian submanifold. Then, then underlying real submanifold
L\subset T^{*}S
of
\Sigma
is a real Lagrangian submanifold of
(T^{*}S,\text{Re}{(e^{i\theta}\omega_{\mathbb{C}})})
, where we can identify
TL
and
T\Sigma
as real rank-2 bundles. Indeed,
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}|_{T\Sigma}\equiv 0
if and only if
e^{i\theta}\omega_{\mathbb{C}}|_{T\Sigma}\equiv 0
, which implies that
\text{Re}{(e^{i\theta}\omega_{\mathbb{C}}|_{T\Sigma})}\equiv 0
.

(ii) Studying the real Lagrangian
L
in
(i)
in the
\theta
-part
(T^{*}S,\text{Re}{(e^{i\theta}\omega_{\mathbb{C}})})
is equivalent to studying the real Lagrangian underlying
e^{i\theta}\Sigma
for
(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
, where
e^{i\theta}\Sigma
is the result of applying fiberwise multiplication to
\Sigma
by the scalar
e^{i\theta}
. We thus denote such real Lagrangians as
L=e^{i\theta}\Sigma
.

(iii) For a generic
\theta\in S^{1}
, the real Lagrangian
e^{i\theta}\Sigma
submanifold of
(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
is not exact, i.e.
\lambda_{\text{st}}
defines a closed but non-exact real 1-form when restricted to
e^{i\theta}\Sigma
. For instance, the real part of
\Sigma
is exact if and only if all the complex periods of
\Sigma
are purely imaginary. Nevertheless, note also that for generic
\theta\in S^{1}
,
e^{i\theta}\Sigma\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
does not bound any holomorphic disks either (in an appropriate adiabatic limit) and, in many aspects, behaves similarly to an exact Lagrangian.
\Box

Figure 4
.
A smooth surface
{\bf S}
of genus
3
with 9 marked points
\boldsymbol{m}=\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{9}\}
, in orange, and fronts for their associated Legendrian links in
{\bf\Lambda}=\{\Lambda_{1},\ldots,\Lambda_{9}\}
, in blue. The annular neighborhoods
A_{i}\subset S
for each marked point are shaded in pink. The positive braid words
\beta_{1},\beta_{5},\beta_{8}
are the empty braid words in 3-strands and, for instance, we can choose
\beta_{6}=\sigma_{2}^{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}^{2}
and
\beta_{9}=\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}
.

2.2.
Betti surfaces

The asymptotics of Betti Lagrangians will be constrained by Legendrian links. Given a marked oriented closed surface
(\bf S,\boldsymbol{m})
, denote
S=\bf S\setminus\boldsymbol{m}
and consider a set
\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{|\boldsymbol{m}|}\}
of small annular neighborhoods
A_{i}\subset S
, each obtained by considering a small disk neighborhood of the
i
th marked point in
S
and removing such marked point. The required asymptotics of
L\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
will be prescribed by a set
{\bf\Lambda}=\{\Lambda_{1},\ldots,\Lambda_{|\boldsymbol{m}|}\}
of Legendrians, each in the ideal contact boundary
\Lambda_{i}\subset(T^{\infty}A_{i},\xi_{\text{st}})
. Since projection of the ideal contact boundary onto the zero section is a Legendrian fibration, the front for
\Lambda_{i}
can (and will) be drawn in
A_{i}\subset S
. See Figure
4
for a depiction of this setup. Given a collection
\beta=\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{|\boldsymbol{m}|}\}
of positive braid words
\beta_{i}\in\mbox{Br}_{n}^{+}
in
n
-strands,
i\in[1,|\boldsymbol{m}|]
, consider the Legendrian link
\Lambda_{\beta}\subset(T^{\infty}S,\xi_{\text{st}})
whose front is given by drawing the braid word
\beta_{i}
circularly around
A_{i}
, according to the orientation of
S
; cf.
[
CN22
, Section 2.2]
and see again Figure
4
. The Legendrian isotopy class of
\Lambda_{\beta}
is independent of the braid word representative of its associated braid element in the braid group.

Remark 2.2
.

The braids in
\beta
need not be algebraic braids, i.e. they are not constrained to be certain iterated cables of the unknot. See the proof of
[
CG22
, Corollary 1.6]
for infinitely many examples of non-cable satellite knots and hyperbolic knots that are associated to positive braids (and necessarily not algebraic). In the meromorphic setting, as in the literature on spectral curves
[
GMN13
,
GMN13a
,
GMN14
]
, the braids are necessarily algebraic.
\Box

Formalizing this, a marked oriented closed surface
(\bf S,\boldsymbol{m})
with such Legendrians links leads to the following:

Definition 2.3

(Betti surfaces)
.

A Betti surface of rank
n
is a triple
({\bf S},\boldsymbol{m};{\bf\Lambda})
such that
{\bf\Lambda}
is a collection of Legendrian links in the ideal contact boundary
(T^{\infty}S,\xi_{\text{st}})
, each of which is associated to an
n
-stranded positive braid circularly drawn around a marked point in
\boldsymbol{m}
.
\Box

See Figure
4
for an example of Definition
2.3
. For each
\Lambda_{\beta_{i}}\subset(T^{\infty}A_{i},\xi_{\text{st}})
, a Reeb chord is said to be positive if, when seen as a crossing in the Lagrangian projection, the upper strand travels from northwest to southeast with respect to the given orientations on the strands; else, we say it is
negative
. For notation purposes,
\Lambda_{\beta}
is said to be Reeb-positive if all the Reeb chords of all
\Lambda_{i}
are positive. After a
C^{\infty}
-small Legendrian isotopy, we can and do assume that each of the Reeb chords and transverse double points (of the fronts in
A_{i}
) are at different angles. The angles in the core
S^{1}\subset A_{i}
where there is a Reeb chord for
\Lambda_{\beta_{i}}
are said to be
crossing points
for that core circle.

Remark 2.4
.

By
[
CN22
, Section 2.2]
, if
\beta_{i}
contains a half-twist
w_{0}
as a subword, then the braid for its front can be drawn such that Reeb chords are in bijection with the crossings of
\beta
. Specifically, the front can be drawn so that there is exactly one Reeb chord (arbitrarily close and) preceding each crossing, and these are all the Reeb chords.
\Box

For Floer-theoretic purposes, it is useful to regard the marked points
\boldsymbol{m}
as giving the
horizontal infinity
of
S
, e.g. as opposed to a 1-point compactification of
S
. In detail, for each
m_{i}\in\boldsymbol{m}
, we identify the associated annular neighborhood
A_{i}\subset S
with
A_{i}\cong S^{1}\times(1,\infty)
with coordinates
(e^{i\theta},r)\in S^{1}\times(1,\infty)
, so that any point converges to the point
m_{i}
in
{\bf S}
as
r\to\infty
. For a Reeb chord with a given crossing point
\theta_{0}
, we call the ray
(e^{i\theta_{0}},r)\subset A_{i}
its
Reeb chord ray
.

Example 2.5

(Betti surfaces from Stokes data)
.

As hinted in
[
KKP08
, Remark 2.17]
, the data of the irregular singularities of a meromorphic connection on a Riemann surface
\mathbb{S}
leads to a Betti surface. This is beautifully crystallized in
[
Boa21
]
, specifically in the notion of an irregular curve
(\mathbb{S},\boldsymbol{m},\Theta)
, as defined in
[
Boa14
, Definition 8.1]
or
[
Boa21
, Definition 5.5]
; cf. also
[
Sab13
, Part I]
,
[
She+19
, Section 3.3]
,
[
Su25
, Appendix B]
. In a nutshell, an irregular curve is a marked Riemann surface
(\mathbb{S},\boldsymbol{m})
with the data
\Theta
of an irregular class at each marked point: the irregular class codifies the formal type of the pole of a meromorphic connection, up to formal gauge transformation. In practice, this can be written as a finite Puiseux series
Q
, representing an orbit of the Galois action of the profinite group
\hat{\mathbb{Z}}
on
\mathbb{C}((z^{1/\infty}))/z^{-1}\mathbb{C}[[z^{1/\infty}]]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\bigcup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{C}((z^{1/N}))/z^{-1}\mathbb{C}[[z^{1/N}]]
. Here we will work with representatives
Q
of the form

Q=\sum_{k_{j}\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}}c_{k_{j}}z^{-k_{j}},\quad c_{k_{j}}\in\mathfrak{t}_{reg}\subset\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{n},

modulo the action of
c_{k_{j}}\to c_{k_{j}}e^{2\pi in/\text{ram}(Q)},n\in[1,\text{ram}(Q)]
, where
\text{ram}(Q)
is the smallest number such that
Q\in\mathbb{C}((z^{1/\text{ram}(Q)}))
. The irregular part of the formal type of the meromorphic connection is then given by
d-dQ
. Since
Q
is a finite Puiseux series with pole orders strictly greater than
0
, it can be seen as a multivalued function on
\mathbb{C}^{*}
. The Stokes diagram associated to such irregular data is given by the graphs of the multivalued functions
\text{Re}(Q(re^{i\theta}))
on
S_{\theta}^{1}
, for fixed
r
large enough. For each marked point
m_{i}
, the Stokes diagram in
A_{i}
is precisely the circular closure of a positive braid, and thus can be interpreted as a front for a Legendrian of the type
\Lambda_{\beta_{i}}
above.

In summary, the Betti surface associated to an irregular curve
(\mathbb{S},\boldsymbol{m},\Theta)
is precisely the underlying smooth surface
({\bf S},\boldsymbol{m},{\bf\Lambda}_{\Theta})
, where
{\bf\Lambda}_{\Theta}
is the Legendrian link in
(T^{\infty}S,\xi_{\text{st}})
defined by the Stokes diagrams of the irregular data
\Theta
, understood as fronts in
S
. In these examples, the points of maximal decay for the Stokes diagram of an irregular type give the Reeb rays for the associated Legendrian link; these are also referred to as singular directions or anti-Stokes directions of the Stokes diagram. By construction, the Stokes directions (a.k.a. oscillatory directions) of the Stokes diagram are precisely the directions where there is a crossing in the front of the associated Legendrian link, i.e. the angles at which we draw the crossings of the associated braid word.
\Box

2.3.
Betti Lagrangians

Given a Betti surface
({\bf S},\boldsymbol{m},{\bf\Lambda})
, we introduce Betti Lagrangians: the Lagrangian surfaces in
(T^{\ast}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
for which we construct and study spectral networks. These Lagrangians are our main object of study. There are two important classes,
exact Betti Lagrangians
and
meromorphic spectral curves
, discussed respectively in Subsections
2.3.1
and
2.3.2
. Many of the Lagrangian fillings of Legendrian links studied in the literature, e.g.
[
CG22
,
CW24
,
CZ22
,
EHK16
]
, provide plenty of examples of this first class of exact Betti Lagrangians. The irregular curves studied in the context of wild character varieties and the irregular Riemann-Hilbert or non-Abelian Hodge correspondences give many examples of the second class, e.g. cf.
[
BB04
,
Boa14
,
Boa14
,
FN21
,
GMN12
,
GMN13a
,
GMN14
,
Kat+15
,
Sab13
]
.

To define Betti Lagrangians, we first discuss the notion of Lagrangian multigraphs on a smooth manifold
M
, as follows. By definition, a properly embedded Lagrangian submanifold
L\subset T^{\ast}M
is said to be a
Lagrangian multi-graph
if outside a codimension-
1
properly closed subset
K_{L}\subset L
, the projection
\pi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L\longrightarrow M
is a smooth immersion outside
K_{L}
and it is a covering over
M-\pi(K_{L})
. Given a point
m\in M-\pi(K_{L})
, a neighborhood of the preimage
\pi^{-1}(m)\cap(L-K_{L})
in
M
consists of
n
disjoint Lagrangian disks, the
smooth sheets
of
L
over
m
. Each such smooth sheet can be realized as the Lagrangian graph of the differential of a smooth function
f_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
i\in[1,n]
, with each
f_{i}
well-defined up to constant.

Definition 2.6

(Betti Lagrangians)
.

Let
({\bf S},\boldsymbol{m},{\bf\Lambda})
be a Betti surface. A Betti Lagrangian
L\subset T^{\ast}S
is a weakly bounded Lagrangian multigraph
L\subset T^{\ast}S
such that the restriction of the projection
\pi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L\to S
onto the zero section
S
is a degree-
n
simple branched cover with finitely many branched points.
\Box

In Definition
2.6
we have used the notation
weakly bounded
, which we use to encode a number of Riemannian properties of the Lagrangian multigraph
L
. Its precise meaning is as follows:

Definition 2.7
.

A Lagrangian multigraph
L\subset T^{*}M
is said to be
weakly bounded
if there exists a Riemannian metric
(M,g)
, constants
\mbox{inj}(g),\rho\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and a precompact subset
K\subset M
containing
\pi(K_{L})
such that:

(i)

The Riemannian metric
g
is complete, the
C^{\infty}
-norm of all the derivatives of the curvature tensor are uniformly bounded (a.k.a. geometrically bounded), and the minimal injectivity radius of
g
is bounded below by
\mbox{inj}(g)>0
.

(ii)

Given a point
m\in M-N_{2\mbox{inj}(g)}(K)
, at distance at least
2r
from
K
, and the functions
f_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}M\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
describing the sheets of
L
over
B_{\mbox{inj}(g)}(m)
as
\mbox{gr}(df_{i})
, then we must have

\inf_{B_{\mbox{inj}(g)}(m)}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert df_{i}-df_{j}\right\rvert}>\rho,\quad\forall q\in B_{\mbox{inj}(g)}(m).

By definition, a weakly bounded Lagrangian multigraph
L
is said to be
uniformly bounded
if
L
lies in the
R
-disk bundle
D_{R}T^{\ast}S\subset T^{*}S
for some
R\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and for the smooth sheets of its fiberwise
\varepsilon
-scaled image
\varepsilon L\subset T^{*}S
over
B_{\mbox{inj}(g)}(m)
,
m\in M-N_{2\mbox{inj}(g)}(K)
, there exist uniform bounds
C_{k}
such that
\|\nabla^{k}f_{i}\|_{g}\leq C_{k}
for all
i\in[1,n]
, where
\nabla
is Levi-Civita connection of
(M,g)
and there is no dependence on
\varepsilon
.
\Box

Intuitively, the second condition for a uniformly bounded Lagrangian multigraph in Definition
2.7
is that away from the preimage of the locus
K
, the sheets of the rescaled Lagrangian
\varepsilon L\subset T^{*}S
uniformly
C^{\infty}
-converge with respect to
g
to the zero section as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

It is worth providing a local description of the Betti Lagrangian near its branch point. The germ
\{w^{2}-z=0\}
in
\mathbb{C}^{2}=T^{\ast}\mathbb{C}
is called the holomorphic cusp singularity, and we call its real part the
D_{4}^{-}
-singularity. Near its branch points,
L
is always locally equivalent to the
D_{4}^{-}
-singularity germ. For the rest of the paper, We will assume that given a branch point
b
, there exists some local conformal coordinate
z
near
b
, and some constant holomorphic covector
c
such that near the ramification locus
\pi^{-1}(b)
,
L
is equal to the real part of
\{(\lambda_{\mathbb{C}}-c)^{2}=zdz^{2}\}\subset T^{\ast}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}
near
z=0
. We will furthermore assume that the smooth sheets of
L
are all holomorphic.

2.3.1.
Exact Betti Lagrangians

For exact Betti Lagrangians, the analysis uses the conical ends, as with the framework of Lagrangian fillings from
[
EHK16
, Section 2.2]
. Consider the Legendrian fiber in
(T^{\infty}{\bf S},\xi_{\text{st}})
above a marked point
m_{i}\in\boldsymbol{m}
and identify a neighborhood of it with
(J^{1}S^{1},\xi_{\text{st}})
, where the fiber is mapped to the zero section. Suppose that the Legendrian link
\Lambda_{i}\subset(T^{\infty}S,\xi_{\text{st}})
is parametrically given by
(x(\theta),y(\theta),z(\theta))
where
x(\theta)=\theta
and the contact 1-form is
dz-ydx
,
(x,y)\in T^{*}S^{1}
and
z\in\mathbb{R}
. Then a conical end is defined as follows:

Definition 2.8

(Conical ends)
.

Let
L\subset(T^{*}D^{2},\lambda_{\text{st}})
be the germ of a Lagrangian surface and
\Lambda\subset(J^{1}S_{\theta}^{1},\xi_{\text{st}})
be parametrized by
(x(\theta),y(\theta),z(\theta))
, where
S^{1}
is identified with the Legendrian fiber
T^{\infty}_{0}D^{2}
of
(T^{\infty}D^{2},\xi_{\text{st}})
above
0
. By definition,
L
has a conical end of type
\Lambda
if
L
admits a parametrization of the form

(1)

\displaystyle(r,\theta)\longmapsto(x(\theta),f(r)y(\theta),r,f^{\prime}(r)z(\theta))\in T^{*}D^{2},

for some strictly increasing positive real function
f(r)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[1,\infty)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
which is linear at infinity. In other words,
L
is the multigraph associated to the multi-valued function
z(\theta)f(r)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S^{1}\times[1,\infty)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
.
\Box

The conical ends in Definition
2.8
allow us to introduce the following:

Definition 2.9

(Exact Betti Lagrangians)
.

Let
({\bf S},\boldsymbol{m},{\bf\Lambda})
be a Betti surface. An
exact Betti Lagrangian

L\subset(T^{\ast}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
of rank
n
is an exact Lagrangian submanifold
L\subset(T^{\ast}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
such that

(i)

The restriction
\pi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L\to S
of the projection
T^{*}S\longrightarrow S
onto the zero section is a degree-
n
simple branched cover, with finitely many branch points.

(ii)

L
has conical ends near each of the marked points in
\boldsymbol{m}
.
\Box

Let
(S,g)
be a Riemannian metric such that
g
can be written as
g=dr^{2}+r^{2}d\theta^{2}
near each of the horizontal infinities of the Betti surface
S
. Then the conical condition in Definition
2.9
.(ii) implies that exact Betti Lagrangians are uniformly bounded with respect to such Riemannian metric
g
. Therefore, exact Betti Lagrangians are Betti Lagrangians, according to Definition
2.6
.

2.3.2.
Meromorphic spectral curves

Let us introduce a second important class of Betti Lagrangians, meromorphic spectral curves, associated to an irregular curve
(\mathbb{S},\boldsymbol{m},\Theta)
; see
[
Boa21
, Definition 5.5]
and Example
2.5
above for irregular curves. Intuitively, these spectral curves are given by meromorphic multi-graphs on
\mathbb{S}
with their behavior at a pole in
\boldsymbol{m}
specified by the irregular data
\Theta
. Locally, the behavior at poles can be described as follows.

Consider a germ of a holomorphic Lagrangian multi-graph
\Sigma\subset T^{\ast}\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
over
\mathbb{C}^{*}
without singularities. Since the projection of
\Sigma
to the base
\mathbb{C}^{*}
defines an
n
-sheeted covering, we can consider the elementary symmetric polynomial
\mathfrak{c}_{i}
,
i\in[1,n]
, over the distinct sheets
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}
of
\Sigma
and obtain holomorphic functions
\mathfrak{c}_{i}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})
on
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
,
i\in[1,n]
. By definition,
\Sigma
is said to extend to a
meromorphic multi-graph
over
\mathbb{C}
if the elementary symmetric polynomials extend over
\mathbb{C}
as meromorphic functions with poles over the origin. After possibly passing to a ramified cover, under
z\to z^{r}
for some
r\in\mathbb{N}
, we regard the sheets
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}
of
\Sigma
as giving holomorphic functions on
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
. By Lagrange’s upper bound
\displaystyle\max\{1,\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\frac{{\mathfrak{c}_{i}}}{{\mathfrak{c}_{n}}}\right\rvert}\}
on the norm of complex roots, cf.
[
Lag73
, Chapter IV]
, there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
large enough such that the norm of
z^{N}\lambda_{i}(z^{r})
is uniformly bounded for all
i\in[1,n]
. Therefore, the functions
\lambda_{i}(z^{r})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{C}^{*}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
all extend to germs of meromorphic functions on
\mathbb{C}
near the origin and we can regard the sheets of
\Sigma
as the Lagrangian graphs of Puiseux series with finite polar parts.

Given a meromorphic multi-graph over
\mathbb{C}
, as above, we consider the differential operator

\partial^{n}_{z}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{c}_{i}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})\partial^{n-i}_{z},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}.

By reducing this operator to a system of order
1
differential operators, it defines a meromorphic connection on the rank-
n
trivial bundle over
\mathbb{C}
with a single pole at the origin. The irregular data is then given by considering the irregular class of such meromorphic connection. Intuitively, these Galois orbits are given by a local primitive of the holomorphic Liouville form
\lambda_{\text{st}}
over the distinct components of the multi-graph
\Sigma
, after dropping the
o(\log(z))
terms.

Definition 2.10

(Meromorphic Spectral Curves)
.

Let
\big(\mathbb{S},\boldsymbol{m},\Theta\big)
be an irregular curve. A holomorphic submanifold
\Sigma\subset T^{\ast}S
is said to be a
meromorphic spectral curve
if

(i)

the germ of
\Sigma
is a meromorphic multi-graph with poles on
\boldsymbol{m}
,

(ii)

the corresponding formal Puiseux series has the same orbits as
\Theta
, with the same multiplicities.

In addition, a meromorphic spectral curve is said to have
O(-1)
-ends if the sheets
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}
and their difference functions have growth bounded below by
z^{-1}
, locally in
z\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
with a pole at
z=0
.
\Box

For the purposes of our results, including Theorems
1
,
2
,
5
and
4
, we always consider meromorphic spectral curve with
O(-1)
-ends. First,
O(-1)
-ends are necessary because, without such hypothesis, one could add a holomorphic perturbation to force the difference between the sheets to blow-up slower than
z^{-1}
as
z\to 0
. Second, having
O(-1)
-ends implies that the
\theta
-real part of a meromorphic spectral curve is a Betti Lagrangian as in Definition
2.6
, as it implies that the underlying multi-graph is weakly bounded. In practice, here and in all situations we shall choose a Kähler metric on
\mathbb{S}
which coincides with
|z|^{-2}dz\otimes dz
near the marked points.

Remark 2.11
.

(i) If an irregular class in the irregular data
\Theta
has multiplicity, the associated components of this same type coincide in
\mathbb{C}((z^{1/\infty}))/z^{-1}\mathbb{C}[[z^{1/\infty}]]
and thus differ by a finite Puiseux series in
z^{-1}\mathbb{C}[[z^{1/\infty}]]
. In contact topological terms, the Legendrian link associated to an irregular class with multiplicity
m\in\mathbb{N}
corresponds to the
m
-copy Reeb push-off of the Legendrian link associated to the irregular class (without multiplicity). This choice guarantees that the
O(-1)
-condition can be satisfied.

(ii) It follows from the construction of Stokes diagrams, see Example
2.5
, that the condition that the difference of the sheets grows faster or equal than
z^{-1}
is equivalent to requiring that the order of the poles of the multi-valued 1-form
dQ_{i}-dQ_{j}
,
i,j\in[1,n]
, are all greater or equal than
1
. That is, the (difference of the) polar terms of
Q
themselves are of degree greater than
0
, e.g. no holomorphic parts.

(iii) An alternative viewpoint of meromorphic multi-graph is as follows, cf.
[
IM21
,
Kuw24
]
. Given a rank
n
meromorphic multi-graph, its cameral cover is given by taking all the possible permutations of the sheets. Then, each pair
i,j\in[1,n]
,
i<j
, induces a covering of
\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
over which the germ of the cameral cover is realized as the germ of a rank
2
quadratic differential near its pole. Then the condition that the difference of the sheets grow faster or equal than
z^{-1}
is equivalent to requiring that the order of the poles of this quadratic differential are all greater or equal than
2
.
\Box

Example 2.12
.

(1)
Airy Equation
. As an explicit instance of Example
2.5
, consider the trivial
\mathbb{C}^{2}
-bundle over the Riemann surface
\mathbb{S}=\mathbb{C}
associated to the Airy differential equation
f^{\prime\prime}(x)=xf(x)
,
x\in\mathbb{C}
. It has an irregular singularity at
x=\infty
, which we take to be the unique marked point of the associated irregular curve
\mathbb{CP}^{1}=\mathbb{S}\cup\{\infty\}
; this is the equation originally studied by G. Stokes
[
Sto09
]
. In the
x
-coordinate, the associated meromorphic connection can be written as

\nabla_{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=d-\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
x&0\\
\end{bmatrix}dx=d-dQ,\quad Q=\begin{bmatrix}0&x\\
x^{2}/2&0\\
\end{bmatrix},\quad x\in\mathbb{C}.

The formal irregular type of
\nabla_{m}
at
x=\infty
is given by
\langle x^{3/2}\rangle
, as the eigenvalues of
Q
are
e^{\pm x^{3/2}/\sqrt{2}}
. Thus we have the irregular curve
(\mathbb{S},\boldsymbol{m},\Theta)=(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\infty,\langle x^{3/2}\rangle)
. Its associated Stokes diagram at
x=\infty
is described by the braid word
\beta=\sigma_{1}^{3}
, so the Betti surface is
(S,\boldsymbol{m},\Lambda)=(S^{2},\infty,\Lambda_{\sigma_{1}^{3}})
. After satelliting the Legendrian zero section of
(J^{1}S^{1},\xi_{\text{st}})
to the max-tb Legendrian unknot in
(\mathbb{R}^{3},\xi_{\text{st}})
, where
S^{1}
is the Legendrian fiber of
T^{\infty}S^{2}\longrightarrow S^{2}
at infinity, the Legendrian knot
\Lambda_{\sigma_{1}^{3}}
satellites to the max-tb unknot itself, presented in the front as the
(-1)
-closure of
\beta=\sigma_{1}^{3}
. Any
\theta
-part of the associated meromorphic spectral curve
\Sigma=\{(x,\eta)\in T^{*}\mathbb{C}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\eta^{2}=x\}\subset T^{*}\mathbb{C}
,
\Sigma\cong\mathbb{C}
, thus gives a Betti Lagrangian disk filling of the max-tb unknot. In terms of weaves, giving the front of the Legendrian lift of the
\theta
-part of
\Sigma
, this particular meromorphic spectral curve gives a 2-weave with a unique trivalent vertex.

For coherence with previous notation, we can rewrite the above in terms of the coordinate
z=x^{-1}
, locally around the pole, as the coordinate
z
is the one used in Example
2.5
and previously in this subsection. Then the Airy equation transforms to
z^{4}f^{\prime\prime}(z)+2z^{3}f^{\prime}(z)=f(z)z^{-1}
: at
z\neq 0
, this is equivalent to
f^{\prime\prime}(z)+2z^{-1}f^{\prime}(z)=f(z)z^{-5}
, which has an irregular singularity at
z=0
because the coefficient
z^{-5}
of
f(z)
has a pole greater than 2 at
z=0
. Its analysis of the irregular type at
z=0
coincides with that of the Airy equation at
z^{-1}=x=\infty
.

(2)
Irreducible isolated plane curve singularities
. The discussion in (1) readily generalizes for differential operators
P(x,\partial_{x})
on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}\setminus\{\infty\}
,
x\in\mathbb{C}
whose spectral curve
\Sigma=\{(x,\eta)\in T^{*}\mathbb{C}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}P(x,\eta)=0\}
has an irreducible isolated singularity at the origin
(x,\eta)=0
. In these cases, the irregular curve
(\mathbb{S},\boldsymbol{m},\Theta)=(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\infty,\Theta_{P})
has as irregular data
\Theta_{P}
a Stokes diagram given by a braid
\beta(P)
that represents – upon satelliting along the unknot – the link of the singularity
P\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{C}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
. The Betti surface is thus of the form
(S,\boldsymbol{m},\Lambda)=(S^{2},\infty,\Lambda_{\beta(P)})
, where the smooth type of
\Lambda_{\beta(P)}
, as a link in
S^{3}=\partial(T^{*}\mathbb{C})
, is that of the link of the singularity. The theory of algebraic links allows to readily compute such smooth type using a Puiseux series for
P
(via Newton exponents), expressing this link as an iterated cable link with positive enough
(p_{i},q_{i})
-coefficients; see e.g.
[
EN85
, Chapter I & II]
or
[
Ghy17
, Chapters 17 & 18]
. By
[
Cas22
, Prop. 2.2]
, algebraic links have a unique max-tb Legendrian representative, and thus the Legendrian isotopy type of
\Lambda_{\beta(P)}
is uniquely determined by the irregular data
\Theta_{P}
.

This construction leads to a large class of Betti Lagrangians in
(\mathbb{R}^{4},\omega_{\text{st}})
which are Lagrangian fillings of max-tb Legendrian representatives of algebraic links.
4
4

4

Technically, as given, they are asymptotically Lagrangian fillings, but this can be corrected as in
[
Cha10
, Section 5.1]
.
At the same time, their associated meromorphic connections are also important objects of study: these include the differential equations of Airy, Bessel, Clifford, Weber, Whittaker and general differential operators of Airy type (cf.
[
HJ22
,
Kat87
]
), among others.
5
5

5

See 48min in P. Boalch’s talk “First Steps in Global Lie Theory” at the Simons Center during the program “The Stokes Phenomenon and its applications in Mathematics and Physics” as well as his
spirograph Stokes diagrams
applet in his website.
By construction, these spectral curves are singular. In order to obtain smooth (embedded) Lagrangian fillings for such links
\Lambda_{\beta(P)}
one must morsify the singularity
P
: as explained in
[
Cas22
]
, any real morsification will give rise to an exact embedded Lagrangian filling. Furthermore, since the front singularities of holomorphic Legendrians are real codimension-2, these Lagrangian fillings can all be described by weaves
[
CZ22
]
. Building on (1) above, a simple instance is that, for generic
\theta
, the
\theta
-part of the meromorphic spectral curve associated to a morsification of
f^{\prime\prime}(x)=x^{3}f(x)
gives Betti Lagrangians for the Betti surface
(S,\boldsymbol{m},\Lambda)=(S^{2},\infty,\Lambda_{\sigma_{1}^{5}})
: as we vary
\theta\in S^{1}
, this actually recovers the 5 known Lagrangian fillings of the max-tb trefoil. In the general case, there is a moduli for the space of meromorphic connections on
\mathbb{C}
with such fixed irregular data at
x=\infty
, including such morsifications, and it is isomorphic to the moduli of sheaves with singular support on these Legendrian links. See e.g.
[
CW24
,
Su25
]
. The
\theta
-parts of different such meromorphic spectral curves give rise to different Lagrangian fillings: both varying
\theta
and varying the meromorphic connection typically changes the Hamiltonian isotopy type of the Betti Lagrangian, see e.g.
[
Hug23
]
for how varying
\theta\in S^{1}
for a fixed morsification of
f^{\prime\prime}(x)=x^{m}f(x)
leads to different orbits of Lagrangian fillings for
(2,m)
-torus links.

(3)
BNR Equation
. Consider the Berk-Nevins-Roberts differential equation, cf.
[
BNR82
]
. This is the order 3 differential equation

\partial_{x}^{3}f(x)-3\partial_{x}f(x)+xf(x)=0,\quad x\in\mathbb{C},

known for being one of the first studied examples with higher rank Stokes phenomenon. In the coordinate
z=x^{-1}
, the equation is transformed to

z^{6}\partial_{z}^{3}f(z)+6z^{5}\partial_{z}^{2}f(z)+(6z^{4}-3z^{2})\partial_{z}f(z)-z^{-1}f(z)=0,\quad z\in\mathbb{C}

which has an irregular singularity at
z=0
, e.g. because the coefficient of
f(z)
has a pole of order
7
. A direct computation of the irregular data gives that the irregular curve is

(\mathbb{S},\boldsymbol{m},\Theta)=(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\boldsymbol{m},\langle x^{4/3}\rangle).

The positive 3-stranded braid word associated to this irregular data is
\beta=(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2})^{2}
and thus the Betti surface is
(S,\boldsymbol{m},\Lambda)=(S^{2},\infty,\Lambda_{(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2})^{2}})
. The Betti Lagrangian
L\subset(T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{2},\lambda_{\text{st}})
associated to this meromorphic spectral curve
\Sigma\subset T^{*}\mathbb{C}
consists of a unique embedded Lagrangian disk, as it is graphical in
x\in\mathbb{C}
. Indeed, it is a 3-weave that can be parametrized by taking the real part of the holomorphic Legendrian lift of

\Sigma=\{(x,\eta)\in T^{*}\mathbb{C}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\eta^{3}-3\eta+x=0\}\subset T^{*}\mathbb{C},

understood as a holomorphic Lagrangian. Such
\Sigma
is a morsification of the function
x(\eta)=\eta^{3}
, so it fits within the description of (2) above, for a smooth germ in this case. In real coordinates
x=u+iv
,
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the front for this real Legendrian lift is parametrized by

\sigma\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3},\quad\sigma(u,v)=\left(u^{3}-3uv^{2},3u^{2}v-v^{3},3(u^{3}v-uv^{3}-uv)\right),

which readily exhibits the boundary braid
\beta=(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2})^{2}
and the embedded Lagrangian disk, as there are no Reeb chords in this front. The contact topological proof that the Berk-Nevins-Roberts equation has no moduli is then simply the observation that
\Lambda_{(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2})^{2}}
, understood as a Legendrian link in
(S^{3},\xi_{\text{st}})
after satelliting the Legendrian (unit) cotangent fiber along the max-tb Legendrian unknot, is actually Legendrian isotopic to the max-tb Legendrian unknot. Note that, in accordance to this, taking different
\theta
-parts leads to a rotation of the 3-weave and the Hamiltonian isotopy type of the Betti Lagrangian remains the same.

\Box

2.4.
Real Morse flowlines

Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian manifold and
L\subset T^{*}M
a Betti Lagrangian. Consider
m\in M-\pi(K_{L})
and let
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\}
define the smooth sheets of
L
over
m
, via the graphs of
df_{i}
.

Definition 2.13
.

A smooth map

\gamma\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\longrightarrow M-K_{L}
is said to be an
ij
-gradient flowline for
L
if it satisfies

(2)

\gamma^{\prime}(s)+\nabla_{g}(f_{i}-f_{j})(\gamma(s))=0,\quad\gamma(0)=m,\quad s\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon),

for the two local sheets
f_{i},f_{j}
of
L
along
\gamma
,
i,j\in[1,n]
. The domain of definition of a flowline can be extended as long as it does not converge to a point in
\pi(K_{L})
: a flowlines obtained by extending the domain of definition are called
continuations
. Maximal continuations of solutions of the gradient flowline equation (
2
) are said to be
trajectories
.
\Box

Given an
ij
-gradient flowline
\gamma\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\longrightarrow M-K_{L}
, consider the lift

\bar{\gamma}^{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\longrightarrow L,\quad\bar{\gamma}^{i}(s)=(\gamma(s),d(f_{i}\circ\gamma)(s))

of
\gamma
to the
i
th sheet of
L
, and note the following energy inequality:

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
(M,g)
be a Riemannian manifold,
L\subset(T^{*}M,d\lambda)
a Betti Lagrangian with
\lambda
the Liouville form, and
\gamma\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(a,b)\longrightarrow M
an
ij
-flowline for
L
. Then

\int_{\bar{\gamma}^{i}-\bar{\gamma}^{j}}\lambda=-\int_{a}^{b}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\nabla(f_{i}-f_{j})\right\rvert}^{2}=(f_{i}-f_{j})(\gamma(b))-(f_{i}-f_{j})(\gamma(a))

Proof.

This follows from
(\bar{\gamma}^{i}-\bar{\gamma}^{j})^{\ast}\lambda=d(f_{i}-f_{j})(\gamma^{\prime}(s))=\partial_{s}f
and Equation (
2
).
∎

Lemma
2.14
implies that the relative homology class
[\bar{\gamma}^{j}-\bar{\gamma}^{i}]\in H_{1}(L,\mathfrak{t})
has positive
\lambda
-length, where
\mathfrak{t}
is the appropriate set of points in
L
giving the boundaries of the lifts
\bar{\gamma}^{i}
. This relative homology class
\bar{\gamma}^{i}-\bar{\gamma}^{j}
is said to be the
cotangent
ij
-lift
of
\gamma
, and its
\lambda
-length above is said to be its
flow-energy
, a.k.a. its symplectic area. Note that, by Definition
2.13
, the difference
f_{i}-f_{j}
strictly
decreases
along the flowline. There are two important classes of flowlines in our context:

(1)

The flowline equation in
(S^{1},g_{flat})
for an immersed Betti Lagrangian
L=L_{\Lambda}\subset T^{*}S^{1}
given by the Lagrangian projection of a Legendrian link
\Lambda\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{(\theta,y(\theta),z(\theta)\}\subset J^{1}S^{1}
whose front in
S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}
has no cusps.

(2)

The flowline equation in a disk
\mathbb{R}^{2}
for a Betti Lagrangian
L\subset T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{2}
near a
D_{4}^{-}
-singularity. That is, near a branch point of the projection
L\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
that, in the spatial wavefront in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}
of the Legendrian lift of
L
, is a non-generic
D_{4}^{-}
singularity.

For case (1)
, we choose a base coordinate
\theta\in S^{1}
and the
ij
-flowline equation for
\gamma\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\longrightarrow S^{1}
reads

(3)

\gamma^{\prime}(s)+\left[(y_{i}(\theta)-y_{j}(\theta))\partial_{\theta}\right]_{|_{\gamma(s)}}=0.

Here we denoted
\gamma^{\prime}(s)=\gamma_{*}(\partial_{s})
, so writing
\gamma^{\prime}(s)=\theta^{\prime}(s)\partial_{\theta}
expresses (
3
) as
\theta^{\prime}(s)+(y_{i}(\theta(s))-y_{j}(\theta(s)))=0
. Near a Reeb chord of the Legendrian link
\Lambda
, i.e. an immersed point of
L_{\Lambda}
, the local configuration of sheets reduces to that of the two Lagrangian strands

L_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(x,-x)\in T^{*}(-1,1)\mbox{ and }L_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=(x,x)\in T^{*}(-1,1)\quad\mbox{meeting at }x=0.

In these local coordinates, the difference
y_{1}(x)-y_{2}(x)=2x
of the sheets
y_{1},y_{2}
gives the
12
-flowline equation
x^{\prime}(s)-2x(s)=0
and the
21
-flowline equation, which is
x^{\prime}(s)+2x(s)=0
. These have solutions
e^{2s}
and
e^{-2s}
, respectively, and it follows that the
12
-flowline equation has
x=0
as its unstable manifold, and the
21
-flowline equation has
x=0
as its stable manifold. Assuming that
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are all oriented in the direction
\partial_{x}
, the Reeb chord is positive if
L_{1}
is above
L_{2}
, and negative otherwise. This settles the local behavoir near a Reeb chord. Globally on
L_{\Lambda}\subset T^{*}S^{1}
, the trajectories must begin and end at some Reeb chord: Lemma
2.14
then implies that the difference function
z_{i}(\theta)-z_{j}(\theta)
decreases along the trajectory. Here the
sign
of the difference function
cannot be
both negative at
s=-\infty
and positive at
s=+\infty
. This describes the qualitative behavior of flowlines for case (1), which we summarize in the following:

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
L\subset T^{*}S^{1}
be an immersed Betti Lagrangian
L_{\Lambda}\subset T^{*}S^{1}
given by the Lagrangian projection of a Legendrian link
\Lambda\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{(\theta,y(\theta),z(\theta)\}\subset J^{1}S^{1}
whose front in
S^{1}_{\theta}\times\mathbb{R}_{z}
has no cusps. Consider a pair of points
c,c^{\prime}\in S^{1}
above each of which lies one crossing of
L_{\Lambda}
. Then:

(1)

There exist finitely many gradient trajectories beginning at
c
and terminating at
c^{\prime}
, up to translation.

(2)

Suppose each such
ij
-gradient trajectory is maximally well-defined an open interval
(a,b)=(a(c,c^{\prime}),b(c,c^{\prime}))\subset\mathbb{R}
in the universal cover
\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow S^{1}
