Now, the tangency locus is a locally finite stratified manifold, see e.g.
[
Wal75
]
. Let us show that the tangency locus is smooth. For that, choose a smooth parametrized
1
-dimensional stratum
\eta(t)
of the tangency locus and note that differentiating the condition
\text{Im}(\phi)=0
leads to
\text{Im}(\phi^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}(t))=0
. This latter question is that of the flowline of
\phi^{\prime}
passing through
l_{1}
. Then Picard–Lindelöf’s uniqueness of solutions of differential equations implies that the tangency locus must be the entire flowline of
\phi^{\prime}
passing through
l_{1}
and thus it is smooth.

Step 2

Let us compute the set of points in the tangency locus where the transversality of the map
F
also fails. By construction, the vector space given by
\text{im}(dF)+T\cdot\mbox{diag}
is generated by the rows of the
4\times 4
matrix:

\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\theta}&0\\
0&e^{i\theta}/\phi\\
1&1\\
\partial_{\theta}l_{1}e^{i\theta}+i(l_{1}+s_{1})e^{i\theta}&(e^{i\theta}/\phi)\partial_{\theta}l_{2}+i(l_{2}+s_{2})e^{i\theta}/{\phi}\end{pmatrix}.

After an elementary transformation eliminate the terms containing
\partial_{\theta}l_{1}
and
\partial_{\theta}l_{2}
, the determinant of the matrix is equal to

(1/\phi)((l_{2}+s_{2})/\phi-(l_{1}+s_{1})).

Therefore the matrix is not invertible, and so transversality fails, if and only if

(17)

\displaystyle l_{1}+s_{1}=(l_{2}+s_{2})/\phi

This set is discrete. Indeed, since
z=(l_{1}+s_{1})e^{i\theta}
and
W(z)=(l_{2}+s_{2})e^{i\theta}
, this set belongs to the zero set of the holomorphic function

z=W(z)/\phi(z)

which is discrete unless
W(z)=z\phi(z)
. This latter equality cannot occur though: differentiating both sides gives
\phi(z)dz=dW=(\phi(z)+z\phi^{\prime}(z))dz
and so
\phi^{\prime}(z)=0
along the 1-stratum
\eta(t)
. This is a contradiction since
\phi(z)
is holomorphic and not a constant function. Therefore, since the set of points where (
17
) holds (and so transversality fails) is finite, standard parametric transversality applies and we conclude that, for generic
\theta
, the
\theta
-deformations do not intersect tangentially.
∎

As argued above, at the beginning of
Section

3.4
,
Theorem

1
.(ii) then follows now that
Lemma

3.19
is proven, after we sort out flow-acyclicity. To see that flow-acyclicity holds, we impose the genericity condition that for each inductive step,
\EuScript{W}_{i}
consists only of vertices that are stable under generic infinitesimal perturbations of
\theta
, and that there does not exist an interaction vertex such that the out-going
ik
-wall is an ancestor to one of the in-going walls (in other words, some of the in-going walls have multiplicities). Such vertices are not generic, since the normal deformation vector for the new WKB flowline at
v
has its norm greater than that of the out-going wall at
v
. We now proceed on by induction. Suppose the acyclicity condition holds for
\EuScript{W}_{k}
but fails for
\EuScript{W}_{k+1}
and let
(\mathfrak{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{w}_{n})
be the directed cycle that satisfies conditions (a) and (b) in
Definition

3.5
, for which the decorations of
\mathfrak{w}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{w}_{n}
agree. Since there are no periodic trajectories, this is possible only if
\mathfrak{w}_{1}
is a wall contained in
\EuScript{W}_{k}
, and so we see that there is some
i
such that
\mathfrak{w}_{i}\subset\EuScript{W}_{k}
is an ancestor to
\mathfrak{w}_{1}
in
\EuScript{W}_{k}
. However, then the terminal vertex of
\mathfrak{w}_{i}
fails to satisfy the genericity condition.
Theorem

1
is thus established, as
Theorem

1
.(i) was concluded in
Section

3.3
.

3.5.
Spectral networks and 2d-4d BPS indices

In the context of the supersymmetric field theories in
[
GMN13a
,
GMN12
,
GMN13
,
GMN14
]
, BPS states are certain irreducible unitary representations of a fixed super Lie algebra acting on a given Hilbert space. In this framework, the counts of BPS states are achieved by considering super traces of certain operators on this Hilbert space. In Floer theory, counts of pseudo-holomorphic disks are given by considering the (oriented) cobordism type of their moduli spaces. For
D_{4}^{-}
-trees, we have the choice of counting them either way: by counting the pseudo-holomorphic strips that limit to them, or by treating them combinatorially via the spectral network
\EuScript{W}
and using supertraces; the results of this article show that these counts coincide, cf.
Section

5.3
. The Floer theoretic count is discussed in
Section

5
, and we now discuss the combinatorial count.

Let
\mathring{S},\mathring{L}
be the unit tangent bundles of
S
and
L
, respectively. Let
H
be the homotopy class representing the sphere bundle fiber. Given
z\in\mathring{S}
, we write
{z_{i}},{i\in[1,n]}
for the lifts of
z
to
\mathring{L}
. We denote by
\mathbb{C}[\pi_{1}\big(\mathring{L})]
the group algebra of the fundamental group of (relative) homotopy classes in
\mathring{L}
: as further discussed in
Section

5
, we do not explicitly include the set of endpoints in the notation, as it is implicitly understood by context. For instance, if we consider a soliton class
{\mathfrak{s}(z;\mathfrak{w})}
, it is implicitly understood that it is a relative homotopy class with endpoints in the lifts above
z\in S
.

Let
\EuScript{F}
be a pre-spectral network and
\mathfrak{w}
a wall. We denote by
t(\mathfrak{w})
, resp.
s(\mathfrak{w})
, the sphere bundle lift of the terminal point of
\mathfrak{w}
, resp. initial, given by the unit velocity vector of
\EuScript{F}(\mathfrak{w})
at the terminal point, resp. initial. Regard
\mathbb{Z}
as a category with a single object, with
\hom
identified with
\mathbb{Z}
(as a set, not as an additive group), and the set of walls
W(F)
as a category with trivial morphisms.

Definition 3.20
.

Let
\EuScript{F}
be a finite pre-spectral network and
W(\EuScript{F})
its set of walls. By definition, the vanilla 2d4d BPS index on
\EuScript{F}
is the functor

(18)

\displaystyle\mu\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{C}[\pi_{1}\big(\mathring{L})]\times W(\EuScript{F})\to\mathbb{Z}

uniquely defined by the following constraints:

(1)

\mu([\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}(z;\mathfrak{w})];\mathfrak{w})=1
for
\mathfrak{w}
an initial wall.

(2)

\mu(\cdot;\mathfrak{w})
vanishes on all classes, except for the
H
-orbits of
\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(z;\mathfrak{w})}\in\{{\mathfrak{s}(z;\mathfrak{w})}\}
for
z\in\mathfrak{w}
.

(3)

\mu(H\rho;\mathfrak{w})=-\mu(\rho;\mathfrak{w}).

(4)

\mu
is independent of the basepoint
z\in\mathfrak{w}
for the classes in
\{{\mathfrak{s}(z;\mathfrak{w})}\}
.

(5)

Given a non-interaction vertex
v
with ingoing walls
\mathfrak{w}_{ij},\mathfrak{w}_{kl}
and outgoing walls
\mathfrak{w}_{ij}^{\prime},\mathfrak{w}_{kl}^{\prime}
, let
v(\mathfrak{w}_{ij})
denote the unit velocity lift of
v
at
\mathfrak{w}_{ij}
. Then

\mu(\rho;\mathfrak{w}_{ij})=\mu(\rho;\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}_{ij}),

for all
\rho\in\hom(v(\mathfrak{w}_{ij})_{j},v(\mathfrak{w}_{ij})_{i})
, and similarly for
\mu(\cdot;\mathfrak{w}_{kl})
and
\mu(\cdot;\mathfrak{w}_{kl}^{\prime})
.

(6)

Let
v
be an interaction vertex with ingoing walls
\mathfrak{w}_{ij},\mathfrak{w}_{jk},\mathfrak{w}_{kl}
, outgoing walls
\mathfrak{w}_{ij}^{\prime},\mathfrak{w}_{jk}^{\prime},\mathfrak{w}_{kl}^{\prime}
. and
v(\mathfrak{w}_{ij}),v(\mathfrak{w}_{jk}),v(\mathfrak{w}_{ik})
the corresponding unit velocity lifts. Then

(19)

\displaystyle\mu(\rho;\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}_{ij})=\mu(\rho;\mathfrak{w}_{ij}),

(20)

\displaystyle\mu(\rho;\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}_{jk})=\mu(\rho;\mathfrak{w}_{jk}),

(21)

\displaystyle\mu(\rho;\mathfrak{w}^{\prime}_{ik})=\mu(\rho;\mathfrak{w}_{ik})+\sum_{[\rho_{1}]\circ[\rho_{2}]=[\rho]}\mu(\rho_{1};\mathfrak{w}_{ij})\mu(\rho_{2};\mathfrak{w}_{jk}),

where
\rho
is a path such that the end points of the projection all lie at
v
, and the sum is over the mod
2H
-orbits.
\Box

By definition, a wall of
\EuScript{W}
is said to be active if its BPS index is non-zero.

Remark 3.21
.

(1)
Eq.

21
is known as the 2d Hori-Vafa wall-crossing formula. In the framework of spectral networks, for generic phase, the counts as captured by
Definition

3.20
are given by a vanilla 2d-4d BPS index which is truly a 4d BPS index modified to accout for the 2d interactions. In the above we are able to restrict to pure 2d BPS indices because in our context they do coincide with the vanilla 2d4d BPS indices.

(2) In
Section

5
it will be proven that
Definition

3.20
is actually a count of pseudo-holomorphic strips for a fiber near that wall: it counts continuation strips oriented by the wall along a
\EuScript{W}
-adapted path.
\Box

3.6.
Spectral networks and rigid flowtrees

Flowtrees are often used in the context of Floer theory as a limiting model for configurations of pseudoholomorphic curves. To wit,
[
Fuk93
, Chapter 1]
studies such trees as a first model of the Fukaya
A_{\infty}
-category, see also
[
FO97
, Theorem 2.3]
and
[
Fuk97
, Chapter 1]
. In the framework of Legendrian submanifolds, rigid flowtrees are studied in
[
Ekh07
]
, compared to pseudo-holomorphic disks and used to provide certain computational models for the Legendrian contact dg-algebra, see also
[
RS20
]
. A first aspect in the Floer-theoretic understanding of spectral networks is its relation to such flowtrees, which we analyze in this subsection. Note that this is far from sufficient in order to prove our main results or compare to
[
GMN12
,
GMN13
,
GMN14
]
, and Sections
4
,
5
and
6
proceed beyond these initial techniques. For now, we use the notation and concepts from
[
Ekh07
]
and establish the following result:

Proposition 3.22

(Augmented
D_{4}^{-}
-trees and rigid flowtrees)
.

Let
(S,\boldsymbol{m},{\bf\Lambda})
be a Betti surface,
L\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
an exact Betti Lagrangian,
(S,g)
an adapted metric and
\EuScript{W}\subset S
a compatible creative Morse spectral network. Then, there exists a front-generic exact Lagrangian
L^{\dagger}\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
Hamiltonian isotopic to
L
, and a perturbed metric
(S,g^{\dagger})
such that:

(1)

L^{\dagger}=L
and
g^{\dagger}=g
outside a compact subset, and otherwise
L^{\dagger}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C^{\infty}}}{{\approx}}L
and
g^{\dagger}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C^{\infty}}}{{\approx}}g
.

(2)

There is a bijective correspondence between augmented
D_{4}^{-}
-trees on
\EuScript{W}
and the rigid flowtrees of
(S,g^{\dagger})
with a single positive puncture on
\partial_{\infty}L^{\dagger}
.

The relation to spectral networks is
Proposition

3.22
.(2), whereas
Proposition

3.22
.(1) describe the type of perturbation that it is used, i.e. the Betti Lagrangian and the metric essentially only need to be modified near the branch points, equiv. the vertices of
\EuScript{W}
.

Note that the results of
[
Ekh07
]
only apply to Legendrians with generic front singularities. The Legendrian lift of an exact Betti Lagrangian is
not
in that class because
D_{4}^{-}
-singularities are not generic real Legendrian singularities. Thus, to establish
Proposition

3.22
, we first need to understand rigid flowtrees near a
D_{4}^{-}
-singularity.

3.6.1.
Trees near
D_{4}^{-}
-singularities

Consider the
D_{4}^{-}
-front singularity, as discussed in Case (2) of
Section

2.4
, cf. also
[
CZ22
, Section 2.2.3]
. Its germ can be parametrized as

\delta_{4}^{-}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{2}_{u,v}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x,y,z},\quad\delta_{4}^{-}(u,v)=\left(u^{2}-v^{2},2uv,\frac{2}{3}(u^{3}-3uv^{2})\right).

The singularity itself is at the origin of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and the front is invariant under
(2\pi/3)
-rotation along the
z
-axis. Since
\delta_{4}^{-}(0,v)=\delta_{4}^{-}(0,-v)
, the three edges of
A_{1}^{2}
-singularities are located at

E_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x<0,y=z=0\}=\delta_{4}^{-}(0,v)=\mathbb{R}_{<0},\quad v\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}

and its two additional images under this rotation, which are

E_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sqrt{3}x=2y,z=0\}=\delta_{4}^{-}(\sqrt{3}v,v)=e^{-2\pi i/3}\mathbb{R}_{<0}

E_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\sqrt{3}x=-2y,z=0\}=\delta_{4}^{-}(-\sqrt{3}v,v)=e^{2\pi i/3}\mathbb{R}_{<0},

also with
v\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
. The three initial rays discussed in Case (2) of
Section

2.4
are

R_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}x>0,y=z=0\}=\mathbb{R}_{>0},\quad R_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=e^{-2\pi i/3}\mathbb{R}_{>0},\quad R_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=e^{2\pi i/3}\mathbb{R}_{>0},

which lie exactly in between these three edges
E_{0},E_{1},E_{1}
, cyclically alternating as
R_{0},E_{1},R_{2},E_{0},R_{1},E_{2}
. The local lifts for these three non-generic flowtrees are depicted in Figure
9
.

Figure 9
.
A non-generic model for the flowtrees near the
D_{4}^{-}
-singularity.

In a nutshell, the claim is that a generic
C^{\infty}
-small compactly supported Hamiltonian perturbation of such non-generic
D_{4}^{-}
-front gives rise to a generic Legendrian front whose only rigid flowtrees are
C^{\infty}
-close to the rays
R_{0},R_{1},R_{2}
in the support of such perturbation, and otherwise coincide with them. This is the content of the following:

Lemma 3.23

(Sneaky trees)
.

Let
\Pi\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be the germ of a
D_{4}^{-}
-singularity, modeled as above, and
\Lambda(\Pi)
its Legendrian lift. There exists a compactly supported
C^{\infty}
-small Legendrian isotopy of
\Lambda(\Pi)
such that the resulting Legendrian
\Lambda^{\dagger}
has a front
\Pi^{\dagger}
such that:

(1)

The Lagrangian projection of
\Lambda^{\dagger}
is embedded.

(2)

there are exactly three rigid flowtrees
T_{0},T_{1},T_{2}
in
(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{x,y},g_{\text{st}})
for the generic Legendrian
\Lambda^{\dagger}
,

(3)

Each
T_{i}
is
C^{\infty}
-close to
R_{i}
and, outside a compact set,
T_{i}=R_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
.

(4)

The compact support of the Legendrian isotopy can be taken to be arbitrarily small near the domain of the
D_{4}^{-}
-singularity.

Figure 10
.
(Left) The spatial front for a generic
C^{\infty}
-small perturbation of the
D_{4}^{-}
-front singularity. (Right) The view for this front from above, with orange encoding
A_{2}
-cusp edges and dark blue edges indicating
A_{1}^{2}
-crossing edges. Each of the three orange dots indicates a
A_{3}
-swallowtail singularity.

Proof.

Choose a
C^{\infty}
-small Legendrian isotopy of
\Lambda(\Pi)
which preserves the
(2\pi/3)
-rotation symmetry of the front and let
\Pi^{\dagger}
be its front. Due to this symmetric choice and the fact that a flowtree between two sheets cannot pass through a crossing of those sheets, it suffices to analyze rigid flow trees in the sectorial region above
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
. A
C^{\infty}
-small perturbation of
D_{4}^{-}
-singularity gives a front
\Pi
with three swallowtails as depicted in Figure
10
. In Figure
10
(right) we choose this region betwee
E_{1},E_{2}
to be the bottom sector, so that
R_{0}
is a vertical semiray from the center downwards. Since the perturbation is compactly supported and the gradient condition is local, any rigid flowtrees for
\Pi
coincide with those of
\Pi^{\dagger}
away from a compact set, and thus must coincide with
R_{0},R_{1},R_{2}
. The argument now contains two parts:

(1)

the construction of a rigid Morse flow tree
\Gamma(D_{4}^{-})
coinciding with
R_{0}
at the boundary,

(2)

showing that no other rigid trees exist in this local model.

Figure 11
.
(Left) The flowtree
\Gamma(D_{4}^{-})
as seen from above, with two black dots indicating its unique switch (S) and end (E). (Right) Three homology classes
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
in the (Legendrian lift of the) front and the flowtree
\Gamma(D_{4}^{-})
seen from far above, so that the three swallowtails are contained in the central red dot.

For Part (1), a flowtree
\Gamma(D_{4}^{-})
is constructed following Figures
11
and
12
. In Figure
11
(left) the flowtree is depicted in green, as seen from above, and Figure
11
(right) determines the relative homology class of its boundary by declaring that it geometrically intersects each relative cycles
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
once,
e_{1},e_{3}
positively and
e_{2}
negatively. We have also described
\Gamma(D_{4}^{-})
in Figure
12
as a movie, slicing the front in Figure
11
(left) with horizontal slices, from bottom to top. This flowtree
\Gamma(D_{4}^{-})
has an asymptotic positive puncture
\rho
at the boundary condition given by
R_{0}
, no negative punctures, one
switch
(S) and one
end
(E). This is a legitimate flowtree by
[
Ekh07
, Section 2]
. We choose the perturbation of
\Lambda(\Pi)
and perturb the metric
g
to
g^{\dagger}
such that the tree is transversely cut out, which can be done by
[
Ekh07
, Prop. 3.14]
. The dimension of the moduli space for a general tree
\Gamma
is given in
[
Ekh07
, Definition 3.4]
as:

\dim(\Gamma)=-2+I_{u}(\rho^{+})-\left(\sum_{k=1}^{|\Gamma^{-}|}(I_{s}(\gamma_{k}^{-})-n+1)\right)+e(\Gamma)-s(\Gamma)-y_{1}(\Gamma),

where
I_{u}(\rho^{+})=2
is the index at the positive puncture
\rho^{+}
, i.e. the dimension
\dim(W^{u}(\rho^{+}))
of the unstable manifold,
I_{s}(\gamma^{-})=2
is the coindex at the negative puncture
\gamma^{-}
, i.e. the dimension
\dim(W^{s}(\gamma^{-})
) of the stable manifold,
e(\Gamma)
is the number of ends in
\Gamma
,
s(\Gamma)
is the number of switches and
y_{1}(\Gamma)
the number of
Y_{1}
-type vertices. In the case of
\Gamma(D_{4}^{-})
, the formula for the dimension yields

\dim(\Gamma)=-2+I_{u}(\rho)+e(\Gamma)-s(\Gamma)=-2+2+1-1=0,

as there are no negative punctures nor
Y_{1}
-vertices. This proves
\Gamma(D_{4}^{-})
is a
rigid
flowtree.

Figure 12
.
Slices of the rigid flowtree
\Gamma(D_{4}^{-})
, drawn in red, in the germ of a
D_{4}^{-}
-front singularity. The front is sliced horizontally, bottom to top. A
switch
(S) occurs at the leftmost diagram in the second row and an
end
(E) occurs at the center diagram in the third row.

For Part (2), let us argue that no additional rigid flowtrees exist, which we deduce using the dimension formula for
\dim(\Gamma)
above. First, for any germ of a Legendrian singularity, there exists a front with no Reeb chords. Hence, given that
\rho
is the only allowed boundary condition away from the singularity and in the sectorial region between
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, there can be no negative punctures. By the classification of vertices in
[
Ekh07
, Section 3]
and the combinatorics from Figure
12
, the rigid flowtrees must have one end, and therefore one switch. Since the switch can only occur as in
\Gamma(D_{4}^{-})
, any rigid flowtree that matches
R_{0}
actually coincides with
\Gamma(D_{4}^{-})
. Finally, the combinatorics of the sheets imply that no two of the three rigid flowtrees, given by
\Gamma(D_{4}^{-})
and its images under the
(2\pi/3)
-rotation, can interact with a
Y_{0}
-vertex. Thus these are the only possible rigid flowtrees.
∎

Remark 3.24
.

These trees in the proof of
Lemma

3.23
were first introduced by the first author in his work
[
CM18
]
, and referred to as sneaky trees, due to their shape.
12
12

12

A coarse chalkboard explanation of these trees can be found in the talk “Differential Algebra of Cubic Graphs” at the Harvard Center of Mathematical Sciences for “A Celebration of Symplectic Geometry 2017: 15 Years of JSG.”, around minute 53.

\Box

3.6.2.
Proof of
Proposition

3.22

This is now a consequence of
Lemma

3.23
and the bijection established in
[
Ekh07
]
. The only point left is appropriately choosing the neighborhoods of the
D_{4}^{-}
-singularities, i.e. the initial vertices of
\EuScript{W}
, cut off the part of the rigid flow-trees that begin at such neighbourhoods, and arguing that the resulting partial flowtrees can be extended. For each
D_{4}^{-}
-singularity
b\in S
, we choose a neighborhood
U_{b}(\delta)\subset S
whose only initial vertex is
b
, there are no non-initial vertices of
\EuScript{W}
, and
\partial U_{b}(\delta)
transversely intersects
\EuScript{W}
at the three initial rays associated to
b
. The perturbation needed is now obtained by locally perturbing according to
Lemma

3.23
at each such
U_{b}(\delta)
. Since the rigid flow-trees must begin at ends, which are located at the cusp-edges, we see that we can cut-off rigid flow-trees along edges that first leave the
U_{b}(\delta)
. Note that the resulting partial flow tree in
U_{b}(\delta)
can be extended to a global flowtree using
Lemma

2.17
.
Such extension is rigid, as rigidity depends only on the vertices of the flowtree, and so it must have been the sneaky trees constructed in
Lemma

3.23
. Then the property in Part (1) holds by construction, and Part (2) holds by
[
Ekh07
, Theorem 1.1.(b)]
.
\Box

Remark 3.25
.

(i) Technically, the perturbation in
[
Ekh07
, Theorem 1.1]
is global, and thus the spectral network
\EuScript{W}
is perturbed by a
C^{\infty}
-small smooth isotopy because of transversality. We implicitly absorb such smooth isotopy in our notation, as spectral networks are invariant under such isotopies.

(ii) We are implicitly using that the exact Betti Lagrangian
L
is Maslov
0
, which will be shown in
Lemma

5.13
, which tells us that the topological index of the boundary must be zero. The topological dimension of such a disk (with single positive puncture) is zero only if the limiting Reeb chord has index
1
(and so the index of the limiting puncture is
2
). This is if and only if the Reeb chord corresponds to a positive crossing, by the argument used in
Section

2.5
(see also
Fig.

5
for a heuristic explanation).

\Box

4.
Adiabatic convergence for
D_{4}^{-}
-trees

The main object of this section is to prove the direction
(\Longleftarrow)
of the characterization in
Theorem

2
. Namely, we show the contrapositive statement: points in the complement of the spectral network
\EuScript{W}
do
not
have such pseudo-holomorphic strips through them and, for
Theorem

2
.(i), that for a point in the spectral network there are no such pseudo-holomorphic strips in any relative homology class except for possibly the soliton class. The precise statement is
Theorem

4.2
. The direction
(\Longrightarrow)
is proven in
Section

5
.

The image of a Betti Lagrangian
L\subset T^{*}S
under the
\varepsilon
-scaling action on
(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
is denoted
\varepsilon L\subset T^{*}S
. Throughout the manuscript, the expression
adiabatic limit
refers to the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
and for this section we assume that
L
has either conical ends or is meromorphic with
O(-1)
-ends. The following notions formalize the scenarios with non-existence of pseudo-holomorphic strips in the adiabatic limit:

Definition 4.1
.

Let
({\bf S},\boldsymbol{m},\Lambda)
be a Betti surface,
L\subset T^{*}S
a Betti Lagrangian adapted to
(S,g)
and
J_{g}
the almost complex structure associated to
g
. Consider a spectral network
\EuScript{W}
compatible with
L
, as in
Definition

3.12
. For a point
z\in S
and the cotangent fiber
F_{z}=T^{*}_{z}S
:

(1)

The pair of Lagrangians
(F_{z},L)
is said to be
uniformly disk-free
in the adiabatic limit if for any given energy
E\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, there is some neighborhood
U_{z}(E)\subset S
of
z
and a positive constant
\varepsilon_{0}(E,z)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that there is no non-constant
J_{g}
-holomorphic disk of energy below
E
between the Lagrangians
\varepsilon L
and
F_{w}
, for all
w\in U_{z}(E)
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}(E,z)]
.

(2)

If
z\in\EuScript{W}
belongs to a wall, the pair of Lagrangians
(F_{z},L)
is said to be
uniformly Stokes
in the adiabatic limit if for any given energy
E\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, there is some neighborhood
U_{z}(E)\subset S
of
z
and a positive constant
\varepsilon_{0}(E,z)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that there is no non-constant
J_{g}
-holomorphic disk of energy below
E
bounded between the Lagrangians
\varepsilon L
and
F_{w}
whose relative homology class is not of the parallel transport of the soliton class associated to
z
, for all
w\in U_{z}(E)
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}(E,z)]
.
\Box

That is, informally,
(L,F_{z})
is uniformly disk-free if there are no pseudo-holomorphic strips bounded by
(\varepsilon L,F_{w})
for
\varepsilon\ll 1
small enough and
w
close to
z
, for a given energy cut-off
E\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Similarly for uniformly Stokes. The use of the word
uniformly
in
Definition

4.1
is to emphasize that the neighborhood
U_{z}(E)
is independent of the adiabatic parameter
\varepsilon
. Since every statement in this section will be in the adiabatic limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
we often drop the cue
in the adiabatic limit
, e.g. we simply refer to being uniformly disk-free or uniformly Stokes. The main result, implying the first part of
Theorem

2
, reads as follows:

Theorem 4.2

(Pseudo-holomorphic strips in spectral networks)
.

Let
({\bf S},\boldsymbol{m},\Lambda)
be a Betti surface,
L\subset T^{*}S
a Betti Lagrangian adapted to
(S,g)
and
\EuScript{W}
a compatible spectral network. Then

(1)

If
z\in\EuScript{W}^{c}
, then
(F_{z},L)
is uniformly disk-free.

(2)

If
z\in\EuScript{W}
, then
(F_{z},L)
is uniformly Stokes.

The purpose of this section is to prove
Theorem

4.2
. The intuition for Part (1) is summarized as follows. If
z\in\EuScript{W}^{c}
was such that
(\varepsilon L,F_{z_{\varepsilon}})
bounded a pseudo-holomorphic strip in
(T^{*}S,J_{g})
, then we want to have such sequences of strips converge, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, to a
D_{4}^{-}
-tree in
S
passing through
z
. If such convergence statement held, then the results in
Section

3
would force the
D_{4}^{-}
-tree to be contained in
\EuScript{W}
and thus
z\in\EuScript{W}
, reaching a contradiction. We remark that all the results we prove in this section rely on the relevant disks having uniformly bounded diameter (c.f.
Lemma

A.2
), which follows from the geometric boundedness of
L
(
Proposition

A.1
).

Of course the technical part is establishing convergence results for such pseudo-holomorphic strips with boundary conditions on
(\varepsilon L,F_{z})
in a manner that the limit is indeed a
D_{4}^{-}
-tree, or at least it is a
D_{4}^{-}
-tree away from a fixed small neighborhood of the branch points. Note that this is a much stronger result than just stating that the strips would converge to a flowtree, as such a weaker statement would provide no control on the trajectories associated to leaves and semi-infinite edges of the tree, and thus the resulting tree would typically
not
be a
D_{4}^{-}
-tree nor there would be any understanding of their behavior with regards to the initial rays at the branch points. In particular, a new aspect in the proof of
Theorem

4.2
is that we must have control on the behavior of the degeneration of such pseudo-holomorphic strips as it approaches the boundary of a neighborhood of the branch points, even if
z\in\EuScript{W}^{c}
is typically far from any branch points. The precise convergence result that we establish reads as follows:

Proposition 4.3

(
\varepsilon
-strips converge to trimmed
D_{4}^{-}
-trees)
.

Let
({\bf S},\boldsymbol{m},\Lambda)
be a Betti surface and
L\subset T^{*}S
a Betti Lagrangian adapted to
(S,g)
with a compatible spectral network
\EuScript{W}
. Suppose that
(u_{\varepsilon},z_{\varepsilon},\Delta_{m}(\varepsilon),E)
is an
\varepsilon
-strip sequence
u_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Delta_{2}\longrightarrow S
, with boundary of
(\varepsilon L,T^{*}_{z_{\varepsilon}}S)
. Then, after possibly considering a subsequence and a reparametrization of the domain
\Delta_{2}
, its limit

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}(u_{\varepsilon},z_{\varepsilon},\Delta_{2},E)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Gamma\longrightarrow S

exists and it is a (broken) trimmed
D_{4}^{-}
-tree. The tree is unbroken if
z
lies in the interior of
\EuScript{W}(E)
.

The concepts needed to state
Proposition

4.3
, including the notion of
\varepsilon
-strip sequences, their limits and broken trimmed
D_{4}^{-}
-trees, are introduced in
Section

4.1
and
Proposition

4.3
is then proven in
Section

4.2
.
Proposition

4.3
is used to conclude
Theorem

2
in
Section

4.3
.
13
13

13

Note that it is nevertheless possible to upgrade
Proposition

4.3
to show convergence to an actual (broken)
D_{4}^{-}
-tree. This would add even more technicality and pages to the manuscript and, for the sake of balance, we have not pursued this technical enhancement.

4.1.
\varepsilon
-strips and their limits

Let us first set up the geometric ingredients to discuss
\varepsilon
-strips and their limits. We endow its cotangent bundle
(T^{*}S,\omega)
of
(S,g)
with the compatible almost-complex structure
J_{g}
associated to
\omega
and
g
via the Levi-Civita (Ehresmann) connection of the latter;
J_{g}
is also known as the Sasaki almost-complex structure.

We use the following type of domains for pseudo-holomorphic strips: fix
\xi\in(0,1)
,
\xi\ll 1
, and consider a point
c=(c_{1},...,c_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. By definition,
\triangle_{d+1}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{d})
is the subdomain of
(-\infty,\infty)\times[0,d+1]\subset\mathbb{C}
given by removing
d
horizontal slits of vertical width
\xi
, each starting at the point
(c_{j},j)
in the direction of
+\infty
, for
j\in[1,d]
. Each such horizontal slit is thus centered around the semi-infinite ray
[c_{j},\infty)\times\{j\}
, see e.g. Figure
13
. If the input position
c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
depends on a parameter
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
but
c
is implicitly understood (or arbitrary and not particularly relevant), then we denote such subdomain by
\triangle_{d+1}(\varepsilon)
. Such domains
\triangle_{d+1}(\varepsilon)
are given the conformal structure inherited as subdomains of
\mathbb{C}
.

Definition 4.4
.

Let
({\bf S},\boldsymbol{m},{\bf\Lambda})
be a Betti surface and
L\subset T^{*}S
a Betti Lagrangian adapted to
(S,g)
. By definition, an
\varepsilon
-strip sequence
(u_{\varepsilon},z_{\varepsilon},\triangle_{m}(\varepsilon),E)
is a collection of
J_{g}
-holomorphic maps

u_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\triangle_{m}(\varepsilon)\longrightarrow T^{*}S,

defined for all
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
small enough and such that:

(1)

the image of
u_{\varepsilon}
is bounded by
L
and
F_{z_{\varepsilon}}
,

(2)

\mbox{area}(u_{\varepsilon})\leq\varepsilon E
.
\Box

An additional piece of notation on such domains
\triangle_{d+1}(c)
: given a boundary component of
\partial\triangle_{d+1}(c)
with both of its ends at
+\infty
, its
boundary minimum
of
I
the unique point with the smallest real part. Also, a subdomain in
\triangle_{d+1}
is said to be
horizontal
if it is of the form
[x_{1},x_{2}]\times[y_{1},y_{2}]
with
[x_{1},x_{2}]\times\{y_{1}\}
and
[x_{1},x_{2}]\times\{y_{2}\}
lying on the boundary of
\triangle_{d+1}
. See Figure
13
.

Figure 13
.
A depiction of the type of domain
\triangle_{d+1}(c)
being used to describe the conformal structure. The domain is drawn in the strip with the slits highlighted in black, and the conformal structure in inherited from
\mathbb{C}
. The
x
-coordinate of the boundary minima is highlighted with a yellow vertical dashed line and the tree associated to the domain is depicted in green.

4.1.1.
Combinatorial trees associated to domains
\triangle_{d+1}

There is a (combinatorial) tree associated to each of the domains
\triangle_{d+1}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{d})
, constructed as follows.

Definition 4.5

(Trees associated to
\triangle_{d+1}
)
.

Let
\triangle_{d+1}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{d})\subset\mathbb{C}
be a domain, its associated tree
T\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=T(\triangle(c))
is the rooted
d
-leaved tree whose interior vertices have valence at least three and satisfies:

(1)

The vertices of
T
are in bijection with the values of
c\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
. These can be visually described by vertical rays passing through the boundary minima of
\triangle_{d+1}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{d})
.

(2)

The edges of
T
are in (natural) bijection with the connected components of the complement of such vertical rays in
\triangle_{d+1}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{d})
.

(3)

By definition, two edges of
T
share a vertex if and only if the corresponding components of those two edges are adjacent along the vertical ray corresponding to that vertex.
\Box

See Figure
13
for an example of
Definition

4.5
. By definition, an open subdomain
D\subset\triangle_{d+1}
is a
subdisk
if the part
\partial D\setminus(\partial\triangle_{d+1})
of its boundary consist of vertical rays avoiding the boundary minima.
14
14

14

These subdisks are often disconnected, each connected component being topologically a disk.
As in
Definition

4.5
, subdisks
D\subset\triangle_{d+1}
yield rooted
(d+1)
-leaved subtrees
T_{D}\subset T
by introducing vertices at each of the edges whose corresponding components contain the vertical boundaries of
\partial D
. We denote by
T/T_{D}
the rooted
(d+1)
-leaved tree obtained by contracting the subtree
T_{D}\subset T
associated to a subdisk
D\subset\triangle
.

We shall cover these domains
\triangle
by subdisk domains. We always implicitly assume that the vertical boundary segments of the components of such covers avoid the boundary minima, are all disjoint, and their intersections are also given by subdisk domains. In the same manner that a subdisk gave a subtree, an open cover
\triangle_{m}=D_{0}\cup D_{1}
by two subdisk domains
D_{0},D_{1}
gives an open cover of the associated tree:
T(\triangle)=T(D_{0})\cup T(D_{1})
. Note that
D_{0},D_{1}
are typically each disconnected.

4.1.2.
Limits of
\varepsilon
-strip sequences

The goal of this subsection is to rigorously explain what it means for an
\varepsilon
-strip sequence, as in
Definition

4.4
, to converge to a trimmed
D_{4}^{-}
-tree. In particular, we refine the type of domain subdivision introduced in
[
Ekh07
, Section 5.2]
, introduce adapted covers in
Definition

4.6
and limits of
\varepsilon
-strip sequences in
Definition

4.8
.

For each branch point
b\in S
, we have fixed a neighborhood
U_{b}\subset S
as in
Section

3.3.1
. We can and do choose such
U_{b}
to be disks of a certain radius
r
, denoting that by
U_{b}=U_{b}(r)
. Specifically, we fix two truncation parameters
\delta,\eta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that the disks
U_{b}(2\delta+\eta\delta)
are disjoint and over each subdisk
U_{b}(2\delta)
, the piece of the Betti Lagrangian
L\cap T^{*}U_{b}(2\delta)\subset T^{*}S
is holomorphic and the metric is the standard flat metric restricted from
\mathbb{C}
. We denote the union of such neighborhoods by

B_{\eta,\delta}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\bigcup_{b\in K_{L}}U_{b}(2\delta+\eta\delta).

By definition, a broken trimmed
D_{4}^{-}
-tree is the intersection of a disjoint union of
D_{4}^{-}
-trees in
S
with the domain
S\setminus B_{\eta,\delta}
such that the flowlines entering each disk
U_{b}(2\delta+\eta\delta)
, if such flowlines exist, coincide with initial rays at
b\in S
. The tree is unbroken if there is a single
D_{4}^{-}
-tree component.

From now on, whenever we discuss the metric and the covariant derivative, we will assume that we are using the Sasaki metric, and the associated Levi-Civita connection. Given an
\varepsilon
-strip sequence
(u_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
, as in
Definition

4.4
, we want to understand its behavior in the adiabatic limit, i.e. as
\varepsilon\to 0
. As observed in
[
Ekh07
, Lemma 5.6]
,
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\nabla u_{\varepsilon}\right\rvert}=O(\varepsilon)
when restricted to a half-disk subdomain of fixed radius, that maps outside some fixed neighborhood of the caustics. However, near the caustics, we will not be guaranteed such a control. The idea is to subdivide the domains of each
u_{\varepsilon}
into open sets where we have some control of either
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\nabla u_{\varepsilon}\right\rvert}
or the image of
u_{\varepsilon}
. As per usual application of gradient estimates, convergence for points where
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\nabla u_{\varepsilon}\right\rvert}
remains uniformly
O(\epsilon)
-bounded follows (c.f.
[
Flo89a
, Theorem 2]
,
[
FO97
, Proposition 9.7, Proposition 9.8]
). The challenge is to find such subdivisions and control the behavior of the image when
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\nabla u_{\varepsilon}\right\rvert}
blows up.

The initial
J_{g}
-holomorphic strip, before performing any
\varepsilon
-scaling, has domain
\triangle_{2}
and maps to
(T^{*}S,J_{g})
, with two punctures, as the boundary lies in
L
and a cotangent fiber
F_{z}
, for some
z\in S
. In order to obtain the subdivision, we will reparametrize that domain
\triangle_{2}
, as we
\varepsilon
-scale, by a sequence of
\varepsilon
-depending domains
\triangle_{m}(\varepsilon)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\triangle_{m}(c(\varepsilon))
,
m\geq 3
, where the parameters
c(\varepsilon)
depend on the adiabatic parameter
\varepsilon
but the number
m\in\mathbb{N}
does not. A first useful open cover for these domains is described as follows:

Definition 4.6

(Adapted covers for
\varepsilon
-strip sequences)
.

Let
(u_{\varepsilon},z_{\varepsilon},\triangle_{m}(\varepsilon),E)
be an
\varepsilon
-strip sequence with
z_{\varepsilon}
converging to a point in
S\setminus B({2\delta+\eta\delta})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. By definition, an
\varepsilon
-sequence of open covers
\triangle_{m}(\varepsilon)=D_{0}(\varepsilon)\cup D_{1}(\varepsilon)
is said to be adapted to
(u_{\varepsilon},z_{\varepsilon},\triangle_{m}(\varepsilon),E)
if the following holds for
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
small enough:

(1)

Each
D_{i}(\varepsilon)
are subdisks,
i=1,2
.

(2)

The topology associated to the domains
D_{i}(\varepsilon)
is independent of
\varepsilon
,
i=1,2
. That is, the order of the parameters
c(\varepsilon)
, the topology of the components of
D_{i}(\varepsilon)
, and the topology of their intersections (eventually) remains constant as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

(3)

u_{\varepsilon}(D_{0}(\varepsilon))
lies outside
B(\delta)
and
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\nabla u_{\varepsilon}\right\rvert}=O(\varepsilon)
for points in
D_{0}(\varepsilon)
.

(4)

u_{\varepsilon}(D_{1}(\varepsilon))
lies inside
B(2\delta)
.

In addition, a subdisk
W_{0}(\varepsilon)\subset D_{0}(\varepsilon)
is said to give an adapted horizontal decomposition of
D_{0}(\varepsilon)
if

(i)

W_{0}
has width
O(\log(\varepsilon^{-1}))
and contains all the boundary minima contained in
D_{0}
.

(ii)

The vertical boundary part of
\partial W_{0}
is disjoint from the vertical boundaries of
D_{i}(\varepsilon)
,
i=1,2
, and its topology remains constant as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

A triple
(D_{0}(\varepsilon),D_{1}(\varepsilon),W_{0}(\varepsilon))
is said to be adapted to
(u_{\varepsilon},z_{\varepsilon},\triangle_{m}(\varepsilon),E)
if
\Delta_{m}(\varepsilon)=D_{0}(\varepsilon)\cup D_{1}(\varepsilon)
is an adapted cover and
W_{0}(\varepsilon)
is an adapted horizontal decomposition of
D_{0}(\varepsilon)
.
\Box

The geometric conditions on
u_{\varepsilon}
are
Definition

4.6
.(3),(4) and (i): on
D_{0}(\varepsilon)
the norm of the derivative
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\nabla u_{\varepsilon}\right\rvert}=O(\varepsilon)
remains controlled (constant with respect to the scaling measure), and the width condition
O(\log(\varepsilon^{-1}))
on its subdomains
W_{0}(\varepsilon)
ensures that the images
u_{\varepsilon}(W_{0})
converge to a point. In particular, the associated trees
T(\varepsilon)/T_{W_{0}(\varepsilon)}
give models for the domain of a map that contracts the connected subdisks in
W_{0}
to points.

Remark 4.7
.

The conditions
Definition

4.6
.(2) and (ii) imply that there is a fixed number of components for
D_{0}(\varepsilon),D_{1}(\varepsilon)
and
W_{0}(\varepsilon)
in an adapted cover, independent of
\varepsilon
. In fact, the combinatorial trees associated to each such domains, as in
Section

4.1.1
, are well-defined and independent of
\varepsilon
. In particular, the combinatorial type of the tree
\Gamma
associated to
\triangle_{m}(\varepsilon)
is independent of
\varepsilon
and so are the corresponding induced subtrees.
\Box

The adapted covers of
Definition

4.6
are still not sufficient to conclude
Proposition

4.3
, i.e. that the
\varepsilon
-strip sequence converges to a trimmed
D_{4}^{-}
-tree. The remaining additional condition that we must require (and we will prove can be achieved) reads as follows:

Definition 4.8

(Limits of
\varepsilon
-strip sequences)
.

The limit of an
\varepsilon
-strip sequence
(u_{\varepsilon},z_{\varepsilon},\triangle_{m}(\varepsilon),E)
is said to exist if there exists an adapted cover
(D_{0}(\varepsilon),D_{1}(\varepsilon),W_{0}(\varepsilon))
such that the following two types of conditions hold:

(1)

There are three types of components of
D_{0}\setminus W_{0}
: fiber, ghost or flowline. They are classified depending on their possible images under
u_{\varepsilon}
, as follows:

(i)

(Fiber components) Some horizontal boundary components map to the cotangent fiber, with projections
C^{\infty}
-converging to the point
\lim z_{\varepsilon}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

(ii)

(Ghost components) Horizontal boundaries map to the same sheets of
L
, with projections
C^{\infty}
-converging to points.

(iii)

(Flowline components) Horizontal boundaries map to different sheets of
L
if the domain has widths of size
O(\varepsilon^{-1})
and the (projection of the) rescaling
u\circ(\varepsilon^{-1})

C^{\infty}
-converges to a flowline. The flowlines are said to have zero length if the resulting limiting flowline is a point.

(2)

Let
T
be the tree associated to
\triangle_{m}(\varepsilon)
and consider the (reduced) tree
\Gamma^{red}
obtained by removing the subtrees of
T/T_{W_{0}(\varepsilon)}
corresponding to fiber components of
D_{0}\setminus W_{0}
, contracting the subtrees corresponding to the ghost components of
D_{0}\setminus W_{0}
, removing the subtrees corresponding to components in
D_{1}
, and removing any 2-valent vertices that connect the two flow-edges of the same index. Then, we require that
\Gamma^{red}
admits a set of edges such that:

(i)

(Edges map to initial
D_{4}^{-}
-rays) Each edge is such that there exists a vertical cut of the associated flowline component subdomain of
\triangle_{m}(\varepsilon)
with
u_{\varepsilon}
mapping its right adjacent component inside
T^{*}B_{\eta,\delta}
, and the edge converges to one of the initial rays associated to a branch point, with the
inward
orientation.
15
15

15

Note that the spectral network
\EuScript{W}
is oriented opposite to the flowline direction.

(ii)

(Maximality) For any flowline component
\Theta\subset D_{0}\setminus W_{0}
with non-zero flow, there exists an edge as in (
i
) that belongs to the right adjacent component of
\Theta
.
\Box

In essence, proving
Proposition

4.3
is tantamount to showing that, given the
\varepsilon
-strip sequence, there exists an adapted cover satisfying the conditions in
Definition

4.8
. The limiting object for the
\varepsilon
-strip sequence is technically not a flow-tree
F\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Gamma\longrightarrow S
due to the presence of the ghost components and flowlines components with zero lengths in
\triangle_{m}(\varepsilon)
, cf.
Definition

4.8
.(
1.ii
) and (
1.iii
). For instance, an entire ghost component might map to a point in the adiabatic limit; these are said to be ghost edges of
\Gamma
. This matter is resolved combinatorially by contracting the edges of the tree
\Gamma
associated to
\triangle_{m}(\varepsilon)
that correspond to these two types of components. The resulting tree is often referred to as the
reduced
tree, and one can effectively work with the reduced tree instead of the original
\Gamma
; this is what is happening in
Definition

4.8
.(2).

If the limit of an
\varepsilon
-strip sequence exists, as in
Definition

4.8
, it is denoted by

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}(u_{\varepsilon},z_{\varepsilon},\Delta_{m}(\varepsilon),E)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Gamma\longrightarrow S.

It is implicitly understood as a flow-tree with ghost components, so the map factors through its reduction
\Gamma^{red}
, as explicitly described in
Definition

4.8
.(2). In particular, the flowline edges of this limit are given by the adiabatic limit of the rescaling
u\circ(\varepsilon^{-1})
along each flowline component of non-zero length.

Remark 4.9
.

The limit of an
\varepsilon
-strip sequence depends on the truncation parameters
(\delta,\eta)
fixed initially and the corresponding trees
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\delta,\eta}
might not be nested if one decreases the parameters
\eta,\delta\to 0
. That said, the associated reduced trees
\Gamma^{red}
can (and we will show will) be nested, in that
\Gamma^{red}_{(\delta,\eta)}\subset\Gamma^{red}_{(\delta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})}
for smaller truncation parameters
(\delta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
. This is another technical reason for reduction.
\Box

4.2.
Proof of
Proposition

4.3

In this subsection we prove the convergence of
\varepsilon
-strip sequences to trimmed
D_{4}^{-}
-trees. In the initial setup, there are a number of technical steps which are mirroring
[
Ekh07
, Section 5]
and
[
Nho24
, Section 5]
; we thus simply refer the necessary parts of those manuscripts and continue focusing on the new part of the argument. Specifically, we must build an adapted cover as in
Definition

4.8
.

First, in order to build such cover satisfying the component constraints from
Definition

4.8
.(1), we proceed as follows. By Lemmata 5.7, 5.13, 5.16 and 5.17 in
[
Ekh07
]
, the domain
\triangle_{m}(\varepsilon)
itself admits an adapted cover
(D_{0}(\varepsilon),D_{1}(\varepsilon),W_{0}(\varepsilon))
. Here the horizontal decomposition subdisk
W_{0}(\varepsilon)
is obtained by considering subdisks which are neighborhoods of the boundary minima of the required width
O(\log(\varepsilon^{-1}))
.

Remark 4.10
.

(i) As far as we know, the construction of the domain-adapted cover in
[
Ekh07
, Section 5]
has a gap in the proof of
[
Ekh07
, Lemma 5.4]
, in the claim that such balls of radius
\delta\varepsilon
exist (his
\lambda
is our
\varepsilon
). It must be justified that there is a uniform upper bound on the boundary length of the images of
u_{\varepsilon}
outside of the union of the domains
T^{*}U_{b}(2\delta)
. This upper bound nevertheless exists, as it follows from the truncated reverse isoperimetric inequality that we prove in the appendix, cf.
Theorem

A.3
.

(ii) Lemmatas 5.6 and 5.7 in
[
Ekh07
]
are stated only for exact Lagrangians. However, the only place the proof uses exactnesss is to establish the
O(\varepsilon)
-estimate on energy. Since this condition is part of the definition of an
\varepsilon
-strip sequence, the proof goes through verbatim.

\Box

The components of
D_{0}(\varepsilon)\setminus W_{0}(\varepsilon)
consist of horizontal subdomains of
\triangle_{m}
of the three types required in
Definition

4.8
.(1). By
[
Nho24
, Lemma 5.15]
, the fiber components
C^{\infty}
-converge to the point
\lim z_{\varepsilon}
. By
[
Nho24
, Lemma 5.17]
, the ghost components also
C^{\infty}
-converge to a point. By
[
Ekh07
, Lemma 5.18]
(see also
[
Nho24
, Lemma 5.17]
), the flowline components
C^{\infty}
-converges to flowlines, after taking the
\varepsilon
-dependent reparametrization
u_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{-1}s,\varepsilon^{-1}t)
. Therefore, the new contribution at this point is to construct the adapted cover such that
Definition

4.8
.(2) holds: controlling the behavior of the limit flowtree near the branch points.

Given the
\varepsilon
-strip sequence
(u_{\varepsilon},z_{\varepsilon},\triangle_{m}(\varepsilon),E)
, denote by
\Gamma
the tree associated to the domain
\triangle_{m}(\varepsilon)
, which is well-defined by the adapted cover constructed above, cf.
Remark

4.7
. Note that, as in
[
Nho24
, Lemma 5.18]
, the
\varepsilon
-finiteness of energy implies that there cannot be flowline edges of infinite length. Thus, given the bound on the covariant derivative, any leaf in (the subtree associated to) the
D_{0}(\varepsilon)
-region of
\Gamma
corresponds to either a ghost component or a fiber component. In particular, there can be no leaves that are flowline edges.Furthermore, the following lemma tells us that there exists at least one external
D_{1}
-edge of
\Gamma
:

Lemma 4.11
.

There exists at least one external edge of
\Gamma
that corresponds to a horizontal component in
D_{1}(\varepsilon)
.

Proof.

Suppose by contradiction that there is no such an external edge. By construction, this is only if the image of
u_{\varepsilon}
lies outside some fixed neighbourhood of the branch points, for small enough
\varepsilon
. It follows that for the given subsequence, the puncture removal step was unnecessary and
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\nabla u_{\varepsilon}\right\rvert}=O(\varepsilon)
. However, there is then a single horizontal strip component which necessarily must be a fiber component. Therefore, the image of
u_{\varepsilon}
converges to the point
\lim z_{\varepsilon}
and that implies that the geometric energy must have been zero, which a contradiction.
∎

Let us cut the domain
\triangle_{m}=\triangle_{m}(\varepsilon)
along the vertical rays through the boundary minima of
\triangle_{m}
that connect to a horizontal boundary component mapping to the fiber. We denote by
\bar{\triangle}_{m_{1}},...,\bar{\triangle}_{m_{n}}
the resulting right-adjacent components and by
\Gamma_{m_{i}}
the induced tree in
\Gamma
associated to each such components. By the above, the
D_{0}(\varepsilon)
-leaves of
\Gamma_{m_{i}}
are all ghost components.

Consider the set of vertices contained in
\Gamma_{m_{i}}
that are terminal points of a flowline edge of non-zero length; such a vertex is not
1
-valent. These vertices are ordered by their
x
-coordinate, and a
maximal vertex
(for a given
m_{i}
) refers to such a vertex with highest
x
-coordinate within
\Gamma_{m_{i}}
. In particular, given two maximal vertices
v,w
, their right-adjacent components are necessarily disjoint. Let
v
be a maximal vertex, and let
\Theta_{v}=\Theta_{v}(\varepsilon)
be the corresponding horizontal domain in
D_{0}(\varepsilon)\setminus W_{0}(\varepsilon)
. Then the component
\Theta_{v}
associated to a maximal vertex satisfies the following:

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
v\in\Gamma
be a maximal vertex as above. Then there exists a branch point
b(v)\in S
such that the right-adjacent components of
\Theta_{v}(\varepsilon)
are mapped into the neighborhood
T^{\ast}U_{b(v)}((2+\eta)\delta)
.

Proof.

Since
\triangle_{m}=D_{0}(\varepsilon)\cup D_{1}(\varepsilon)
is adapted, the components in
D_{1}(\varepsilon)
map into the region
T^{\ast}B_{\eta,\delta}\subset T^{*}S
. By maximality, it also follows that the horizontal domains of
D_{0}(\varepsilon)\setminus W_{0}(\varepsilon)
right-adjacent to
\Theta_{v}
are either ghosts, fiber components or flowline components of zero length. Since there are finitely many such components, their images
C^{\infty}
-converge to points, and by construction the subdisk
W_{0}
also converges to points. Since the neighborhoods
U_{b}(2\delta+\eta\delta)
are all disjoint and the right-adjacent component to
\Theta_{v}
is connected, it must map into one connected component
T^{\ast}U_{b}(2\delta+\eta\delta)
of
T^{\ast}B_{\eta,\delta}
, which determines the branch point
b(v)=b\in S
.
∎

Now we arrive at the crux of the argument: we must show that
\Theta_{v}(\varepsilon)
, possibly after refining with vertical cuts, is such that its right-adjacent component not only maps to
T^{\ast}U_{b(v)}(2\delta+\eta\delta)
but does so converging near
T^{\ast}\partial(U_{b(v)}(2\delta+\eta\delta))
to an initial flowline ray of the
D_{4}^{-}
-branch point
b(v)\in S
in the adiabatic limit. This is the content of the following:

Proposition 4.13

(Behavior near
D_{4}^{-}
-points)
.

Let
(u_{\varepsilon},z_{\varepsilon},\triangle_{m}(\varepsilon),E)
be an
\varepsilon
-strip sequence,
v\in\Gamma
a maximal vertex and
b(v)\in S
its associated branch point. Then, after possibly taking a subsequence, there exists a sequence of vertical cuts
v_{\varepsilon}
for the domain
\Theta_{v}(\varepsilon)
such that the restriction
u_{\varepsilon}|_{\Theta^{\rightarrow}_{v}}
of
u_{\varepsilon}
converges to one of the initial flowline rays of
b(v)
, where
\Theta^{\rightarrow}_{v}
denotes the right adjacent component of
\Theta_{v}(\varepsilon)
after the cuts.

Proof.

By construction, the open set
U_{b(v)}({2\delta+\eta\delta})
is such that the metric
g
is flat, the associated Sasaki metric coincides with the standard flat metric on
\mathbb{C}^{2}\cong T^{*}\mathbb{C}
, and the Betti Lagrangian
L\subset T^{*}S
above this open set splits into the holomorphic cusp component and the smooth sheet components. Consider the limiting flowline
\gamma_{v}
associated to the component
\Theta_{v}
. Since it is not a ghost edge, fiber component or zero-length flowline, it must (eventually) enter
B_{\eta,\delta}
, specifically the connected component containing
b(v)\in S
. Choose
\eta^{\prime}\in(\eta/2,\eta)
such that
\gamma_{v}
is transverse to the boundary
\partial B_{\eta^{\prime},\delta}
and let
v(z)
be the final intersection point at which
\gamma_{v}
is oriented inward. After passing to a subsequence, there is a sequence of vertical rays
v_{\varepsilon}
for
\Theta_{v}
converging to the point
v(z)
; fix such a sequence of rays. We claim that the energy
\int\lambda_{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}(\varepsilon^{-1}(u_{\varepsilon}|_{v_{\varepsilon}}))
of the
\varepsilon
-scaled
u_{\varepsilon}
converges to zero along these vertical cuts.

Indeed, write the restriction
u_{\varepsilon}|_{v_{\varepsilon}}=(z_{\varepsilon},w_{\varepsilon})
in coordinates and apply the gradient estimate
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\nabla z_{\varepsilon}\right\rvert}=O(\varepsilon)
to obtain

(22)

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\int_{v_{\varepsilon}}\lambda_{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}({\varepsilon}^{-1}u_{\varepsilon})|_{v_{\varepsilon}}\right\rvert}\leq m\cdot\sup\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\varepsilon^{-1}w_{\varepsilon}\right\rvert}\cdot\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\nabla z_{\varepsilon}\right\rvert}.

Here the quantity
m
appears in the upper bound because the height of
\Theta_{v}
is at most
m
. Since
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\varepsilon^{-1}w_{\varepsilon}\right\rvert}
is uniformly bounded and
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\nabla z_{\varepsilon}\right\rvert}
is of order
O(\varepsilon)
, the integral on the left converges to zero in the adiabatic limit, as claimed.

Let
v_{\varepsilon}(\text{top})
and
v_{\varepsilon}(\text{bottom})
denote the top and the bottom end-points of each vertical cut
v_{\varepsilon}
in
\Theta_{v}
,
\triangle_{v_{\varepsilon}}
the right-adjacent components to
v_{\varepsilon}
and
\partial^{h}(\triangle_{v_{\varepsilon}})
the horizontal boundary of
\triangle_{v_{\varepsilon}}
. Since the restriction
u_{\varepsilon}|_{\triangle_{v_{\varepsilon}}}
is
J_{g}
-holomorphic, with
g=g_{\text{st}}
, the pointwise symplectic area
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}u_{\varepsilon}
over
{\triangle_{v_{\varepsilon}}}
is real and positive, except possibly at finitely many points. Therefore, the symplectic area

(23)

\int_{\triangle_{v_{\varepsilon}}}\omega_{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}u_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}

is real and positive, which will be used momentarily in
Eq.

24
. Independently, the restriction
u|_{\partial^{h}(\triangle_{v})}
extends to a connected curve in
L
and, since
\Theta_{v}
is a flowline component,
\partial^{h}(\Theta_{v})
maps to distinct sheets of the Betti Lagrangian
L
. Given that the smooth sheets of
L
are disjoint over
B_{\eta,\delta}
,
Lemma

4.12
implies that
u|_{\partial^{h}(\triangle_{v})}
must map to the holomorphic cusp component. Thus the flowline
\gamma_{v}
is a cusp-cusp flowline.

Let us finally deduce that
\gamma_{v}
coincides with an initial trivalent ray at
b
, as follows. By applying Stokes’ theorem, the
\varepsilon
-scaled energy reads

(24)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{\triangle_{v_{\varepsilon}}}\omega_{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}u_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{\partial^{h}\triangle_{\varepsilon}}\lambda_{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}\partial u_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{v_{\varepsilon}}\lambda_{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}u_{\varepsilon}|_{v_{\varepsilon}}=\pm\Big((z(v_{\varepsilon}(\text{top}))^{3/2}-(z(v_{\varepsilon}(\text{bottom}))^{3/2}\Big)-\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{v_{\varepsilon}}\lambda_{\mathbb{C}}^{\ast}u|_{v_{\varepsilon}}.

By
Eq.

22
, the second term on the right hand side of
Eq.

24
converges to zero in the adiabatic limit
\varepsilon\to 0
. Since
(z(v_{\varepsilon}(\text{top}))
and
(z(v_{\varepsilon}(\text{bottom}))
converge to
z(v)
, the right hand side of
Eq.

24
converges to
\pm z(v)^{3/2}
. By
Eq.

23
, the left hand side of
Eq.

24
is real and positive and thus
\pm z(v)^{3/2}
is also real and positive. This can only occur if the (image of the) point
v
lied on one of the initial rays for its associated branch point
b(v)
. Since the cusp-cusp flowlines form a singular foliation, the leaves of which lie outside
b(v)
, except for the three trivalent rays, we see that the restriction
u_{\varepsilon}|\Theta_{v}
must converge to one of the initial rays coming out of
b(v)
in the adiabatic limit.
∎

Let us apply
Proposition

4.13
to each maximal vertex
v^{i}
associated to the subdomains
\triangle_{m_{i}}
for the initial adapted cover
(D_{0}(\varepsilon),D_{1}(\varepsilon),W_{0}(\varepsilon)
at the beginning of the argument.
Proposition

4.13
implies that these adapted covers can be refined to satisfy the condition in
Definition

4.8
.(2.i). The maximality condition
Definition

4.8
.(2.ii) follows from our choice of domains
\triangle_{m_{i}}
and maximal vertices. In consequence, there exists an adapted cover for the given
\varepsilon
-sequence such that its limit exists.

Let
\{\Gamma\}
be the set of all
D_{4}^{-}
-trees in
\EuScript{W}
with the univalent edges given by the initial edges of
(u_{\varepsilon},z_{\varepsilon},\triangle(\varepsilon),E)
, with energy less than
E
. The energy and the endpoint constraint imply that the set of such trees must be finite. By construction, each component
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}(u_{\varepsilon},z_{\varepsilon},\triangle(\varepsilon),E)
must be a trimmed
D_{4}^{-}
-tree in
\{\Gamma\}
, since the initial edges in
Proposition

4.13
are directed inward. Furthermore, the internal vertices of such trees can only appear at the joints of
\EuScript{W}(E)
. It follows that the
D_{4}^{-}
-tree is contained entirely in
\EuScript{W}(E)
. Furthermore, in the case
z
lies in the interior of
\EuScript{W}(E)
, the tree cannot be broken because their soliton classes cannot concatenate. Thus we have established
Proposition

4.3
.
\Box

4.3.
Proof of
Theorem

4.2

For Part (1), suppose that the point
z\in\EuScript{W}^{c}
is not uniformly disk-free. By definition, there exists an
\varepsilon
-strip sequence
(u_{\varepsilon},z_{\varepsilon},\triangle_{m}(\varepsilon),E)
with
z=\lim z_{\varepsilon}
. Consider the spectral subnetwork
\EuScript{W}(E)\subset\EuScript{W}
with energy at most
E
and fix the truncation parameters
\delta,\eta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
as in the proof of
Proposition

4.3
, so that the only vertices of
\EuScript{W}(E)
that lie in
B_{\eta,\delta}
are the initial trivalent vertices.
Proposition

4.3
implies that, after possibly considering a subsequence, the limit
\lim(u_{\varepsilon},z_{\varepsilon},\triangle_{m}(\varepsilon),E)
exists and it is a trimmed
D_{4}^{-}
-tree through
z
with energy below
E
. Furthermore, such a
D_{4}^{-}
tree must be entirely contained in
\EuScript{W}(E)
. It thus follows that
z\in\EuScript{W}(E)
, which contradicts the initial hypothesis
z\in\EuScript{W}^{c}
. Part (2) follows analogously.
\Box

5.
Family Floer and Non-abelianized Local Systems

Let
(S,\boldsymbol{m},{\bf\Lambda})
be a Betti surface and
L\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
a Betti Lagrangian of rank
n
. The object of this section is two-fold. First, in the exact case, to construct two functors
\mathbb{F}
and
\Phi{W}
that carry
\mbox{GL}_{1}
-local systems on the Betti Lagrangian to
\mbox{GL}_{n}
-local systems on the base
S
and show that they are equivalent. Second, finish the proof of
Theorem

2
, which concerns both exact and meromorphic Betti Lagrangians. The construction of the functors depends on the existence of a compatible spectral network
\EuScript{W}\subset S
. In a nutshell:

(1)

The functor
\Phi{W}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}_{1}(L)\longrightarrow\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}_{n}(S)

is constructed by considering a variant of
D_{4}^{-}
-trees, with the parallel transport of image local system being modified according to the interaction of a given path in
S
with the spectral network
\EuScript{W}
. It is constructed in
Section

5.1
.
16
16

16

Technically,
\Phi{W}(V)
has only parallel transport defined for
\EuScript{W}
-paths. In contrast,
\mathbb{F}(V)
has parallel transport for all paths.

(2)

The functor
\mathbb{F}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}_{1}(L)\longrightarrow\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}_{n}(S)
is constructed by considering continuation maps between the Family Floer cohomology groups of the Lagrangian
L
with the cotangent fibres of
T^{*}S
. It is constructed in
Section

5.2
.

(3)

A natural transformation between these two functors is established in
Section

5.3
. The proof of
Theorem

2
is then completed in
Section

5.4
.

Note that the projection
L\longrightarrow S
, given by restricting to
L
the projection
T^{*}S\longrightarrow S
to the zero section, is a branch cover and thus the push-foward of a local system on
L
does
not
give a local system on
S
, just a constructible sheaf on
S
. The point of these two functors above is that they conceptually explain how to correct such constructible sheaf on
S
(coming from the push-forward of a local system on
L
) back to a local system on
S
. Each approach presents a different type of correction:
\Phi{W}
uses the detour paths coming from
\EuScript{W}
to correct the lack of flatness, in line with
[
GMN13a
,
GMN13
,
GMN14
]
, whereas
\mathbb{F}
uses pseudo-holomorphic strip counts. As one of the main themes of this article is the comparison between spectral networks and Floer theory, we shall explain how the former is an expression of the adiabatic limit of the latter.

In the above,
\mbox{Loc}_{k}(M)
denotes the dg-category of rank-
k
local systems of
\mathbb{C}
-modules on a smooth manifold
M
, and
\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}_{k}(M)
denotes the dg-category of rank-
k
twisted local systems of
\mathbb{C}
-modules on a smooth manifold
M
. We recall that a twisted local system on
M
is a local system on the unit cotangent bundle
T^{\infty}M
of
M
whose monodromy around the spherical cotangent fiber is given by
-\mbox{Id}
. The unit cotangent bundle will also be denoted
\mathring{M}
and its projection to the base by
\pi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathring{M}\longrightarrow M
. There are equivalent descriptions of local systems of
\mathbb{C}
-modules, e.g. as locally constant sheaves, as modules
\mbox{Fun}(\Pi(M),\mathbb{C}\mbox{-mod})
over the fundamental
\infty
-groupoid
\Pi(M)
of
M
, or as
\mathbb{C}
-modules over chains
C_{-*}(\Omega M;\mathbb{C})
on the base loop space; e.g. cf.
[
higher_algebra
]
. For an open surface
M=K(\pi_{1}(M),1)
and
\mathring{M}\cong M\times S^{1}
, so (twisted) local systems effectively translates to representations
\pi_{1}(M,m)\longrightarrow\mbox{GL}(V)
of the fundamental group of
M
, where
V
is a
\mathbb{C}
-vector space and
m\in M
a base point, which will be implicitly understood. Such representations are equivalent to modules over the group ring
\mathbb{C}[\pi_{1}(M)]
, and the functors
\mathbb{F}
and
\Phi{W}
will essentially be described as

\mathbb{F},\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{C}[\pi_{1}(\mathring{L})]\mbox{-mod}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\pi_{1}(\mathring{S})]\mbox{-mod}.

In fact, both
\mathbb{F},\Phi{W}
will be described locally (for short paths) and thus we shall be considering paths with varying endpoints. So our precise choice for describing
\mbox{Loc}_{k}(M)
will be that of the fundamental
\infty
-groupoid. That is, for this manuscript, a local system
\mathcal{L}\in\mbox{Loc}_{k}(M)
consists of the data of a stalk
\mathcal{L}_{m}
at every point
m\in M
, where
\mathcal{L}_{m}
is a
k
-dimensional
\mathbb{C}
-vector space, and an isomorphism
\mathcal{L}(\gamma)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{L}_{x}\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{y}
for any continuous path
\gamma\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,1]\longrightarrow M
,
\gamma(0)=x,\gamma(1)=y
, which only depends on the homotopy class of
\gamma
. The structure group of the associated bundles for these local systems will always be
\mbox{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, if we are in a given rank
n
.

Remark 5.1
.

(1) The branch cover
\pi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L\longrightarrow S
lifts to a branch cover
\mathring{\pi}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathring{L}\longrightarrow\mathring{S}
. In this case, there are identifications
\mathring{L}\cong L\times S^{1}
,
\mathring{S}\cong S\times S^{1}
, and the latter map reads
\mathring{\pi}=(\pi,\mbox{id})
.

(2) Intuitively, the functors
\mathbb{F},\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{C}[\pi_{1}(\mathring{L})]\mbox{-mod}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\pi_{1}(\mathring{S})]\mbox{-mod}
will be described by giving a group algebra morphism
\mathbb{C}[\pi_{1}(\mathring{S})]\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\pi_{1}(\mathring{L})]
. That is, they are described by explaining how to lift paths in
\mathring{S}
to paths in
\mathring{L}
. Rigorously, a path in
\mathring{S}
with two endpoints
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathring{S}
will lift to a collection of paths in
\mathring{L}
whose endpoints are allowed to be in a set of
2n
points (the pre-images of
z,z^{\prime}
). Such paths are not morphisms in the fundamental groupoid of
\mathring{L}
: they would roughly be morphisms between direct sums of objects, which only make sense once we consider
\mathbb{C}
-modules. This is a technical reason to describe the functors between the module categories.

(3) For an open surface
M
, there is a dg-equivalence
\mbox{Loc}_{k}(M)\simeq\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}_{k}(M)
once a spin structure is chosen; cf.
Section

A.3
or
[
CL22
, Appendix B.2]
. Since protected spin characters are not the scope of this article, this effectively implies that
\mathbb{F},\Phi{W}
can be understood as functors carrying a
\mbox{GL}_{1}
-local system on the Betti Lagrangian
L\subset T^{*}S
to a rank
\mbox{GL}_{n}
-local system on the base
S
.
\Box

Due to
Remark

5.1
.(2), we adopt the following notational conventions. First,
\pi_{1}(\mathring{S})
will stand for the relative homotopy classes
\pi_{1}(\mathring{S},\mathfrak{t}_{S})
, where
\mathfrak{t}_{S}
is a collection of points in
S
, often understood implicitly by context. Second, consider the functor
f^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(L)\longrightarrow\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(S)
that is the pull-back of a group algebra morphism
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{C}[\pi_{1}(\mathring{S})]\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\pi_{1}(\mathring{L})]
and
V\in\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(L)
. Given a path
\gamma\subset S
with

f(\gamma)=\sum_{i=1}^{g}a_{i}\tau_{i},\quad\tau_{i}\in\pi_{1}(\mathring{L},\mathfrak{t}_{L}),a_{i}\in\mathbb{C}

a linear combination of relative homotopy classes in
\pi_{1}(\mathring{L},\mathfrak{t}_{L})
, with the points in
\mathfrak{t}_{L}
projecting to those in
\mathfrak{t}_{S}
, we denote

f^{*}(V)(\gamma)=\sum_{i=1}^{g}a_{i}V(\tau_{i})[\tau_{i}]

to indicate that the parallel transport
f^{*}(V)(\gamma)
of
f^{*}(V)
along
\gamma
is given by

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{g}a_{i}V(\tau_{i})

and zero otherwise, and that the map
f^{*}
in fact admitted the homotopy refinement
f
which (further) keeps track of the relative homotopy classes
[\tau_{i}]
, a linear combination of which gives the image of
\gamma
. This notation is used in the following subsections, c.f. e.g.
Definition

5.6
and
Corollary

5.22
.

Remark 5.2
.

Alternatively, we can add the dependence on base points
\mathfrak{t}_{S}
and
\mathfrak{t}_{L}
explicitly in the notation, as in
Section

3.5
. Such notation becomes rather excessive and we have leaned towards having such endpoints of relative homotopy classes implicitly understood. This is not a particular challenge: the sets are always given a point in
S
, or
\mathring{S}
, where we will consider the cotangent fiber, and its lifts in the Betti Lagrangian
L\subset T^{*}S
, or
\mathring{L}
.
\Box

5.1.
The functor
\Phi{W}
: non-abelianization of local systems

The goal of this section is to define the functor

\Phi{W}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}_{1}(L)\longrightarrow\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}_{n}(S),

using a finite spectral network
\EuScript{W}
compatible with a Betti Lagrangian
L\subset T^{*}S
. It formalizes the constructions in
[
GMN13a
,
GMN13
,
GMN14
]
, where such types of local systems were studied in the context of providing coordinates for the moduli of flat connections on Riemann surfaces, see
[
GMN13a
, Section 10.1]
. In particular, the images
\Phi{W}(V)
of (twisted) local systems
V\in\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}_{1}(L)
encode the formal generating functions of framed
2d-4d
states from
[
GMN13a
]
after evaluating the spin character at
y=1
. The local systems
\Phi{W}(V)
will be described by giving their stalks
\Phi{W}(V)_{z}
at
z\in\mathring{S}
and the associated parallel transport isomorphisms
\Phi{W}(V)(\rho)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Phi{W}(V)_{z}\longrightarrow\Phi{W}(V)_{z^{\prime}}
along certain paths
\rho\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,1]\longrightarrow\mathring{S}
with
\rho(0)=z,\rho(1)=z^{\prime}
.

Remark 5.3
.

For notational ease, we often denote by
z\in\mathring{S}
points in
\mathring{S}
, even if
z
also denoted points in
S
. Given a regularly parametrized path in
S
that starts at
\rho(0)=z\in S
, the unit cotangent lift via the velocity vector
\rho^{\prime}(0)
at
z
gives a unique point in
\mathring{S}
projecting to
z
. That is the type of point we also denote
z\in\mathring{S}
, as it is uniquely determined by and determines
z\in S
once the path is chosen.
\Box

The stalks
\Phi{W}(V)_{z}
at
z\in\mathring{S}
are simple to describe for points
z\in\mathring{S}
with
\pi(z)\not\in K_{L}
not a branch point. By definition, we choose

\Phi{W}(V)_{z}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=V_{z_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus V_{z_{n}},

where
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\in\mathring{L}
are the lifts to
\mathring{L}
of the
n
-preimages of
\pi(z)\in S
via
\pi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L\longrightarrow S
.
17
17

17

Since
V
is a twisted local system, all choices of lifts are isomorphic.
The requirement that
\Phi{W}(V)\in\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(S)
determines the stalks at all
z\in\mathring{S}
. If
V\in\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}_{1}(S)
,
V_{z_{i}}
is a rank-
1

\mathbb{C}
-module and
\Phi{W}(V)_{z}
is therefore a rank-
n

\mathbb{C}
-module.

The parallel transport isomorphisms
\Phi{W}(V)(\rho)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Phi{W}(V)_{z}\longrightarrow\Phi{W}(V)_{z^{\prime}}
are more interesting and depend on the spectral network
\EuScript{W}
. In order to describe them, we shall define parallel transport for an open dense set of paths
\rho
that interact generically with
\EuScript{W}
, as follows.

Definition 5.4

(
\EuScript{W}
-adapted paths)
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be a finite closed interval. An immersed path
\rho\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\to\mathring{S}
is:

(i)

\EuScript{W}
-adapted, if its projection
\pi\big(\rho)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\longrightarrow S
is immersed and transverse to
\EuScript{W}\subset S
.

(ii)

free, if the image of
\pi\big(\rho)
is embedded and disjoint from
\EuScript{W}
.

(iii)

short, if its the unit-velocity lift of an embedded path in
S
intersecting
\EuScript{W}
transversely and exactly once.
\Box

Any relative homotopy class with endpoints in
\EuScript{W}^{c}
is represented by a
\EuScript{W}
-adapted path, as in
Definition

5.4
. Given a
\EuScript{W}
-adapted path, we can trivialize the sheets of
L
along the path
\pi(\rho)
: we denote by
\rho^{i}
be the lifts of
\rho
to
\mathring{L}
,
i\in[1,n]
; they are given by lifting
\pi(\rho)
to
n
lifts in
L
and then considering the unit-velocity lifts to
\mathring{L}
.

Let
\rho\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]\to\mathring{S}
be a short
\EuScript{W}
-adapted path (with its projection) intersecting
\EuScript{W}
at a point
z=\rho(0)
in an
(ij)
-wall
\mathfrak{w}
. Let
\mathfrak{S}(z;\mathfrak{w})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{{\mathfrak{s}(z;\mathfrak{w})}\}
be the set of soliton classes at
z
: each
{\mathfrak{s}(z;\mathfrak{w})}
defines a regular homotopy class with relative endpoints
z^{j}
and
z^{i}
, oriented from the former to the latter.We denote by
\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(z)}
be the unit-velocity lift of
{\mathfrak{s}(z;\mathfrak{w})}
to
\mathring{L}
and set the signs
\sgn(\rho)(z)=1
if the frame
\left<\EuScript{W}^{\prime}(z),\rho^{\prime}(0)\right>
is positively oriented, and
-1
otherwise. We need the following deformations for short paths. If
\sgn(\rho)(z)=1
, then we deform
\pi(\rho)|_{[-\varepsilon,0]}
near
0
so that it becomes tangential to
\EuScript{W}
at
0
and directed against the orientation on
\EuScript{W}
: denote by
\rho_{-}
the lift to
\mathring{S}
of such resulting path. Similarly, let
\rho_{+}
be the lift to
\mathring{S}
of a deformation of
\pi(\rho)|_{[0,\varepsilon]}
near
0
so that it becomes tangential to
\EuScript{W}
at
0
, now in the same direction as that of
\EuScript{W}
. The following definition formalizes the
detour paths
in the physics literature, cf. e.g.
[
GLM15
, Section 3.2]
or
[
Lon18
]
.

Definition 5.5

(Detour paths)
.

Let
\rho\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]
be a short
\EuScript{W}
-adapted path with
\sgn(z)=+1
and
\rho_{\pm}
its two deformations. By definition, the regular homotopy class
[\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(\rho)}]
, with relative endpoints
\rho^{i}(-\varepsilon)
to
\rho^{j}(\varepsilon)
, is given by

[\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(\rho)}]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\big[\big(\rho_{+}\big)^{i}\circ\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(z)}\circ\big(\rho_{-})^{j}\big].

For
\sgn(z)=-1
, the regular homotopy class
[\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(\rho)}]
is defined to be
[\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(\rho)}]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\big[\overline{\rho}_{-}^{i}\circ\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(z)}\circ\overline{\rho}_{+}^{i}\big]
, where
\bar{\rho}
denotes the inverse path.
\Box

Intuitively, the representative
\big(\rho_{+}\big)^{i}\circ\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(z)}\circ\big(\rho_{-})^{j}
of
[\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(\rho)}]
in
Definition

5.5
is (the lift of) a path that starts as
\rho
, when it hits
z
it takes a detour going around the soliton
{\mathfrak{s}(z;\mathfrak{w})}
, and then goes back to
z
and continues the path
\rho
. These detour paths are the crucial correction so as to define
\Phi{W}
in a manner that
\Phi{W}(V)
is indeed a local system on
\mathring{S}
, and not merely a constructible sheaf. For instance, at a
D_{4}^{-}
-singularity, these detour paths correct the non-trivial monodromy around a branch point to be the identity. Specifically, the parallel transport isomorphisms for
\Phi{W}(V)
are defined as follows:

Definition 5.6

(Parallel transport for
\Phi{W}
)
.

Let
V\in\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}_{1}(L)
,
(\EuScript{W},\mu)
and indexed spectral network, and
\rho\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\longrightarrow\mathring{S}
a
\EuScript{W}
-adapted path. By definition, the parallel transport isomorphisms
\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}(V)(\rho)
are defined as follows:

(i)

If
\rho
is free, then

\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}(V)(\rho)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}V(\rho^{i})[\rho^{i}],

where the sum runs over the
n
lifts
\rho^{i}
of
\rho
to
\mathring{L}
,
i\in[1,n]
.

(ii)

If
\rho
is short, with its projection intersecting
\EuScript{W}
at
z
, then

\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}(V)(\rho)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}V(\rho^{i})[\rho^{i}]+\sum_{\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(z)}\in\mathfrak{S}(z;\mathfrak{w})}\mu(\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(z)})[\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(\rho)}]V\big(\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(\rho)}\big),

where the second sum runs over all the soliton classes
{\mathfrak{s}(z;\mathfrak{w})}
associated to
z\in\EuScript{W}
.

(iii)

If
\rho
is given as a composition
\rho=\rho_{1}\circ\rho_{2}\circ\ldots\circ\rho_{n}
, each
\rho_{i}
either short or free, then

\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}(V)(\rho)=\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}(V)(\rho_{1})\circ\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}(V)(\rho_{2})\circ\ldots\circ\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}(V)(\rho_{k}).

\Box

Definition

5.6
allows us to define parallel transport for all
\EuScript{W}
-adapted paths, by declaring parallel transport along a homotopy class to be independent of a representative. For instance, if a class is represented by a free path, then
Definition

5.6
.(i) applies. The contributions from the second summand of
Definition

5.6
.(ii) are said to come from
detour paths
, in line with
Definition

5.5
. It is possible to verify combinatorially by hand that
Definition

5.6
yields a well-defined local system, independent of the representative of a given (twisted) homotopy class of a path, see e.g.
[
GMN13a
, Section 5.6]
. Given the Floer-theoretic framework we develop, this property will be rather a consequence of interpreting
\EuScript{W}
in terms of pseudo-holomorphic strips.

Remark 5.7
.

A brief explanation of the homotopy invariance from a Floer-theoretic viewpoint is as follows. Given a
\EuScript{W}
-adapted path
\rho
, we can consider the exact Lagrangian isotopy
T^{\ast}_{\pi(\rho(t))}S
given by moving the cotangent fibres along the path
\rho
. After an appropriate choice of (a family of) almost complex structures, we consider continuation strips with boundary on
L
and
T^{\ast}_{\pi(\rho(t))}S
and, using the data of
\rho(t)
and the (twisted) local system
V
, we orient the moduli space of such strips. The cobordism type of such moduli space depends only on the twisted isotopy class of
\rho
, and thus invariance follows once one shows that the formal sum of the boundaries of such continuation strips coincides with
\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}(V)(\rho)
.
\Box

Note that
Definition

5.6
suffices to define parallel transport for paths with endpoints in the dense complement
\EuScript{W}^{c}
. This is in line with the current understand of wall-crossing phenomena and related constructions in the literature. In contrast to see, the Floer theoretic parallel transports are well-defined for paths with endpoints on
\EuScript{W}
.

Due to the presence of Reeb chords at infinity, given by the data
{\bf\Lambda}
of the Betti surface, we also have an
infinite
version of
\EuScript{W}
-adapted paths. By definition, an infinite path
\rho\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(-\infty,\infty)\to\mathring{S}
is said to be
\EuScript{W}
-adapted if
\rho
is
\EuScript{W}
-adapted over any interval
[-N,N]
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, with a uniformly finite number of intersection points with
\EuScript{W}
, and its projection
\pi(\rho(t)),t\to\pm\infty
asymptotes to radial Reeb chord rays near a subset of marked points. The discussion above all apply to these types of paths as well.

Given a Reeb chord
\mathfrak{r}
of a Legendrian link in
\bf{\Lambda}
at a marked point
m\in\boldsymbol{m}
. By
Proposition

2.19
, there exists a small sectorial neighborhood

S_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{r})\cong\{(e^{i\theta},r)\in S^{1}\times\mathbb{R}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\theta\in[\theta_{0}-\varepsilon,\theta_{0}+\varepsilon],r>R\},

with
R\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
large enough, such that the Reeb chord
\mathfrak{r}
corresponds to
\theta_{0}
and
r\to\infty
and
S_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{r})
contains only the edges of
\EuScript{W}
that are asymptotic to
\mathfrak{r}
. Let
