\rho(\mathfrak{r})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(-\infty,\infty)\longrightarrow S
be the infinite path given by smooth the boundary
\partial S_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{r})
, with
\rho(\infty)=\rho(-\infty)=m
, which we can assume intersects transversely
\EuScript{W}
. Such infinite paths
\rho(\mathfrak{r})
are said to be the Reeb paths of
\mathfrak{r}
. The following result establishes that the morphism of the functor
\Phi{W}
over a Reeb path, even with its homotopy refinement, is an invariant of the Hamiltonian isotopy class of
L\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
, up to compactly supported Hamiltonian isotopy.

Theorem 5.8

(Invariance of soliton classes for Reeb paths)
.

Let
(S,\boldsymbol{m},{\bf\Lambda})
be a Betti surface,
\mathfrak{r}
a Reeb chord of a component of
{\bf\Lambda}
near a marked point with Reeb path
\rho(\mathfrak{r})
, and
L\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
an exact Betti Lagrangian endowed with a compatible spectral network
\EuScript{W}\subset S
. Then
\Phi{W}(V)(\rho(\mathfrak{r}))
is an invariant of
L
, for any
V\in\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(L)
and up to compactly supported Hamiltonian isotopy.

Theorem

5.8
will be a consequence of the results we establish in
Section

5.2
and
Section

5.3
, c.f.
Theorem

5.24
.

5.2.
The functor
\mathbb{F}
: Family Floer and local systems

The goal of this section is to define a functor

\mathbb{F}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}_{1}(L)\longrightarrow\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}_{n}(S)

using Floer-theoretic methods, and independently of a choice of a spectral network in
S
. For that, we use a version of Family Floer cohomology. Family Floer cohomology was initially pioneered by K. Fukaya, see e.g.
[
Fuk02
]
, along with
[
Abo14
]
and references therein. In brief, adapting this framework to exact Betti Lagrangians allows us to define a type of Floer cochain complexes
CF^{\bullet}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L)
, recording Lagrangian intersections between
L\subset T^{*}S
and the cotangent fiber
T^{\ast}_{z}S\subset T^{*}S
, and continuation maps between these as
z\in S
varies. Such maps are defined by studying certain (cobordism type of) moduli spaces of pseudo-holomorphic strips. In particular, given a path between
z,z^{\prime}\in S
, it is possible to construct a parallel transport from
CF^{\bullet}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L)
to
CF^{\bullet}(T^{\ast}_{z^{\prime}}S,L)
, which is what ultimately yields the functor
\mathbb{F}
. This section presents the necessary details to construct Family Floer cohomology for Betti Lagrangians and use it to define
\mathbb{F}
. For the rest of the section, we will assume that
L
is an exact Betti Lagrangian.

5.2.1.
Framework for Floer cohomology

Let
L\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
be a Betti Lagrangian. The difference between Betti Lagrangian and other types of Lagrangians previously studied in the literature (e.g. closed cases, or partial wrappings in Liouville sectors) is the existence of horizontal ends for the former, c.f. the conical and
O(-1)
-ends in
Section

2
. These ends naturally appear when studying spectral curves, and thus we now develop the Floer-theoretic techniques for them. In this context, the type of Hamiltonian isotopies that we employ is described as follows:

Definition 5.9
.

A Hamiltonian
H\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}T^{\ast}S\to\mathbb{R}
is said to be
cylindrical
if it is of the form
H=h\cdot r
outside the radius-
R
disk bundle
D_{R}^{\ast}S
, for some sufficiently large
R\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, where
h\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathring{S}\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth function on the unit sphere bundle and
r\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}T^{*}S\to S
is the (radial) distance coordinate to the zero section. Such a Hamiltonian
H
is said to have compact
horizontal support
if the projection
\pi(\text{supp}(dH))
lies in a pre-compact subset of
S
. Families of Hamiltonians parametrized over a compact manifold are said to be
uniformly horizontally supported
if these Hamiltonians are themselves horizontally supported over a compact set and are uniformly cylindrical.
\Box

Let
Z
be the Liouville vector field on
T^{\ast}S
uniquely determined by
\iota_{Z}\omega_{st}=\lambda_{st}
. By definition, an
\omega_{st}
-compatible almost complex structure
J
is
cylindrical
if
L_{Z}J=J
for large enough
r
. As in
[
Ekh+13
]
, we deform the Sasaki almost complex structure to make it cylindrical via

(25)

\displaystyle J_{cyl}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\begin{bmatrix}0&\beta(r)^{-1}g^{-1}\\
-\beta(r)g&0\end{bmatrix}

where
\beta\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[1,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a smooth increasing positive function such that
\rho(r)\equiv 1
near
r=1
and
\rho\equiv r
near infinity. We onwards implicitly assume that the chosen (families of) cylindrical almost complex structure
J
agree with
J_{cyl}
outside the cotangent bundle over a pre-compact subset of
S
.

Remark 5.10
.

For simplicity, we provide the construction of
\mathbb{F}
for exact Betti Lagrangians. The key result obtained (e.g.
Theorem

2
) for the WKB case is similar: the presence of
O(-1)
-ends, rather than conical or cylindrical ends, requires modifying some of the arguments but the qualitative descriptions remains the same, as illustrated by several of the results proven above in both settings. However, in the current paper, we will not construct the full functor
\mathbb{F}
for the non-exact case, though we essentially compute it for infinitesimally short line segments.
\Box

Let
\mathcal{J}(T^{\ast}S)
denote the space of compatible almost complex structures on
(T^{\ast}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
. To introduce the precise Floer data needed to construct Floer cochain complexes, we use the following interplay between the certain Lagrangians
P\subset T^{*}S
and almost complex structures:

Definition 5.11
.

Let
J_{s}\in\mathcal{J}(T^{\ast}S)
be a 1-dimensional family of almost complex structures, with real parameter
s\in\mathbb{R}\subset\mathbb{C}
. By definition, a Lagrangian
P\subset T^{*}S
is said to be
uniformly geometrically bounded
with respect to
J_{s}
if it is geometrically bounded with respect to the family of metrics induced by
J_{s}
and
\omega_{\text{st}}
. A 1-dimensional family of Lagrangians
P_{s}
is said to be uniformly geometrically bounded if given the associated movie totally real submanifold
\{(P_{s},s)\}\subset(T^{\ast}S\times\mathbb{C},\omega_{\text{st}}\oplus\omega_{\text{st}})
, there exists a constant
C\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and a
J_{s}
-compatible symplectic form
\omega_{P_{s}}
such that
(P_{s},s)
is Lagrangian and the triple
(\omega_{P_{s}},J_{s},(P_{s},s))
is geometrically bounded.
\Box

A summary result of the needed outcome from the literature on Lagrangian Floer theory reads as follows:

Proposition 5.12

(Floer Complexes)
.

Let
P,K\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
be a transverse pair of spin Lagrangians, each endowed local systems
\textbf{V}_{P},\textbf{V}_{K}
of
\mathbb{C}
-modules and with chosen spin structures. The following holds:

(1)

Let
J_{P,K}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,1]\to\mathcal{J}(T^{\ast}S)
be a generic time-dependent family of cylindrical almost complex structures
18
18

18

This is sometimes known as the Floer data.
such that
P
and
K
are uniformly geometrically bounded. Then, there exists a cochain complex

(CF^{\ast}((P,\textbf{V}_{P}),(K,\textbf{V}_{K});J_{P,K}),\partial),

known as the Lagrangian Floer complex, generated by the intersection points in
P\cap K
and whose differential is obtained by counting the
0
-dimensional part of the moduli space of
J_{P,K}
-holomorphic strips bounded by
P
and
K
.

(2)

The chain-homotopy type of the complex in (1) is independent on the choice of
J_{P,K}
: in particular, a homotopy from
J_{P,K}
to
J^{\prime}_{P,K}
, for which
P
and
K
are uniformly geometrically bounded, induces a quasi-isomorphism

(CF^{\ast}((P,\textbf{V}_{P}),(K,\textbf{V}_{K});J_{P,K}),\partial)\longrightarrow(CF^{\ast}((P,\textbf{V}_{P}),(K,\textbf{V}_{K});J^{\prime}_{P,K}),\partial).

(3)

Let
K_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}K\times[0,1]\to T^{\ast}S
be a Lagrangian isotopy, with associated local systems
\textbf{V}_{K_{s}}
, such that either its movie is uniformly geometrically bounded, or the isotopy generating
K_{s}
is a composition of a compactly supported Hamiltonian isotopy with a global Hamiltonian isotopy fixing
P
. Then
\{K_{s}\}_{s}
induces a continuation quasi-isomorphism

c\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(CF^{\ast}((P,\textbf{V}_{P}),(K_{0},\textbf{V}_{K_{0}});J_{P,K}),\partial)\longrightarrow(CF^{\ast}((P,\textbf{V}_{P}),(K_{1},\textbf{V}_{K_{1}});J_{P,K}),\partial),

which is compatible with concatenation, and depends on
\{K_{s},\textbf{V}_{K_{s}}\}
only up to chain homotopy.

(4)

If
P,K
are both Maslov zero Lagrangians, then the differential of (1) vanishes and thus

(CF^{\ast}((P,\textbf{V}_{P}),(K,\textbf{V}_{K});J_{P,K}),\partial)\cong\displaystyle\bigoplus_{x\in P\pitchfork K}(\hom(\textbf{V}_{P}(x),\textbf{V}_{K}(x))).

is the
\mathbb{C}
-module generated by the intersection points, endowed with the zero differential.
\Box

Proof.

For
P,K
closed Lagrangians and
\textbf{V}_{P},\textbf{V}_{K}
, the Lagrangian Floer complex and its invariance were established in
[
Flo88
]
. A more contemporary treatment which includes the construction and invariance of such complexes for exact and monotone Lagrangians is
[
Oh15
, Chapter 16]
. The case were the local systems
\textbf{V}_{P},\textbf{V}_{K}
are non-trivial is addressed in
[
Kon95
]
. This addresses Parts (1) and (2) in these cases. The method of moving Lagrangians, for Part (3), was introduced in
[
Oh93
]
, with
[
Oh15
, Chapter 14.4]
providing the necessary energy estimates. For
P,K
(and their movies) not necessarily compact Lagrangians, but geometrically bounded, the statement follows by using the monotonicty inequalities introduced in
[
AL94
, Section 5]
, cf. also the arguments surrounding the proof of
[
GPS20
, Proposition 3.19]
. For Part (4), with
P,K
Maslov
0
, the vanishing of the differential is a consequence of the virtual dimension of the moduli space of strips between
P
and
K
: it necessarily has to be
-1
and so, for regular Floer data, it must be empty.
∎

The case of interest in this manuscript is
P=T^{\ast}_{z}S
a cotangent fiber transverse to
L
, and
K=L
a Betti Lagrangian. For
Proposition

5.12
.(4) to apply, we must ensure the hypothesis of Maslov
0
for
L
. It is verified as follows:

Lemma 5.13
.

A Betti Lagrangian
L\subset T^{*}S
is Maslov
0
.

Proof.

By definition, the metric
g
induces a complex structure on
S
, and
L
is holomorphic over some neighborhood of the
D_{4}^{-}
-singularities. Now, over any pre-compact subset, the smooth sheets of the rescaled
\varepsilon L\subset T^{*}S
uniformly
C^{\infty}
-converge to the zero section, outside of some fixed neighborhood of the
D_{4}^{-}
-singularities. Since the zero section is holomorphic and
L
is holomorphic near the branch points,
\varepsilon L
is arbitrarily close to being holomorphic over any precompact subset of
S
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Therefore the phase function of
\varepsilon L
is arbitrarily close to zero over any pre-compact subset of
S
and thus
L
is Maslov
0
(cf. also
[
Nho24
, Section 6.2.1]
).
∎

The computation of the Floer complexes in
Proposition

5.12
.(1) and the continuation maps in
Proposition

5.12
.(3) is typically challenging: for reasonably generic Lagrangians
P,K
, there is no explicit description of the continuation maps beyond their definition. This in part is caused by a difficulty in explicitly computing the (cobordism type of the moduli spaces of) pseudo-holomorphic strips involved in these maps. An important conceptual point is that in our setting we are able to use a spectral network
\EuScript{W}
adapted to a Betti Lagrangian
L\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
in order to succeed at explicitly computing such pseudo-holomorphic strips, at least in the adiabatic limit. For instance, the arguments in
Section

4
ensure that, adiabatically, there are no pseudo-holomorphic strips bounded by
\varepsilon L
and
T^{\ast}_{z}S
for
z\in\EuScript{W}^{c}
, cf.
Theorem

4.2
.(1), which is the key ingredient behind showing that the continuation strips for
(CF^{\ast}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L),\partial)
with
z\in\rho
moving along a
\EuScript{W}
-free path
\rho
are “trivial” (we will make this notion of “trivial strips” precise using
Lemma

5.28
and the discussion afterward).

Remark 5.14
.

(1) The dependence on spin structures in
Proposition

5.12
can be more compactly repackaged by passing to sphere bundles, after choosing the Floer data. This is related to the map
\Phi{W}
in
Section

5.1
being defined on homotopy classes of paths on the unit sphere bundle; see also
Remark

5.1
.(3).

(2) We often write
CF^{\ast}(P,K)
for the Lagrangian Floer complex, due to the invariance
Proposition

5.12
.(2), and implicitly understanding
\partial
and the local systems if the context allows for that. For instance, if
P=T^{\ast}_{z}S
is a cotangent fibre, the local system
\textbf{V}_{K}
will always be trivial.
\Box

5.2.2.
The stalks of
\mathbb{F}(V)

A naive application of
Proposition

5.12
leads to the following ansatz for constructing
\mathbb{F}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}_{1}(L)\longrightarrow\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}_{n}(S)
. Given a local system
V\in\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}_{1}(L)
of rank-1
\mathbb{C}
-modules, we define the stalk
\mathbb{F}(V)_{z}
of
\mathbb{F}(V)
at a point
z\in S
to be
\mathbb{C}
-module underlying
CF(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L;V)
. The following first considerations then appear:

(1)

\mathbb{F}(V)_{z}
is well-defined as a cochain complex. Thus considering just its underlying
\mathbb{C}
-module is not invariant unless the differential vanishes.
19
19

19

In which case the
\mathbb{C}
-module coincides with its cohomology and is invariant.

(2)

\mathbb{F}(V)_{z}
is a rank-
n

\mathbb{C}
-module if
L\subset T^{*}S
is a Betti Lagrangian only if
z\not\in K_{L}
is not a branch point.

(3)

The stalk
\mathbb{F}(V)_{z}
and the resulting local system
\mathbb{F}(V)
are independent of the adiabatic parameter
\varepsilon
, up to isomorphism. However,
\Phi{W}
and the comparison to spectral network certainly requires passing to the adiabatic limit
\varepsilon\to 0
.

The three consideration are intertwined, and they are all solved at once by considering
z\in\EuScript{W}^{c}
and introducing an adiabatic parameter
\varepsilon\to 0
. This is achieved by considering the stalk
\mathbb{F}(V)_{z}
to be
\mathbb{C}
-module underlying
CF(T^{\ast}_{z}S,\varepsilon L;V)
for
\varepsilon
small enough. This is well-defined and addresses the above thanks to the following fact, which allows us to choose the Floer datum to be
J_{cyl}
.

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
z\in\EuScript{W}^{c}
and
U_{z}\subset S
be the disk-free neighborhood. Then, there exists
\varepsilon(z)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
the moduli space of non-constant
J_{cyl}
-holomorphic strips between
\varepsilon L
and
T^{\ast}_{w}S
is empty for any
w\in U_{z}
.

Proof.

Choose
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
small enough such that
\varepsilon L
lies in the unit disk bundle. Then, the integrated maximum principle argument (see e.g.
[
Nho24
, Lemma 3.28]
) implies that there exists a bijective correspondence between
J_{cyl}
-holomorphic strips bounded by
\varepsilon L
and
T^{\ast}_{z}S
, and
J_{g}
-holomorphic strips bounded
\varepsilon L
and
T^{\ast}_{z}S
. Indeed, the integrated maximum principle guarantees that the images of the
J_{cyl}
and
J_{g}
-holomorphic strips lie within the unit disk bundle, and
J_{cyl}\equiv J_{g}
coincide inside this disk bundle. Then
Theorem

4.2
implies the statement.
∎

Remark 5.16
.

The dependence of such putative stalk
\mathbb{F}(V)_{z}
(more precisely, the Lagrangian
L
) on the adiabatic parameter
\varepsilon
can also effectively be removed, at the cost of introducing
\varepsilon
-dependence on the Floer datum instance. Indeed, it follows from
[
SW06
, Remark 1.3]
that the rescaling
v(s,t)=\varepsilon^{-1}\cdot u(s,t)
gives a bijective correspondence between
J_{g}
-holomorphic discs bounded by
\varepsilon L
and
T^{\ast}_{z}S
, and
J_{\varepsilon^{-1}g}
-holomorphic discs bounded by
L
and
T^{\ast}_{z}S
.
\Box

Let
V\in\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(L)
, then the above discussion, including
Proposition

5.15
and
Remark

5.16
, leads to the following definition for the stalks of
\mathbb{F}(V)\in\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(S)
:

Definition 5.17

(Stalks for
\mathbb{F}(V)
)
.

Let
V\in\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(L)
and
z\in\mathring{S}
be such that
\pi(z)\in\EuScript{W}^{c}
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. The stalk
\mathbb{F}(V)_{z}
is defined to be

(26)

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}(V)_{z}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=CF(T^{\ast}_{z}S,\varepsilon L)=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}V_{z^{i}.}

where
z^{i}\in\mathring{L}
are the lifts of
z
. That is, the stalk at
z
is the Lagrangian Floer complex defined by the rescaled Betti Lagrangian
\varepsilon L
and the cotangent fiber
T^{\ast}_{\pi(z)}S\subset T^{*}S
, where all the elements are concentrated in degree
0
and the differential is trivial.
\Box

Two comments on
Definition

5.17
:

(1)

We use of the sphere bundles
\mathring{S}
and
\mathring{L}
in order to have signs over
\mathbb{Z}
(and thus
\mathbb{C}
) well-defined. As said above, spin structures could be used instead when working with the Betti Lagrangian
L
and cotangent fibres in
T^{*}S
. An argument in favor of using sphere bundles is that one can refine this framework to quantum local systems, where the
q
-variable, keeping track of the protected spin character
y
in
[
GMN13
, Section 3.2]
(see also
[
GMN12
, Section 4.2]
), is now the value of the monodromy of the local system along the sphere fiber. This restricts to twisted local systems when
q\to-1
, which is our setup, but it is also valuable to develop the theory without specializing the
q
-variable and thus we setup the groundwork for such generalization to be rather natural.

(2)

The dependence on
\varepsilon
is emphasized as a reminder that computations take place in the adiabatic limit
\varepsilon\to 0
, where
\EuScript{W}
can be characterized Floer-theoretically. It is nevertheless not strictly necessary, which can be argued as follows. First,
L
is a uniformly bounded multi-graph and thus there exists a radius
R\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that the Betti Lagrangian
L\subset D^{\ast}_{R}S
lies within the disk bundle of
T^{*}S
of radius
R
. Second, consider a smooth family
\beta_{s}(r),s\in[\varepsilon,1]
, of deformation functions such that

(i)

Each
\beta_{s}(r)
is constant for
r\leq R
and are uniformly linear at infinity
r\to\infty
,

(ii)

\beta_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon
for
r\in[0,2R)
and
\beta_{\varepsilon}=r
for sufficiently large
r
,

(iii)

\beta_{1}=1
for
r\in[0,3R)
.

If
J_{cyl}^{s}=J_{g}^{\beta_{s}}
denotes the associated family of deformed Sasaki almost complex structures, then
J_{cyl}^{s}
is such that
J_{cyl}^{\varepsilon}=J_{\varepsilon^{-1}g}
for
r\leq R
and
J_{cyl}^{1}=J_{g}
for
r\leq R
. Also, the family is uniformly geometrically bounded and of generalized contact type, in the sense that
r^{2}\beta^{-1}_{s}
are all
J_{cyl}^{s}
-plurisubharmonic. Therefore, we can apply
Proposition

5.15
and
Remark

5.16
and identify
CF(T^{\ast}_{z}S,(\varepsilon L,\textbf{V}_{\varepsilon L}),J_{cyl})\cong CF(T^{\ast}_{z}S,(L,\textbf{V}_{L}),J_{cyl}^{\varepsilon})
, the latter being defined using
J_{cyl}^{\varepsilon}
. Note that interpolating
CF(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L;J_{cyl}^{s})
from
s=\varepsilon
to
s=1
can be done using standard techniques because there are no moving boundaries involved.

(3)

The stalks for
\mathbb{F}(V)_{z}
are defined for points in
z\in\EuScript{W}
. Similarly, the parallel transport in
Section

5.2.3
is also defined for paths with endpoints on
\EuScript{W}
. The choices of Floer data, which are done homotopy-coherently, allow for such well-definedness on the entirety of
S
.

The stalks for
\mathbb{F}(V)
given as in
Definition

5.17
, we now move to the parallel transport for the twisted local systems
\mathbb{F}(V)
. As with
Section

5.1
, the parallel transport isomorphisms contain the more subtle and interesting information, in this case that of continuation strips.

5.2.3.
The parallel transport of
\mathbb{F}(V)

Let
V\in\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}_{1}(L)
be a twisted
\mbox{GL}_{1}
-local system. In
Section

5.2.2
, we explained the choice of stalks for
\mathbb{F}(V)
, which consisted of
\mathbb{F}(V)_{z}=CF(T^{\ast}_{z}S,\varepsilon L)=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}V_{z^{i}}
for a point
z\in\mathring{S}
whose projection to
S
is not a branch point of the projection
L\longrightarrow S
. Given a path
\rho\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,1]\longrightarrow\mathring{S}
, with
z=\rho(0)
and
z^{\prime}=\rho(1)
, we now want to construct a parallel transport isomorphism
\mathbb{F}(V)(\rho)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{F}(V)_{z}\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}(V)_{z^{\prime}}
. This will uniquely determine the image twisted local system
\mathbb{F}(V)\in\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}_{n}(S)
. We will first construct a local system
\mathbb{F}_{\epsilon}(V)
for each adiabatic parameter
\epsilon>0
with the stalks as in
Definition

5.17
, for points contained outside some
\varepsilon
-dependent nested neighbourhood of
\EuScript{W}
, that converges to
\EuScript{W}^{c}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. These local systems will come with the property that for all
0<\varepsilon^{\prime}<\varepsilon
, there is a unique natural isomorphism
\mathbb{F}_{\epsilon}(V)
and
\mathbb{F}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(V)
, which stabilizes to the identity map over morphisms with end-points on
\EuScript{W}^{c}
. We then define
\mathbb{F}(V)
as the direct limit local system. Intuitively, the parallel transport for
\mathbb{F}_{\epsilon}(V)
is a generalization of the continuation maps in
Proposition

5.12
.(3). The continuations maps in
Proposition

5.12
.(3) cannot be applied in our case because the (moving) fibers
T^{\ast}_{z}S
are not geometrically bounded. We will consider instead deformed continuations strips, which introduce correction terms to the standard continuation maps that account for the failure of geometric boundedness of the moving fiber. Such parallel transport is constructed as follows.

Let
H_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}T^{*}S\times[0,1]\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a uniformly horizontally supported time-dependent Hamiltonian isotopy generating the fiber transport along the
inverse
path of
\bar{\alpha}
of
\alpha
,
X_{s}
its Hamiltonian vector field and
\psi_{s}
its flow, and consider the infinite strip
\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\mathbb{R}_{s}\times[0,1]_{t}\subset\mathbb{C}
. Choose a smooth, increasing function
l\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(-\infty,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that
l=0
near
-\infty
and
l=1
near
+\infty
. We call such functions elongation functions. Let
J_{0}(t)
and
J_{1}(t)
be a family of almost complex structures obtained by compactly supported perturbations of
J_{cyl}
, and let
J(s,t)
be a family of almost complex structures such that

(i)

J(s,t)=J_{0}(t)
for
s\ll 1
and
J(s,t)=J_{1}(t)
for
s\gg 1
,

(ii)

J(s,t)
is also given as a compactly supported perturbation of
J_{cyl}
.

Such family of almost complex structures is said to be
admissible
.

Definition 5.18
.

Let
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,1]\longrightarrow S
be a path,
L\subset T^{*}S
a Betti Lagrangian, and
J(s,t)
be an admissible family of almost complex structures. A map
u\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{Z}\to T^{\ast}S
is said to be a
deformed continuation strip
for
\alpha
and
L
if it solves the following partial differential equation:

(27)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{s}u+{J}(s,t)\partial_{t}u-l^{\prime}(s)X_{l(s)}(u)=0&\\
u(s,1)\subset L&\\
u(s,0)\subset T^{\ast}_{\bar{\alpha}(l(s))}S&\\
\displaystyle\lim_{s\to\infty}u(s,t)\in T^{\ast}_{\alpha(1)}S\cap L\\
\displaystyle\lim_{s\to-\infty}u(s,t)\in T^{\ast}_{\alpha(0)}S\cap L.\end{cases}

For paths
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[a,b]\to S
, the deformed continuation strip for
\alpha
will mean
Eq.

27
using the path
\alpha(bt+(1-t)a)
instead.

\Box

In
Eq.

27
, the condition
u(s,0)\subset T^{\ast}_{\bar{\alpha}(l(s)))}S
is a moving boundary condition and, in a sense, the term
l^{\prime}(s)X_{l(s)}(u)
accounts for that. Indeed, up to composing with the Hamiltonian flow
\psi
of
H_{s}
, the deformed continuation strips in
Definition

5.18
are essentially pseudo-holomorphic strips: taking the gauge transform
\tilde{u}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\psi^{-1}_{l(s)}u
and
\tilde{J}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\psi^{\ast}J(s,t)
, we recover the homogeneous pseudo-holomorphic curve equation
\partial_{\tilde{J}}\tilde{u}=0
with moving boundary condition on
L
(instead of the fibers). By the exponential estimate
[
Oh15
, Proposition 14.1.5]
, the solutions of (
27
) are asymptotic to the intersection points of
L
and
T^{\ast}_{\alpha(1)}S
, as
s\to-\infty
, and of of
L
and
T^{\ast}_{\alpha(0)}S
, for
s\to\infty
. By
[
Nho24
, Proposition 3.37]
, the solutions of the gauge transforms have bounded diameter, and this diameter can be chosen uniformly for a uniform family of Hamiltonian isotopies. Thus the moduli space of solutions of (
27
) admits a Gromov compactification. By the transversality arguments in
[
Sei08a
, Chapter 2]
, the solutions of (
27
) are transversely cut-out for a generic choice of
J
and the 0-dimensional part of the moduli space of solutions of (
27
) gives a finite set. Given a generic family
J(s,t)
of admissible almost complex structures, we denote by
M(\alpha,J(s,t))
the moduli space of solutions of
Eq.

27
. The cobordism type of
M(\alpha,J(s,t))
is invariant under deformations of
J(s,t)
,
l(s,t)
and
\alpha
. Observe that since we are in cohomological grading, the induced continuation map sends a generator in
L\pitchfork T^{\ast}_{\alpha(0)}S
at
s=+\infty
to
L\pitchfork T^{\ast}_{\alpha(1)}S
at
s=-\infty
. This explains why we are using
\bar{\alpha}
, and the inverse of the Hamiltonian isotopy instead.

The parallel transports for
\mathbb{F}
are defined by counting the solutions of (
27
): intuitively, it is the number of points in
M(\rho,J(s,t))
. Since only the cobordism type of
M(\rho,J(s,t))
is invariant, the count must be such that a canceling pair of points contributes zero to the count. Specifically, to count solutions of (
27
) we must invariantly orient
M(\rho,J(s,t))
.

For that, we first use the following fact, often used in Floer theory: given a bordered
20
20

20

For our purposes, it is sufficient to consider
C
being the disk, the strip
\mathcal{Z}
, or the half plane.
Riemann surface
C\subset\mathbb{C}
and a Lagrangian sub-bundle
E\longrightarrow\partial C
of a bundle
\mathbb{C}^{2}\longrightarrow C
, a boundary condition on the Lagrangian sub-bundle
E
induces the Cauchy-Riemann operator

\overline{\partial}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}C^{\infty}(C,\mathbb{C}^{2},E)\to C^{\infty}(C,\Omega_{C}^{0,1}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}),

where
C^{\infty}(C,\mathbb{C}^{2},E)
denotes the space of
C^{\infty}
-sections of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
whose restriction to the boundary lies in
E
.

Remark 5.19

(On orientations)
.

1. Let
U_{2}/O_{2}
be the Lagrangian Grassmannian of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
,
\rho\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}S^{1}\to U_{2}/O_{2}
a loop of index
0
, and
E_{\rho}
be the Lagrangian sub-bundle of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
over
S^{1}
given by
\rho
. If the index of the induced Cauchy-Riemann operator
\bar{\partial}
on
(D^{2},S^{1})
operator is
2
, then
\ker(\bar{\partial})=\rho(1)
and the homotopy class of the trivialization of
(\mathbb{C}^{2},E)\simeq(\mathbb{C}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{2})
determines the orientation on
\det\bar{\partial}
; cf. e.g.
[
Fuk+09a
, Chapter 6]
.

2. The homotopy class of the trivialization is detected by
\pi_{2}(U_{2}/O_{2})
: the second Whitney class defines a map
w_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\pi_{2}(U_{2}/O_{2})\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and we can identify
\pi_{2}(U_{2}/O_{2})\simeq\mathbb{Z}\langle H\rangle
. Under the natural homomorphism
\pi_{2}(U_{2}/O_{2})\to\pi_{1}(O_{2})
,
H
is the generator that gets mapped to the family of orthonormal frames on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, whose lift to the sphere bundle
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times S^{1}
winds around the the unit sphere fiber once. The orientation of
\bar{\partial}
depends only on the mod
2H
class; declaring the orientation induced by the trivial loop to be
+1
, the orientation induced by
H
is the opposite one
[
Fuk+09a
, Chapter 6.2]
.
\Box

Technically, the stalks as defined in
Definition

5.17
implicitly use an orientation of the determinant line bundle of the associated linearized Cauchy-Riemann operator, which would themselves be tensoring the stalks
V_{z^{i}}
in the Floer complex. Let us precise on the choice of these orientations, which thus trivialize these line bundles and legitimize our choice of stalk in
Definition

5.17
. Near each intersection point in
L\pitchfork T^{\ast}_{z}S
, we use a specific linearized Cauchy-Riemann operator of the constant half-disk at each
z^{i}\in L\pitchfork T^{\ast}_{z}S
, described as follows. The configuration tuple
(T^{\ast}S;L,T^{\ast}_{z}S,\omega_{st})
is locally isomorphic to

(\mathbb{C}^{2};\left<u_{1},u_{2}\right>,i\mathbb{R}_{(x,y)}^{2},\omega_{std})
, where
u_{1},u_{2}
is a unitary frame. Consider
U
the unitary matrix with columns
u_{1},u_{2}
and the path of unitary matrices

\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,1]_{t}\longrightarrow U_{2},\quad\phi(t)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=i\exp(-t\log(U)),

each of which acts on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
as an isometry by left multiplication. Consider the path of linear Lagrangians

L_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\phi_{t}(\left<u_{1},u_{2}\right>)\subset T^{\ast}\mathbb{C}^{2}

from
\langle u_{1},u_{2}\rangle
to
i\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The path
\phi_{t}
takes an orthonormal basis on
\left<u_{1},u_{2}\right>
to an orthonormal basis on
i\mathbb{R}^{2}
and the square phase of this as a path in the Lagrangian Grassmannian
U_{2}/O_{2}
is
e^{-2\pi t}
and so the grading of the path becomes
-2t,t\in[0,1].
The path
L_{t}
defines a linearized Cauchy-Riemann operator of the constant half-disk at each
z^{i}\in L\pitchfork T^{\ast}_{z}S
by the moving boundary condition on
L_{t}
.

We now discuss the specific trivialization to choose on the moving boundary part
T^{\ast}_{\alpha(s)}S
. Given a
\EuScript{W}
-adapted path
\rho
, the metric
g
on
S
defines a complex structure
I
and we can consider the frame
F=\left<\rho(a),I\rho(a)\right>
at
T_{\pi(\rho(a))}S
. Choose the frame on the tangent space of
T^{\ast}_{z}S\pitchfork L
to be the one given by the image of
\exp(\log(U))
on the dual frame
F^{\ast}=\left<\rho^{\ast}(a),I^{\ast}\rho^{\ast}(a)\right>
. The trivial spin cover over the frames
(\phi_{t}\circ\phi_{1}^{-1})(F)
gives an orientation on the linearized Cauchy Riemann operator, which is then identified with the trivial line bundle. This is our trivialization, and thus we conclude the discussion on orienting the moduli spaces
M(\rho,J(s,t))
of deformed continuation strips, solving
Eq.

27
.

We can now construct the continuation maps without the assumption that the Lagrangian
K
in
Proposition

5.12
.(3) is geometrically bounded, which is needed in this framework of spectral networks as we must apply it to
P=T^{\ast}_{z}S
a cotangent fiber. The result reads as follows:

Proposition 5.20

(Continuation of
CF^{\ast}
under moving fiber)
.

Let
(S,\boldsymbol{m},{\bf\Lambda})
be a Betti surface,
L\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
a Betti Lagrangian,
\EuScript{W}
a compatible spectral network and
V\in\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(L)
. Then:

(1)

(Well-defined) Given
\rho\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,1]\longrightarrow\mathring{S}
be a
\EuScript{W}
-adapted path from
z_{0}=\rho(0)
and
z_{1}=\rho(1)
, the signed count of points in the (oriented cobordism type of the) moduli spaces
M(\rho,J(s,t))
of solutions to
Eq.

27
determines a continuation map

(28)

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}_{\varepsilon}(V)(\rho)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}CF^{\ast}(T^{\ast}_{\pi(z_{0})}S,(\varepsilon\Sigma,V))\to CF^{\ast}(T^{\ast}_{\pi(z_{1})}S,(\varepsilon\Sigma,V))

which is independent of the representative of the smooth homotopy class
\rho
.

(2)

(Twisted equivariance) For any
\rho\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,1]\longrightarrow\mathring{S}
be a
\EuScript{W}
-adapted path, the continuation map in
(1)
satisfies

\mathbb{F}_{\varepsilon}(V)(H{\rho})=-\mathbb{F}_{\varepsilon}(V)(\rho),

where
H
is the homotopy class of the cotangent circle.

(3)

(Concatenation morphism) Given two
\EuScript{W}-
adapted path
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
, the continuation map in
(1)
satisfies

\mathbb{F}_{\varepsilon}(V)(\rho_{1}\circ\rho_{2})=\mathbb{F}_{\varepsilon}(V)(\rho_{1})\circ\mathbb{F}_{\varepsilon}(V)(\rho_{2}).

Proof.

(of
Proposition

5.20
)
It suffices to orient the moduli
M(\rho,J(s,t))
using the twisted local system
V\in\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(L)
such that it becomes cobordism invariant with respect to
\rho
. Indeed, once that is established, the cobordism argument in Floer theory, cf. e.g.
[
Sei08a
]
, concludes invariance (1) and the concatenation property (3).
To orient, consider a deformed continuation strip
u
traveling between two lifts
z^{i}_{0}
to
z^{j}_{1}
in
\mathring{L}
, i.e. with positive punctures on these two points, the lower boundary mapped to
L
and the upper boundary to the moving fibers along
\rho
. Choose a sphere bundle lift of the path
u(s,1)
,
s\in(-\infty,\infty)
, so that it agrees with
\phi_{1}^{-1}(\rho(a))
at
z^{i}_{a}
, for all
a=0
and
a=1
. The induced metric on
S
gives a framing of
S
along
u(s,1)
. Considering the two points
z^{i}_{0},z^{j}_{1}\in\mathring{L}
as constant half-disks, we can glue them to the ends of the strip
u
and obtain the glued disk
z^{i}_{0}\sharp u\sharp z^{j}_{1}
. As discussed above, this data defines a linearized Cauchy Riemann operator over the disk
z^{i}_{0}\sharp u\sharp z^{j}_{1}
which has index
2
. Its orientation depends on the choice of the trivialization of the pull-back of the tangent bundles of the Lagrangian spaces: our choice for orientation is to concatenate the sphere bundle lift of
u(s,1)
traveling through
L
, the paths
(\phi_{t}\circ\phi_{1}^{-1})(F)
and then (the dual frame given by) the sphere bundle path
\rho
traveling through the moving fibers. This choice is independent of the sphere bundle lift of
u(s,1)
and, by construction, twisting the path
\rho
by
H
reverses the sign of the orientation, which established (2).∎

We now construct the local systems
\mathbb{F}_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathbb{F}
. Firstly, make a choice of the regular Floer datum
21
21

21

This datum must also contain a time-dependent Hamiltonian term in the case that
z
is a branch point, c.f.
[
Sei08a
, Section 8(e)]
.
for each pair
(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L)
,
z\in S
, so that we get the stalk given by the (abstract) Floer cohomology groups
HF(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L)
. Furthermore, the parallel transport is well-defined once the Floer datum has been chosen at each intersection point, since the moduli space of solutions of
Definition

5.18
is well-defined, up to cobordism. Thus, regardless of the choice of the regular Floer datum, the family Floer twisted local system, regarded as a functor
\Pi_{1}(\mathring{S})\to\text{Vect}_{n}
, is well-defined, up to unique natural isomorphism.

The dependence on the adiabatic parameter
\varepsilon
is incorporated as follows. By
Theorem

4.2
, for any precompact subset
U\subset S-\EuScript{W}
there exists
\varepsilon_{U}>0
such that for all
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{U}
, the Floer datum
J_{cyl}^{\varepsilon}
for
(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L,J_{cyl}),z\in\overline{U}
, is regular. Choosing
J_{cyl}^{\varepsilon}
to be the regular Floer datum for
(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L)
for
z
outside
\overline{U}
, we obtain a local system, which we consider of as a functor
\mathbb{F}_{\varepsilon}(V)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\Pi_{1}(\mathring{S})\to Vect_{k}
. Furthermore, by our description of
J_{cyl}^{\varepsilon}
, given any
U\subset U^{\prime}
, and scaling stability (
Lemma

5.31
) the induced natural isomorphism between
\mathbb{F}_{\varepsilon}(V)
and
\mathbb{F}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(V)
acts as the identity on morphisms that represent paths that are contained fully in
U
, for
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\min(\varepsilon_{U^{\prime}},\varepsilon)
.

Definition 5.21
.

The family Floer cohomology local system
\mathbb{F}
is the direct limit local system
\varinjlim\mathbb{F}_{\varepsilon}
.
\Box

The discussion above implies that
\mathbb{F}
is well-defined up to unique isomorphism. By scaling stability, whenever we compute parallel transport maps of
\mathbb{F}
, we may compute it for
\varepsilon
small enough. The results of the current section then can be regarded as showing that the local system
\mathbb{F}
coincides with
\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}(V)
, over objects that lie outside
\EuScript{W}^{c}
.

We conclude this section with a discussion on how the continuation maps in
Proposition

5.20
can be enhanced to record (twisted) homotopy classes, while remaining invariant. Specifically, let
M(\rho;J(s,t),V)
denote the moduli space
M(\rho,J(s,t))
equipped with the orientation (from
V
) as in the proof of
Proposition

5.20
. For each deformed continuation strip
u\in M(\rho;J(s,t),V)
, we denote by
\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{u}
the sphere bundle lift of
u(s,0)
and by
\sigma(\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{u},\rho)
the sign of
{\det(D\bar{\partial}_{J}u)}_{\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{u},\rho}
, the oriented determinant line of the linearized Cauchy-Riemann operator obtained using
\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{u}
and
\rho
.

Corollary 5.22

(Homotopy refinement)
.

In the notation above, the homotopy refinement of the deformed continuation map in
Proposition

5.20
is

(29)

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}(V)(\rho)=\sum_{u\in M(\rho;J(s,t),V)}\sigma(\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{u},\rho)V(\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{u})[u(-s,1)],

and it only depends on the homotopy class of
\rho
, and is compatible with concatenation.

An advantage of
Eq.

29
over
Eq.

28
is that the former is invariant under the
H
-action, as an
H
-twist in the sphere bundle lift
\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{u}
is canceled out by the parallel transport
V(\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{u})
. We shall momentarily show in
Section

5.3
that the parallel transport
\mathbb{F}(V)
as in
Eq.

29
yields, in the adiabatic limit, the homotopy refinement of
\Phi{W}(V)
.

5.2.4.
Ancillary discussion on spin structures

The (twisted) Floer complexes constructed above used the sphere bundles
\mathring{S},\mathring{L}
instead of working directly with
S,L
, each endowed with spin structures. Two reasons are that, with this groundwork, it is reasonably direct to improve the above discussion to include the protected spin characters
y
-variable in
[
GLM15
]
and, more technically, it allows us to package the discussion on the dependence on spin structures directly in the sphere bundle classes. The former is achieved by keeping track of the homotopy class on the sphere bundle, without forcing the twisted evaluation
y\to-1
. Indeed, it is possible to show that the solutions of
Eq.

27
are immersed and thus have canonical lifts to the unit sphere bundles, where their (relative) homotopy classes can be recorded.

To compare the Floer complexes constructed above using the sphere bundles, cf.
Proposition

5.20
, with the Floer complexes one would define using
S,L
and their spin structures, we proceed as follows. First, suppose that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}},\mathfrak{s},\beta
are all trivial near
L\pitchfork T^{\ast}_{z}S
, and that the frame bundle of
L
has its torsor at
\overline{z}\in T^{\ast}_{z}S\pitchfork L
so that it is equal to the one given by the
\phi_{t}^{-1}
image of some chosen frame
\left<e_{1},e_{2}\right>
on
i\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Taking the trivial spin cover over the framing
(\phi_{t}\circ\phi^{-1}_{1})\left<e_{1},e_{2}\right>
gives a path of spin structures on
L_{t}
and the induced orientation is the trivial one. Then consider the sphere bundle lift of the path
\alpha
and the sphere bundle lift of the path
L_{t}
, as prescribed by
\mathring{S}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}
. The orientation on the induced linearized Cauchy-Riemann operator then determines the sign and it coincides with that of the holonomy of
\mathbb{F}(V)
along
u(s,0)
. As a consequence of
Theorem

5.24
and this discussion, we can summarize the outcome as follows:

Corollary 5.23
.

Let
(S,\mathfrak{s})
and
(L,\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}})
be endowed with spin structures, and consider
\textbf{V}\in\mbox{Loc}(L)
and
V\in\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(L)
such that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}^{\ast}V=\textbf{V}
. Then the family Floer local system obtained using
V
is the
\mathfrak{s}^{\ast}
-pullback of
\mathbb{F}(V)
.
\Box

5.3.
Equivalence between
\mathbb{F}
and
\Phi{W}

The aim of this section is to prove the following result, comparing the functors
\mathbb{F}
and
\Phi{W}
, i.e. the twisted local systems obtained from Family Floer and those obtained via the combinatorics of spectral networks:

Theorem 5.24

(
\mathbb{F}
and
\Phi{W}
are equivalent)
.

Let
(S,\boldsymbol{m},{\bf\Lambda})
be a Betti surface,
L\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
an exact Betti Lagrangian,
(S,g)
a compatible metric and
\EuScript{W}
an adapted Morse spectral network. Then the two functors

\mathbb{F},\Phi{W}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}_{1}(L)\longrightarrow\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}_{n}(S)

are equivalent. In particular, the combinatorial corrections from detour paths on
\EuScript{W}
coincide with the Floer-theoretic corrections from deformed continuations strips.

To compare these two functors it suffices to focus on the objects, as morphisms naturally commute with the construction. The statement of
Theorem

5.24
also holds for the enhancements of those functors that keep track of specific (relative) homotopy classes in the unit sphere bundles.
Theorem

5.24
is proven by analyzing the behavior of
\mathbb{F}
in the adiabatic limit
\varepsilon\to 0
. Namely, understanding the deformed continuation strips from
Eq.

27
with one boundary condition on
\varepsilon L
and the other (moving) boundary condition on cotangent fibers as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Since
\EuScript{W}
controls the adiabatic behavior of pseudo-holomorphic strips, there are two regions to consider:

(1)

In the complement
\EuScript{W}^{c}
of the spectral network, the parallel transport of
\Phi{W}
is trivial. Therefore, we need to show that the deformed continuation strips defining the parallel transport for
\mathbb{F}
are also trivial if the path lies in
\EuScript{W}^{c}
.

(2)

For
\EuScript{W}
-adapted short paths, we must show that the adiabatic limit of deformed continuation strips across a wall of
\EuScript{W}
is exactly given by the trivial strips
and
strips whose flowtree limit coincides with the detour paths introduced in
Section

5.1
.

In order to prove these facts above, we start with a quantitative refinement of
Eq.

27
, estimating the behaviors of deformed pseudo-holomorphic strips in terms of their length. Specifically, consider
z\in S
such that
T^{\ast}_{z}S
is transverse to the Betti Lagrangian
L
and fix a unit tangent vector
v\in\mathring{S}
base at
z
. Employing the Riemannian metric
(S,g)
, consider the path
\alpha_{v}(s)=\exp_{z}(sv)
starting at
z
in the direction of
v
. For
s
sufficiently small,
\alpha_{v}
is an embedding and
T^{\ast}_{\alpha_{v}(s)}S
remains transverse to
L
. This latter condition implies in particular that there are no
D_{4}^{-}
-singularities in a small enough neighborhood of the image of
\alpha_{v}
. We model such a path by considering local coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2})
near
z=x_{1}+ix_{2}
so that
\alpha_{v}(s)=(s,0)
, and then the (local) Hamiltonian
H^{v}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{2}_{x_{1},x_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
H^{v}(x_{1},x_{2};p_{1},p_{2})=-p_{1}
generates the transport of
T^{\ast}_{z}S
along
\bar{\alpha}_{v}(s)
. To achieve compactly supported behavior, localizing this fiber transport along
\bar{\alpha}_{v}(s)
, let us cut-off
H^{v}
to a Hamiltonian
b(x_{1},x_{2})H^{v}
with
b(x_{1},x_{2})
a bump function concentrated at
\alpha_{v}(s)
, i.e.
b(x_{1},x_{2})
is constant equal to
1
in a neighborhood of
\alpha_{v}(s)
and vanishes outside a slightly larger neighborhood. To simplify, we still denote by
H^{v}
the cut-off Hamiltonian, denote by
X_{v}
its Hamiltonian vector field and by
\psi_{v}
its flow. The system
Eq.

27
defining deformed continuation strips can now be specialized to paths
\alpha_{v}(s)
, where dependence on the unit tangent vector
v
is acquired and
H^{v}
is fixed.

Definition 5.25
.

Let
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
J(s,t)
be an
\varepsilon
-admissible family of almost complex structures on
\mathcal{Z}
, and consider an elongation function
l(s)
and a length
d\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. By definition, a map
u_{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{Z}\longrightarrow T^{\ast}S
is said to be a continuation strip along
\alpha_{v}(s)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,d]_{s}\to{\bf S}
if it solves the system

(30)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{s}u_{d}+{J}(s,t)\partial_{t}u_{d}-d\cdot l^{\prime}(s)X_{v}(u_{d})=0&\\
u_{d}(s,1)\subset L&\\
u_{d}(s,0)\subset T^{\ast}_{\bar{\alpha}_{v}(d\cdot l(s))}S&\\
\displaystyle\lim_{s\to-\infty}u_{d}(s,t)\in T^{\ast}_{\alpha_{v}(d)}S\cap L\\
\displaystyle\lim_{s\to+\infty}u_{d}(s,t)\in T^{\ast}_{z}S\cap L.\end{cases}

We often denote
u(s,t)=u_{d}(s,t)
, understanding the dependence on
d
implicitly.
\Box

To ease notation, given a value for the adiabatic parameter
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we assume that we have fixed an
s
-dependent deformation function
\rho_{s}(r)
, as in
Section

5.2.1
, and an
\varepsilon
-admissible family of almost complex structures refers to an admissible family of almost complex structures obtained by deforming
J_{cyl}^{\rho_{\varepsilon}}
, instead of
J_{cyl}
. Our first task is choosing the elongation function
l(s)
such that solutions to
Eq.

30
of small enough length have controlled behavior. This is the content of the following:

Proposition 5.26
.

In the notation for
Eq.

30
, there exists a choice of an elongation function
l(s)
and a constant
d_{v}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that the following holds:

(1)

The continuation strips
u_{d}(s,t)
along
\alpha_{v}(s)
of length
d\in(0,d_{v})
whose limiting endpoints lie in the same smooth sheet of
L
cannot leave the
d_{v}
-neighborhood of their endpoints.

(2)

For the continuation strips
u_{d}(s,t)
along
\alpha_{v}(s)
of length
d\in(0,d_{v})
whose limiting endpoints lie in different smooth sheets of
L
, there exists a sequence of lengths
d_{n}\to 0
such that the associated family
u_{d_{n}}
of strips Gromov converges to a broken
J(s,t)
-pseudo-holomorphic strip bounded between
L
and
T^{\ast}_{z}S
.

Proof.

For Part (1), let
u=u_{d}(s,t)
be a solution of
Eq.

30
and consider its gauge transform
\tilde{u}=\psi^{-1}_{l(s)}u
. Then the symplectic area of
\tilde{u}
is

(31)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\tilde{u}^{\ast}\omega=-\int_{u(s,1)}\lambda_{st}-d\cdot\int H^{v}(u(s,1))ds=W((\psi^{-1}_{1}\circ u)(-\infty,0))-W(u(+\infty,0))-d\cdot\int H^{v}(u(s,1))ds,

where
dW=\lambda_{\text{st}}|_{L}
is a primitive of the Liouville form along
L
. Along
\alpha_{v}(s)
, let
h>0
be such that over some small neighbourhood of
\alpha_{v}(s)
, the smooth sheets of
\epsilon L
have distance at least
h>0
apart, with respect to the Sasaki metric. For each
z\in\alpha_{v}(s)
, let
\cup B_{h/2}(z^{i})\subset T^{\ast}S
denote the small neighbourhood of the intersection points between
T^{\ast}_{z}S
and
L
. Let
\ell\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
be a constant so that
J_{cyl}
-holomorphic half-disks with energy less than
\ell/2
intersecting
\cup_{i}B_{h/4}(z^{i})
cannot leave the larger neighbourhood
\cup_{i}B_{h/2}(z^{i})
, for all
z\in\alpha_{v}(s)
.

Choose an elongation function
l(s)
so that
l^{\prime}(s)
is supported on
[0,\ell]
. By the hypothesis that the endpoints lie on the same sheet, the limiting endpoints of the gauged transformed strips have to be the unique intersection point of
T^{\ast}_{z}S
and
L
lying on the same sheet as the endpoints of
u_{d}
. By
Eq.

31
, the symplectic area (a.k.a. energy) of the gauge-transformed strip is of the form
O(d)
for for small enough. Therefore, the graph of
\psi_{d\cdot l(s)}^{-1}(u(s,t))
on the moving part has energy
O(d)+l
.

In addition, for
d
small enough, the symplectic 2-form making the movie
(\psi_{d\cdot l(s)}^{-1}(L),s)
Lagrangian can be made arbitrarily close to
\omega_{st}\oplus\omega_{st}
and the metric induced by
\psi_{d\cdot l(s)}^{\ast}J(s,t)
can be made arbitrarily close to the correspondingly standard metric. Thus,
[
Nho24
, Proposition 3.37]
implies that the graph of
\psi_{l(s)}^{-1}(u(s,t))
on the moving part cannot leave the
(3\ell)/2
-neighborhood of the intersection points between
L
and
T^{\ast}_{z}S
for small enough
d
, which concludes Part (1). For Part (2), it suffices to verify that the symplectic area of the family of solutions (
30
) admits a uniform positive lower bound. This follows from the fact that
u_{d}(s,t)
travels between distinct sheets of
L
(see
[
Nho24
, Eq.6.1.10]
). Therefore, the Gromov compactness argument developed in
[
Nho24
, Section 6.1.5]
applies and
u_{d}(s,t)
Gromov converges to a broken
J(s,t)
-pseudo-holomorphic strips bounded between
L
and
T^{\ast}_{z}S
for a subsequence of lengths
d_{n}\to 0
.
∎

By definition, a continuation strip
u
is said to be
small
if it is as in
Proposition

5.26
.(1).

Remark 5.27
.

Note that there is a difference between
Proposition

5.26
and
[
Nho24
, Proposition 6.11]
: in the result above, the Hamiltonian isotopy does not necessarily fix the neighborhood of the intersection points. In order to correct this matter, we need to be very careful with the choice of the elongation function
l
.
\Box

After a small perturbation of
J(s,t)
, we can and do assume that the moduli space of same sheet continuation strips consist of small continuation strips, and that it is transversely cut-out.

Lemma 5.28
.

In the notation above, for small enough
d\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the moduli space of small continuation strips of length
d
is cobordant to a singleton moduli space, consisting of a single strip whose boundary on
L
may be identified with a lift of the arc
\alpha_{v}(s),s\in(0,d_{v})
.

Proof.

Near
z
, a smooth sheet of
L
can be described a graph of a smooth function
f
. As observed in
[
Flo89a
]
, we may regard
f
as a (locally-defined) Hamiltonian on
T^{\ast}S
and write
\varepsilon\cdot\text{gr}(f)
as the image of the zero section under the time
\varepsilon
-flow
\Psi
of
f
. Taking the inverse of the fibre-preserving
global
Hamiltonian isotopy
\Psi^{-1}_{\varepsilon}
, the configuration
(\varepsilon L,T^{\ast}_{z}S,J_{cyl})
transforms into
(Z,T^{\ast}_{z}S,\Psi_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(J_{cyl}))
. Under this transformation, the perturbation term in
Eq.

27
turns into
(d\Psi^{-1}_{\varepsilon})_{\ast}(X_{v}(u_{d}))
. Since we are only interested in small continuation strips, whose diameter we can a priori control, using the monotonicity argument in the proof of
[
Nho24
, Proposition 6.11]
, we are free to interpolate the perturbation term from
(d\Psi^{-1}_{\varepsilon})_{\ast}X_{v}
to
l^{\prime}(s)X_{v}
. We claim that the solutions of the new equation are all constant, up to gauge-transformation. This leads to a cobordism of the moduli space of small continuation strips, whose other end consists of a single strip.

To verify the claim, consider the solutions of
Eq.

27
with
J(s,t)
replaced by
\psi^{\ast}_{\varepsilon}J(s,t)
and the boundary condition
L
replaced by the zero section. Since the Hamiltonian fixes the zero section, we are back to the situation of
[
Nho24
, Proposition 6.11]
where the Hamiltonian fixes the Lagrangians near the intersection points. Therefore, after taking the gauge-transform
\Psi_{s}^{-1}u(s,t)
, the strip becomes
constant
. As claimed, we may identify its boundary on
L
with a lift of the arc
\alpha_{v}(s),s\in(0,d_{v})
to the smooth sheet
\text{gr}(f)
.
∎

Strips as in
Lemma

5.28
are said to be
trivial strips
.

We introduce the following notions, analogous to
Definition

4.1
:

Definition 5.29
.

Let
L\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
be a Betti Lagrangian and
\EuScript{W}
an adapted finite creative Morse spectral network.

(i)

A point
z\in S
is said to be
continuation-free
in the adiabatic limit if there exists
\varepsilon(z)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and a neighbourhood
U_{z}
such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon(z))
,
(T^{\ast}_{w}S,L)
is
J_{cyl}^{\varepsilon}
disk-free for
w\in U_{z}
, and there exists a length
d(z,\varepsilon)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and an elongation function
l(s,\epsilon)
such that for all points
w\in U_{z}
there are
no

J_{cyl}^{\varepsilon}
-holomorphic continuation strips that are not small along arcs of the form
\alpha_{v}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,d]_{s}\to S
, for
d\in(0,d(z,\varepsilon))
,
w\subset U_{z}
and
v\in T_{w}S
a unit tangent vector at
w

(ii)

A point
z\in\EuScript{W}
different from a vertex is said to have
Stokes transport in the adiabatic limit
if there exists
\varepsilon(z)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and a length
d(z)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon(z))
, there exists an elongation function
l(s,\varepsilon)
for which there are
no
large
J_{cyl}^{\varepsilon}
-holomorphic strips for
L
along the arc
\alpha_{v}(s)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[-d(z),d(z)]_{s}\to S
, that do not belong to the path detour class along
\alpha_{v}(s)
, where
v
denotes the outward normal vector to
\EuScript{W}
at
z
.

Note that vertices of
\EuScript{W}
do not need to be considered in
Definition

5.29
because the behavior near them has been fixed by the local models. We must now establish the analogue to
Theorem

4.2
for continuation strips, which reads as follows:

Proposition 5.30
.

Let
L\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
be a Betti Lagrangian and
\EuScript{W}
an adapted finite creative Morse spectral network. Then the following holds:

(i)

If
z\in\EuScript{W}^{c}
, then
z
is continuation-free in the adiabatic limit.

(ii)

If
z\in\EuScript{W}
and not a vertex, then
z
has Stokes transport in the adiabatic limit.

Proof.

For Part (1), fix a precompact open subset of
\EuScript{W}^{c}
whose closure does not intersect
\EuScript{W}
. We argue by contradiction: suppose that there is a sequence of points
z_{n}
, unit tangent vectors
v_{n}
of
z_{n}
, with lengths
d(z_{n})
converging to zero but such that there are
J_{cyl}^{\varepsilon}
-holomorphic continuation strips which are not small; for
L
and along arcs
\alpha_{v_{n}}(z_{n})
. By
Proposition

5.26
such a sequence has a subsequence that converges to a broken
J_{cyl}
-holomorphic strip. By the integrated maximum principle and the equality
J_{cyl}^{\varepsilon}=J_{\varepsilon^{-1}g}
along the disk bundle containing
L
,
J_{cyl}^{\varepsilon}
-holomorphic strips have to necessarily be
J_{\varepsilon^{-1}g}
-holomorphic strips. Then
Remark

5.16
and
Theorem

4.2
together imply that such broken
J_{\varepsilon^{-1}g}
-holomorphic strips cannot exist, thus a contradiction. This establishes Part (1).

For part (2), let
U_{z}
be the Stokes neighbourhood of
z
. By
Theorem

4.2
, we see that there exists some
\varepsilon(z)>0
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon(z))
, the only disks in
(T^{\ast}_{w}S,\varepsilon L)
must be parallel transports of the soliton classes. We claim that the maximal arc
\alpha_{v}(s)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[-d_{z},d_{z}]_{s}\to S
contained in such a neighbourhood is the desired one. To see this, shrink
\varepsilon(z)
so that
(T^{\ast}_{\alpha_{v}(0)}S,L)
and
(T^{\ast}_{\alpha_{v}(1)}S,L)
are all
J_{cyl}^{\varepsilon}
disk-free, for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon(z))
. Then we can combine
Proposition

5.26
and the previous argument to conclude that there exists an elongation function
l(s)
such that for each
t\in[-d_{v}(s),d_{v}(s)]
, there exists
d_{t}>0
such that along
\alpha_{v}(t+s)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,d]_{s}\to S
, for
d\in(0,d_{t})
, the large
J_{cyl}^{\varepsilon}
-continuation strips must have the homotopy class of the detour path across
z
(deformed to have the end-points of
\alpha_{v}(t+s)
, in a straightforward way). Indeed, this follows from
Theorem

4.2
because the path homotopy class of its boundary must converge, as
d\to 0
, to broken chains of
{\mathfrak{s}(z)}
, none of which can concatenate. Then, using the compactness of
\alpha_{v}(s)
, we can split
\alpha_{v}(s)
into finitely such segments. We can now perturb slightly to make sure the continuation strips are all transversely cut-out. By Gromov compactness, having no large continuation strips of certain homotopy class is a condition invariant under small enough perturbations of
J(s,t)
. We then apply the ”solitons cannot concatenate” argument again to conclude that the glued continuation strips must be of the path detour class. We now turn off the perturbation, and argue by Gromov compactness again, to conclude that for some elongation function, the homotopy class must also be the path detour class, concluding (2).

∎

In addition, a simpler argument leads to the following type of stability under
\varepsilon
-scaling:

Lemma 5.31

(Scaling stability)
.

For
z\in\EuScript{W}^{c}
, there exists
\varepsilon(z)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for all
\varepsilon^{\prime},\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon(z))
,
\varepsilon^{\prime}<\varepsilon
, the continuation map
CF(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L;J_{cyl}^{e})\to CF(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L;J_{cyl}^{e^{\prime}})
is the identity map.

Proof.

The proof is similar to Part (1) in
Proposition

5.30
. Take
\varepsilon(z)
as in
Theorem

4.2
so that
(\epsilon L,T^{\ast}_{z}S)
is disk-free for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon(z))
. By the previous discussion, we obtain a family
J_{cyl}^{s}
such that
J_{cyl}^{s}=J_{s^{-1}g}
in the disk bundle for
\varepsilon^{\prime}\leq s\leq\varepsilon
.
Fix an elongation function
l
, then for each
\varepsilon^{\prime}<a^{\prime}<a<e
, the continuation map from
CF(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L;J_{cyl}^{a})
to
CF(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L;J_{cyl}^{a^{\prime}})
is given by counting pseudo-holomorphic strips with the boundary conditions

(32)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{s}u+{J}(s,t)\partial_{t}u=0&\\
u(s,1)\subset L&\\
u(s,0)\subset T^{\ast}_{z}S&\\
\displaystyle\lim_{s\to\pm\infty}u(s,t)\in T^{\ast}_{z}S\cap L,\\
\end{cases}

using the family almost complex structures
J(s,t)
that are given by small perturbations of the family
J_{cyl}^{l(s)a+[1-l(s)]a^{\prime}}
. Since the equation is homogeneous, the strips that travel between the same lifts of
T^{\ast}_{z}S
must all be constant, and the strips that travel between distinct lifts of
T^{\ast}_{z}S
must have energy a priori bounded below by some small
h\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. The same argument as in
Proposition

5.30
implies that for
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert a^{\prime}-a\right\rvert}
small enough, the continuation strips are all constant, and so they induce the identity map. The rest follows from concatenation compatibility, from which we conclude that the continuation map from
s=\varepsilon
to
s=\varepsilon^{\prime}
must be the identity map.
∎

Let us finally establish the equivalence between
\mathbb{F}
and
\Phi{W}
:

Proof of
Theorem

5.24
.

Let us first compare the parallel transports for a path of the form
\rho(s)=\alpha_{v}(s)
starting at
z\in\EuScript{W}^{c}
and
s
small enough. Since any
\Phi{W}(V)
has trivial parallel transport for
V\in\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(L)
, c.f.
Definition

5.6
.(i), we must argue that the same holds for
\mathbb{F}(V)
, for
\varepsilon
small enough. Fix a small neighbourhood
U_{\rho}\subset\EuScript{W}^{c}
of
\rho
. By
Theorem

4.2
, there exists some
\varepsilon(\rho)>0
such that for all
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon(\rho)
,
(T^{\ast}_{z}S,\varepsilon L),z\in U_{\rho}
is disk-free. Fix
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon({\rho})
, then by
Section

5.2.3
, the parallel transport of the latter is given by the moduli spaces of deformed continuation strips, cf.
Proposition

5.20
. By
Lemma

5.28
and
Proposition

5.30
the corresponding moduli spaces of continuation strips for such a path away from
\EuScript{W}
is cobordant to the moduli space of trivial strips. For the orientations, it suffices to note that the sphere bundle counts of the trivial strips give the required orientation, see e.g.
[
Nho24
, Lemma 6.14]
. This implies that the count of deformed continuation strips is that of trivial strips, and therefore the parallel transport of
\mathbb{F}(V)
along such
\alpha_{v}(s)\subset\EuScript{W}^{c}
equals that of
\Phi{W}(V)
. By concatenation compatibility, we conclude that the parallel transports agree over any free
\EuScript{W}
-adapted path.

For each
z\in\EuScript{W}
, we can consider a
\EuScript{W}
-adapted short path of the form
\rho(s)=\alpha_{v}(s)
, intersecting
\EuScript{W}
at
z
, just as in
Definition

5.29
. By Gromov compactness, we can perturb by a small amount so that the moduli space of large continuation strips is transversely cut-out, and so that the boundary homotopy classes belong to the path detour class.
Let
u(s,t)
be the resulting continuation strip along such path: we must show that the assignment
{\mathfrak{s}(z;\mathfrak{w})}\to\sigma(\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{u},\alpha_{v}(s))
(where the count is defined using the homotopy refinement
Corollary

5.22
) gives a functor with the properties as in
Definition

3.20
. For each vertex
v\in\EuScript{W}
, introduce nearby points
b\in\EuScript{W}
on each edge adjacent to
v
and for each such
b
, consider the corresponding short path arcs
\alpha_{\nu}^{(b)}(s)
, intersecting
\EuScript{W}
in the positive direction. We now proceed on inductively, by starting with the points close to the initial walls, and then following the birth of new walls along
\EuScript{W}
. First, for these points
b
close enough to the initial rays of the
D_{4}^{-}
-vertex
v
, direct computation gives the count
\sigma(\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{u},\alpha_{v}(s))=1
, e.g. c.f.
[
GMN13a
, Section 5.6, Figure 21]
. It thus suffices to show that
\sigma(\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{u},\alpha_{v}(s))
satisfies the same properties as in
Definition

3.20
near the creation joints of
\EuScript{W}
. At the same time, that these properties are satisfied is a consequence of
Proposition

5.20
and the fact that the trivial loop encircling the vertex of
\EuScript{W}
is contractible, and thus the associated (cobordism class of) moduli space of continuation strips consists of constant strips (one per intersection point), up to cobordism. The cancellation of the concatenation of non-trivial continuation strips along the trivial loop is encoded in the 2d Hori-vafa formula
Definition

3.20
. Therefore, since the counting functions are the same, the two local systems
\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}(V)
and
\mathbb{F}(V)
must also be isomorphic.
∎

Theorem

5.8
now follows from
Theorem

5.24
:

Proof of
Theorem

5.8
.

By
Theorem

5.24
, the parallel transport
\Phi{W}(V)(\rho(\mathfrak{r}))
is equivalent to that of
\mathbb{F}(V)(\rho(\mathfrak{r}))
. In the adiabatic limit, the deformed continuation strips contributing to this latter parallel transport converge to
D_{4}^{-}
-flowtrees with semi-infinite root edges asymptotic to
\mathfrak{r}
, and the counts are in fact equal. Indeed, by
[
EHK16
, Lemma 5.12]
and
[
EHK16
, Theorem 1.5]
, there exists a bijective correspondence between rigid flow-trees for (a perturbed and neck-stretched version of)
22
22

22

This modification of
L
is denoted by
L^{\dagger}
in
[
Ekh07
,
EHK16
]
.

L
and rigid index-
0
pseudo-holomorphic disks (adjusted, in the sense of
[
EHK16
, Section 3.2]
) with boundary on that exact Betti Lagrangian, all constrained to be asymptotic to
\mathfrak{r}
. In general, the cobordism type of the latter moduli space of pseudo-holomorphic disks is not an invariant because of bifurcations given by index
-1
disks, see e.g.
[
EHK16
, Lemma 3.13]
. That said,
Lemma

5.13
shows that Betti Lagrangians
L\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
have Maslov index
0
and thus such index-
(-1)
disks cannot appear. Therefore, the cobordism type of this moduli space is an invariant up to compactly supported Hamiltonian isotopy and, consequently, so is the count of
D_{4}^{-}
-trees asymptotic to
\mathfrak{r}
.
∎

5.4.
Proof of
Theorem

2

The object of this subsection is to conclude the proof of
Theorem

2
. The direction
(\Longleftarrow)
of the characterization in
Theorem

2
was established in
Section

4
, c.f.
Theorem

4.2
. We must thus show the implication
(\Longrightarrow)
. This will be first done for exact Betti Lagrangians in
Section

5.4.1
, using the results of this section (c.f.
Proposition

5.30
) and then
Section

5.4.2
is devoted to the meromorphic WKB case. To align with
Definition

4.1
, the existence of a pseudo-holomorphic disk in the adiabatic limit can be made precise as follows:

Definition 5.32
.

The pair of Lagrangians
(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L)
bounds a pseudo-holomorphic disk in the adiabatic limit if there exists a constant
\varepsilon_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
there exists a neighborhood
U_{\varepsilon}(z)\subset S
and a point
w\in U_{\varepsilon}(z)
in that neighborhood for which
(\varepsilon L,T^{\ast}_{w}S)
bounds a non-constant pseudo-holomorphic disk.
\Box

The results established thus far, specifically
Theorem

4.2
and
Proposition

5.30
, now suffice to conclude
Theorem

2
for exact Betti Lagrangians.

5.4.1.
Proof of
Theorem

2
for exact Betti Lagrangians

Let
z\in\EuScript{W}
be a point in a wall of the spectral network, we must argue that there exists a pseudo-holomorphic strip between
T_{z}^{*}S
and
L
in the adiabatic limit. By
Theorem

4.2
, given any neighborhood
U\subset S
of
z
, we can find a nested neighborhood
W\subset U
and a constant
\varepsilon_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, depending on
U
, such that the pair of Lagrangians
(T^{\ast}_{w}S,\varepsilon L)
is disk-free for all points
w\in U\setminus W
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
. Consider a
\EuScript{W}
-adapted short path
\alpha_{v}
with its endpoints in
U\setminus W
and intersecting
\EuScript{W}
and
z
. By the same argument as in the proof of
Theorem

5.24
, there exists some
\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime})
the Floer-theoretic parallel transport
\mathbb{F}(V)(\mathring{\alpha}_{v}(s))
equals that of
\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}(V)(\mathring{\alpha}_{v}(s))
, where
\mathring{\alpha}_{v}(s)
denotes the lift to the unit cotangent bundle
\mathring{S}
. The latter parallel transport is non-trivial, because the 2d-4d BPS counting indices from
Section

3.5
are non-zero at all walls of
\EuScript{W}
, as
\EuScript{W}
is creative. Thus the former parallel transport
\mathbb{F}(V)(\mathring{\alpha}_{v}(s))
is non-trivial. This implies the existence of a point
w\in W
such that
(T^{\ast}_{w}S,\varepsilon L)
is not disk-free, as otherwise
Proposition

5.30
would imply
\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}(V){(\mathring{\alpha}_{v}(s)))}
was trivial. In conclusion, as the adiabatic parameter
\varepsilon\to 0
tends to zero, this concludes the existence of a pseudo-holomorphic strip between
L
and
T^{*}_{z}S
in the adiabatic limit, as required.

5.4.2.
Proof of
Theorem

2
for meromorphic spectral curves

There are a few differences between the case of a meromorphic spectral curve
\Sigma\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
and an exact Betti Lagrangian
L\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
. That said, when studying pseudo-holomorphic curves with respect to a generic meromorphic spectral curve
\Sigma\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
, we will now establish results showing that the behaviors that occur are
as if
we were under an exactness assumption. In that sense, the proof follows closely that of the exact case and we only highlight the key ingredients. First, we have the following strengthening of the Stokes condition in
Definition

4.1
.

Lemma 5.33
.

Let
z\in\EuScript{W}
be a non-initial point. Then, for every
E\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, there exists a neighbourhood
U_{z}(E)\subset S
of
z
and
\varepsilon_{0}(E,z)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that there are no non-constant
J_{g}
-holomorphic strips bounded between
\varepsilon\Sigma
and
T^{\ast}_{w}S
of energy less than
E
, for
w\in\EuScript{W}^{c}(E)
.

Proof.

From
Theorem

4.2
, such pseudo-holomophic strips must have the homology class of the parallel transport of the solitons with energy less than
E
. Since the integral of the holomorphic Liouville 1-form along the boundary of such a strip must be real and positive,
z
must lie on
\EuScript{W}(E)
.
∎

The following notation is useful:

Definition 5.34
.

Let
L\subset T^{*}S
be a Betti Lagrangian. By definition,
L
is said to be disk-free in the adiabatic limit, if for every
E\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
there exists
\varepsilon_{0}(E)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}(E))
, the scaled Lagrangian
\varepsilon L
does not bound a pseudo-holomorphic disk of energy less than equal to
E
.
\Box

Then the same argument used for
Theorem

4.2
implies the following variation, which tells us that
\Sigma
is tautologically unobstructed, up to some energy. In other words, we can run the usual Floer theoretic machinery (see c.f
[
Sei15
, Section 8(c)]
) up to some energy cut-off.

Proposition 5.35
.

Let
\Sigma
be a meromorphic spectral curve with a WKB spectral network
\EuScript{W}
. Then
\Sigma
is disk-free in the adiabatic limit.

Proof.

Let
u_{\varepsilon}
be a sequence of somewhere injective disks of energy less than
E
with boundary on
\varepsilon\Sigma
. Introduce three punctures on the boundary to fix the conformal structure and apply the same adiabatic degeneration argument as in
Theorem

4.2
. By
Section

2.7
, the walls of
\EuScript{W}
never return to the branch points and thus the initial edge condition implies that the disks cannot close up. Thus
\varepsilon\Sigma
must be disk-free for small enough
\varepsilon
.
∎

The scaling relations is as follows: the adiabatic cut-off
\varepsilon_{0}(E)
tends to zero as the energy
E
is sent to infinity, i.e.
\varepsilon_{0}(E)\to 0
for
E\to\infty
. The necessary variant of
Proposition

5.26
can be discussed as follows.

Consider an energy cut-off
E\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. The gapped condition implies that there exists a small enough parameter
h\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
\EuScript{W}(E)
and
\EuScript{W}(E+h)
are isotopic as stratified 1-manifolds with boundary. For each vertex
v
of the energy-truncated
\EuScript{W}(E)
, choose a Stokes neighborhood and a bump function
\rho
. Fix a neighborhood of
v
contained in that of
Proposition

5.35
, with diameter
C\delta
for some constant
C\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. From
Proposition

5.35
, the pair
(T^{\ast}_{z}S,\varepsilon\Sigma)
is disk-free for
z\in B_{C\delta}(v)\setminus\EuScript{W}(E)
. Choose
C
such that
C\delta
is less than the injectivity radius of
v
consider
\alpha(s)
a geodesic line segment contained in
B_{C\delta}(v)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be an
(ij)
-wall of
\EuScript{W}(E)
,
W\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int\lambda^{i}-\lambda^{j}=x+iy
be the conformal flat coordinate and
(p^{x},p^{y})
dual coordinates to
(x,y)
; here
W
is defined by a line integral from the given point to the end variable point. For
z\in\mathfrak{w}
, we write
\alpha_{z}(s)
for the path given by
s\to z+is
in the
W
-coordinate. Let
\sup_{\rho}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert L\right\rvert}
be the supremum of the norm of the sheets of
L
, over a small neighbourhood of
\rho
that contains the support of the generating Hamiltonian
H^{v}
. Then observe that the Hamiltonian term
H^{v}
in
Eq.

31
is less than
\epsilon\sup_{\rho}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert L\right\rvert}
. The analogue of
Proposition

5.26
then reads:

Proposition 5.36
.

In the notation above, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}(z,E)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}(z,E))
the following holds:

(1)

There exists
d(z,\varepsilon)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and an elongation function such that, given a free
\EuScript{W}
-adapted line segment
\alpha(s)
contained in
B_{C\delta}
of length
\varepsilon
, the moduli of
J_{g}
-holomorphic continuation strips of energy less than
\varepsilon(E+\hbar)
are all small.

(2)

For an interior point
z\in\EuScript{W}(E)\cap B_{C\delta}
, there exists
\delta(z,\varepsilon)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and an elongation function such that along the short path
\alpha_{z}(s)
,
s\in[-\delta(z,\varepsilon),\delta(z,\varepsilon)]
, any large
J_{g}
-holomorphic continuation strips of energy less than
\varepsilon(E+\hbar)
must have the homotopy class of a soliton at
z
.

Proof.

The argument follows that of
Proposition

5.26
. For (1), choosing the elongation function as in
Proposition

5.26
, since the Hamiltonian term contribution in the energy formula is of size
d\cdot\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\sup_{\rho}{L}\right\rvert}\epsilon\cdot\int l^{\prime}(s)ds
, for
d
small enough any pseudo-holomorphic strip bounded by
(T^{\ast}_{z}S,\varepsilon\Sigma)
which comes from breaking off a continuation strip must have energy less than
\varepsilon E
. Therefore, for any
s_{0}\in[0,1]
, there exists
\eta(s_{0})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that the moduli of
J_{g}
-continuation strips along
[s-\eta(s_{0}),s+\eta(s_{0})]
is cobordant to the moduli of trivial strips. For (2), a similar argument applies: the key point is that for
J_{g}
, we can fix the homotopy classes of continuation strips with energy bounded above by
\leq E+\delta
.
∎

Since there are finitely many vertices in
\EuScript{W}(E)
, let us choose
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
Lemma

5.33
and
Proposition

5.36
hold. Given an
(ij)
-wall
\mathfrak{w}
, which is compact, we choose a (small) neighborhood
U_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset S
of
\mathfrak{w}
so that
Lemma

5.33
holds for all
w\in U_{\mathfrak{w}}\setminus\EuScript{W}(E)
. We also (slightly) truncate
\mathfrak{w}
off near the starting ends if
\mathfrak{w}
begins at the initial vertices of
\EuScript{W}(E)
. The following proposition has exactly the same proof as
Proposition

5.36
:

Proposition 5.37
.

In the notation above, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}(v)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that, for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}(v,E))
, there exists
\delta(\mathfrak{w},\varepsilon)>0
such that the following conditions hold for strips along the path
s\to z+is
,
s\in[-\delta(\mathfrak{w},\varepsilon),\delta(\mathfrak{w},\varepsilon)]
:

(1)

For any
z\in U_{\mathfrak{w}}\setminus\EuScript{W}
, the
J_{g}
-holomorphic continuation strips of energy less than
\varepsilon(E+\hbar)
are all small.

(2)

For any
z\in\mathfrak{w}\cap U_{\mathfrak{w}}
, then the large
J_{g}
-holomorphic continuation strips of energy less than
\varepsilon(E+\hbar)
all have the homotopy class of a soliton at
z
.

In general,
J_{g}
-holomorphic continuation strips are not going to be regular in this content. Fortunately, given the energy bound, all the relevant moduli space of continuation strips are cobordant with the given energy cut-off (here the perturbation of the almost complex structure must be chosen in a way that it agrees with
J_{g}
on the boundary mapping to
L
, to avoid boundary bubbling, c.f
[
Sei15
, Section 8(c)]
). Indeed, the energy formula for short enough arc implies that the dominant term in the energy is
\int\lambda_{st}
over the boundary mapping to
\varepsilon L
, the Hamiltonian term being proportionately small, which is invariant up to homotopy. Therefore, the energy of the perturbed continuation strip is determined by the boundary homotopy class.
23
23

23

See also the energy estimates in
[
Fuk+09a
, Section 5.3.5]
; in their terminology, the Hofer norm of the Hamiltonian isotopy as observed by the disk is small enough.
It follows that we can regularize the moduli space by choosing some unspecified perturbation of
J_{g}
to make the strips regular. The following terminology is convenient:

Definition 5.38
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}
be an active wall of
\EuScript{W}(E)
and
z\in\mathfrak{w}
. By definition,
z
supports a disk in the adiabatic limit if for any
E\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
there exists
\varepsilon(z)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}(z))
there exists
\delta(z,\varepsilon)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that the moduli space of large
J_{g}
-holomorphic continuation strip along
\alpha_{z}(s)
,
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert s\right\rvert}<\delta(z,\varepsilon)
, of energy less than
\varepsilon E
is non-empty and its regularization is not cobordant to the empty set.

By definition, a wall
\mathfrak{w}
is said to support a non-constant disk in the adiabatic limit if the set of points
z\in\mathfrak{w}
that support a disk in the adiabatic limit is non-empty.
\Box

Proof of
Theorem

2
in meromorphic case.

First, consider a wall
\mathfrak{w}
of
\EuScript{W}(E)
. Let us show that the set of points on
\mathfrak{w}
that support a disk in the adiabatic limit is open and closed. Indeed, openness is a consequence of Gromov compactness and regularity of strips being an open condition. For closedness, note that
[
Nho24
, Proposition 4.4]
shows the set is non-empty. Consider then a sequence of points
z_{n}\to z
such that each support a disk in the adiabatic limit. For large enough
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
z_{n}
belongs to the Stokes neighborhood of
z
and, by assumption, the moduli space of non-diagonal
J_{g}
-holomorphic continuation strips along
\alpha_{z_{n}}(s)
is non-empty. Concatenate the horizontal arc coming from the top of
\alpha_{z}(s)
to the arc
\alpha_{z_{n}}(s)
, and further concatenate it with the horizontal arc connecting to the bottom of
\alpha_{z}(s)
. The resulting path is isotopic to the arc
\alpha_{z}(s)
. Now, any two moduli space of
J_{g}
-holomorphic continuation strips with given energy less than
\varepsilon(E+\hbar)
are cobordant, and since the moduli space of large
J_{g}
-holomorphic continuation strips along
\alpha_{z_{n}}(s)
is not (virtually) null-cobordant, the same holds for
\alpha_{z}(s)
.

Second, we discuss vertices of the spectral network. Suppose that the active ingoing walls all support disks in the adiabatic limit. We claim that this is also the case for the active outgoing walls. Indeed, by
Proposition

5.36
, for a small neighborhood of
v
the moduli space of
J_{g}
-holomorphic continuation strips for the short paths across the outgoing walls is virtually cobordant to the gluing of the ingoing moduli spaces. The count of these gluings is controlled by the index in
Definition

3.20
, and so we are reduced to the exact situation.
∎

6.
Spectral networks and 4d Fukaya Categories

In this section we relate spectral networks and Fukaya categories in 4-dimensions, in particular proving
Theorem

4
. Specifically, we show how exact Betti Lagrangians can be understood as objects of a Fukaya category in two related ways, cf. Sections
6.1
and
6.3
, and how the parallel transport rules dictated by a spectral network
\EuScript{W}
are in fact encoding
\mu_{2}
-type morphisms for the underlying
A_{\infty}
-structures naturally appearing in Floer theory, cf.
Theorem

6.3
, proven in
Section

6.2
, and
Theorem

6.12
, proven in
Section

6.3
. An explicit description of the associated Yoneda
A_{\infty}
-module over
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
is also provided in
Section

6.4
. See
[
GPS20
]
for references and context on (partially) wrapped Fukaya
A_{\infty}
-categories. In brief, the present section enhances the Floer-theoretic characterization of spectral networks and non-abelian parallel transport, as presented in Sections
4
and
5
, to the
A_{\infty}
-categorical framework. In this section, we will focus our attention on exact Betti Lagrangians.

6.1.
The Yoneda module of an exact Betti Lagrangian

Let
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
denote the wrapped Fukaya
A_{\infty}
-category of the open Liouville sector
(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
, and
\mbox{mod}_{k}
the dg-category of chain complexes of
k
-modules, understood as an
A_{\infty}
-category with higher
\mu_{k}
vanishing,
k\geq 2
