. A Betti Lagrangian
L\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
does
not
define an object in
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
, due to the nature of its asymptotic ends near the marked points, cf.
Section

2
. Thus, even if we endow a Betti Lagrangian
L
with a local system
24
24

24

Since
[
GPS20
]
uses local systems, not twisted, we use spin structures for Betti Lagrangians to orient. The relation between twisted local systems and spin structures is discussed in
Section

A.3
. We can use
Corollary

5.23
to pass from spin structure count to twisted count. Alternatively, one could develop
[
GPS20
]
for twisted local systems.

V\in\mbox{Loc}(L)
, it is a priori unclear how Betti Lagrangians and their spectral networks fits within the context of Fukaya categories. There are at least two solutions to address this, depending on whether we want to preserve the geometry of
L
at infinity or we allow geometric modifications of
L
at infinity. The first approach is presented in this section and the second in
Section

6.3
.

For the first approach, the algebraic idea is simple: if
C\in\mbox{Ob}(\EuScript{C})
is an object in a category, then
\mbox{Hom}_{\EuScript{C}}(\cdot,C)\in\mbox{Fun}(\EuScript{C}^{op},\mbox{mod}_{k})
defines a module over
\EuScript{C}
. This module is often known as the Yoneda module. Since a Betti Lagrangian
L\notin\mbox{Ob}(\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S))
is not an object, as it does not have compact horizontal support, we cannot merely construct a Yoneda module in this manner. Nevertheless, given a Betti Lagrangian
(L,V)
endowed with a local system
V\in\mbox{Loc}(L)
, we now construct an
A_{\infty}
-module over
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
which behaves
as if
it was the Yoneda module of an object given by
(L,V)
. Intuitively, its value on objects
P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}\in\mbox{Ob}(\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S))
is given by counting pseudo-holomorphic polygons with boundary on the Lagrangians
P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}
and the Betti Lagrangian
L
. Consider the Floer cohomology groups
HF^{\ast}(P,(L,V))
as in
Section

5
, the result reads as follows:

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
L\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
be a Betti Lagrangian endowed with a local system
V\in\mbox{Loc}(L)
. Then there exists an
A_{\infty}
-module

\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)\longrightarrow\mbox{mod}_{k}
with cohomologies

H^{\ast}(\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}(P))\cong HF^{\ast}(P,L),

where
P\subset T^{*}S
is any exact cylindrical Lagrangian with compact horizontal support.

Proof.

The intuition is to formally add the Betti Lagrangian
L\subset T^{*}S
as the most negative object in
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
. We provide the necessary details, as follows. By
[
GPS20
, Prop. 3.43]
, the wrapped Fukaya
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
can be obtained as the strictly unital directed
A_{\infty}
-category of a certain (terminal) decorated poset
\overrightarrow{P}(S)_{ter}
for
(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
localized at the set of continuation morphisms, as discussed in Section 3.8 in ibid. To begin, consider a decorated poset
\overrightarrow{P}(S)
, not necessarily terminal, which satisfies the following conditions:

(a)

Each Lagrangian
P_{k}
in the poset is transverse to
L
, and each family of almost complex structure can be extended to an almost complex structure on
T^{\ast}S
, that agrees with
J_{cyl}
outside some uniform compact base.

(b)

For each chain
p_{0}>p_{1}>\ldots>p_{k}
, the moduli space of pseudo-holomorphic polygons bounded by the (ordered) tuple of Lagrangians
(P_{p_{0}},P_{p_{1}},\ldots,P_{p_{k}},L)
is also transversely cut-out and compatible with gluing.

Such decorated poset exists after perturbing the almost complex structure. This requires the fact that the relevant pseudo-holomorphic polygons do not escape off to infinity. Namely, that given the exact Betti Lagrangian
L
, the (pairwise transverse) cylindrical Lagrangians
P_{1},\dots,P_{k}\subset T^{\ast}S
and
\{J_{s}\}
a family of almost complex structures on
T^{\ast}S
, we need that any
J_{s}
-holomorphic polygon with boundary conditions given by
(P_{1},\dots,P_{k},L)
has uniformly bounded diameter, and the same holds with moving Lagrangian boundary conditions on
P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}
given by positive isotopies. This geometric condition does hold in our case:
L
is contained in a finite-radius disk bundle, it is uniformly geometrically bounded with respect to
J_{s}
and, being exact, the energy of such polygons is a priori bounded by the action of the intersection points. Therefore, the monotonicity argument in
[
Nho24
, Proposition 3.29]
applies to guarantee such condition.

We then construct an
A_{\infty}
-module
\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}^{pre}
over the poset in
\overrightarrow{P}(S)
with its
A_{\infty}
-operations given by counting pseudo-holomorphic polygons between the Lagrangians
\{P_{i}\}
in
\overrightarrow{P}(S)
. For such chain of Lagrangians
\{P_{i}\}
, we define the map

\mu_{k-1|1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{O}(P_{1},P_{2})\otimes\dots\mathcal{O}(P_{k-1},P_{k})\otimes\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}^{pre}(P_{k})\longrightarrow\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}^{pre}(P_{1}),\quad P_{1}>P_{2}...>P_{k},

by counting pseudo-holomorphic
(k+1)
-gons with boundary conditions given by
(P_{1},....,P_{k},L)
, with
\mathcal{O}
as in Eq. (3.52) in ibid. We set the
A_{\infty}
-module map to be equal to zero otherwise, except for the case
\mu_{1|1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}^{pre}(P)\longrightarrow\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}^{pre}(P)
where
\overrightarrow{P}(P,P)=\mathbb{Z}
acts as the identity. The above defines
\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}^{pre}
as
A_{\infty}
-module over the poset in
\overrightarrow{P}(S)
, we need to argue that it descends to the localization
\mathfrak{W}_{\overrightarrow{P}(S)}
by continuation morphisms. For that, note that given any positive isotopy of cylindrical Lagrangians
\{K_{t}\}
,
t\in[0,1]
, the continuation map
CF(K_{0},L)\to CF(K_{1},L)
is invertible. Indeed, since
L
lies in a finite disk bundle,
L
and
K_{t}
do not intersect at infinity, thus the conclusion follows from the argument in
[
GPS20
, Lemma 3.21]
and the final assertion in
[
GPS20
, Lemma 3.26]
. It also follows that
C^{\ast}(\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}^{pre}(P))\simeq CF^{\ast}(P,L)
.

It now suffices to relate
\mathfrak{W}_{\overrightarrow{P}(S)}
to
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
. For that, consider the category of decorated posets which satisfy
(a)
and
(b)
above, are downward-closed and duplicate-free. Denote its terminal object by
\overrightarrow{P}(S,L)_{ter}
, cf.
[
GPS20
, Lemma 3.42]
. The inclusion
\overrightarrow{P}(S,L)_{ter}\subset\overrightarrow{P}(S)_{ter}
of posets induces an inclusion functor between the associated localized categories, and thus
\mathfrak{W}_{\overrightarrow{P}(S)}
admits a tautological inclusion functor into
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
, which is in fact an equivalence. By precomposing
\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}^{pre}
with the inverse of this equivalence, we obtain the required
A_{\infty}
-module
\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}
.∎

Remark 6.2
.

We use the following convention for composition and wrapping: the
\mu_{2}
-maps are given as

\mu_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}CF(A,B)\otimes CF(B,C)\to CF(A,C)

and, when wrapping is involved, a morphism
A\to B
has its domain
A
more positive than its codomain
B
, i.e. positive wrapping gives
CF(A^{w},A)\otimes CF(A,B)\to CF(A^{w},B)
. In particular, the Betti Lagrangian
L\subset T^{*}S
, e.g. a spectral curve, is always taken on the right slot of
CF(\cdot,\cdot)
, as it is the most negative object. Similarly, in the partially wrapped setting, the positive push-off of the (cylindrized) Betti Lagrangian is the most negative object, in that small negative wrapping makes it fall into
\partial_{\infty}L
.
\Box

In the same line as the equivalence established in
Section

5.3
, cf.
Theorem

5.24
, the
A_{\infty}
-modules
\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}
in
Proposition

6.1
are closely related to the non-abelianized local systems, as follows. First, for technical reasons, we normalize the Riemannian metric
(S,g)
so that
\mbox{inj}(g)>2
, which is achieved after a constant metric rescaling
g\to c^{2}g
; this is necessary for the proof of
Proposition

6.6
to estimate action. Now, by definition, two points
z,w\in({\bf S},g)
in a Betti surface with a spectral network
\EuScript{W}\subset S
are said to be
\EuScript{W}
-adapted if
\mbox{dist}_{g}(z,w)<\mbox{inj}(g)/20
and the minimal geodesic
\alpha_{zw}
between
z,w
is a
\EuScript{W}
-adapted path (cf.
Definition

5.4
). Since the metric
(S,g)
is complete, we can and do choose
w
in terms of
z
such that the energy functional on the path space
\Omega_{z,w}
between
z
and
w
is a Morse functional, cf.
[
Mil63
, Theorems 14.2, 18.1 & 18.2]
. This ensures the transversality of intersections of cotangent fibres in
(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
after wrapping by the geodesic flow.

We implicitly identify
\mbox{Hom}_{\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,T^{\ast}_{w}S)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=CF^{\ast}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,T^{\ast}_{w}S)
with chains on the path space
\Omega_{z,w}S
, see
[
Abo12
]
. In fact,
CF^{\ast}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,T^{\ast}_{z}S)
is
A_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphic to
C_{-\ast}(\Omega_{z}S)
endowed with the
A_{\infty}
-refined Pontryagin product. The reason we focus on cotangent fibers is that
[
Abo11
]
shows that any cotangent fiber
T^{\ast}_{z}S\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
generates
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
, and thus we have an
A_{\infty}
-quasi-equivalence
\mbox{Tw}(\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S))\simeq\mbox{Tw}(CF^{\ast}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,T^{\ast}_{z}S))
. We denote by

\mu_{1|1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{Hom}_{\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,T^{\ast}_{w}S)\otimes\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}\longrightarrow\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}

the structure map of the
A_{\infty}
-module
\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}
in these degrees evaluated at two cotangent fibers. The categorical statement relating Floer theory to non-abelianized local system reads as follows:

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
(S,g)
be a Betti surface,
L\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
a Betti Lagrangian,
V\in\mbox{Loc}(L)
,
\EuScript{W}\subset S
an adapted spectral network and
z,w\in S
an
\EuScript{W}
-adapted pair. Then the minimal geodesic generator
[\alpha_{zw}]\in\mbox{Hom}_{\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,T^{\ast}_{w}S)
satisfies

(33)

\mu_{1|1}([\alpha_{zw}],\cdot)=\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}(V)(\alpha_{zw}).

\Box

In brief,
Eq.

33
equates a map given by Floer-theoretically counting pseudo-holomorphic strips, on its left, to the topological parallel transport given by the spectral network, on the right. Note that the left hand side of the isomorphism
Eq.

33
can be identified with
\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}([\alpha_{zw}])
, understanding
\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}
as being applied to a morphism in
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
. Thus the structure map
\mu_{1|1}
appearing in
Theorem

6.3
categorically (and cohomologically) describes the parallel transport map from
z
to
w
when its domain is evaluated at
\mbox{Hom}_{\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,T^{\ast}_{w}S)\otimes\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}(T^{\ast}_{z}S)
.
Theorem

6.3
will be proven in a moment, in
Section

6.2
, after we discuss a useful corollary.

Given the
A_{\infty}
-quasi-equivalence
\mbox{Tw}(\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S))\simeq\mbox{Tw}(CF^{\ast}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,T^{\ast}_{z}S))
, the right hand side is a model for
\mbox{Loc}(S)\simeq C_{-\ast}(\Omega_{z}S)
-mod. Thus, a local system in
\mbox{Loc}(S)
can be understood as a
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
-module. It is in general challenging to explicitly present
A_{\infty}
-modules over
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
, as it involves solving partial differential equations, significant preliminary Floer data to be specified and homotopy (co)limits need to be computed. An advantageous consequence of
Theorem

6.3
is that we can explicitly describe the class of
A_{\infty}
-modules over
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
arising as non-abelianized local system via the
A_{\infty}
-quasi-equivalence above. Specifically, under these identifications, we have the following

Corollary 6.4
.

Let
(S,g)
be a Betti surface,
L\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
a Betti Lagrangian,
V\in\mbox{Loc}(L)
, and
\EuScript{W}\subset S
a spectral network adapted to
L
. Then, under the
A_{\infty}
-quasi-equivalence
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)\mbox{-mod}\cong C_{-\ast}(\Omega_{z}S)
-mod,

\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}\simeq\Phi{W}(V),

as
A_{\infty}
-modules over
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
. That is, the
A_{\infty}
-module

\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)\longrightarrow\mbox{mod}_{k}
is homotopic to the
A_{\infty}
-module corresponding to
\Phi{W}(V)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{Loc}(S)\longrightarrow\mbox{mod}_{k}
.
\Box

6.2.
Proof of
Theorem

6.3
(Floer
\mu_{1|1}
is spectral transport)

We need to compute the wrapped Floer complex using a model that is also suitable for computing the
\mu_{1|1}
-maps in the statement of
Theorem

6.3
. For that, we introduce such a model in
Section

6.2.1
, then compute the structure maps in
Section

6.2.3
, and conclude the argument in
Section

6.2.4
.

6.2.1.
A model for computing the wrapped Floer complex

Given two nearby points
z,w\in S
, the goal of this subsection is to establish that appropriately chosen positive isotopies applied to
T^{\ast}_{z}S
exactly lead to a degree-0 cycle in
\mbox{Hom}_{\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,T^{\ast}_{w}S)
represented by the minimal geodesic
\alpha_{zw}
between
z
and
w
, with this morphism complex given by direct limits as in
[
GPS20
]
.

Recall that an exact isotopy
\{P\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of cylindrical Lagrangians is said to be positive (at infinity), denoted by
P_{0}\rightsquigarrow P_{1}
, if
\alpha(\partial_{t}(\partial_{\infty}P_{t}))>0
, where
\partial_{\infty}P_{t}
denotes the boundary at infinity of
P_{t}
and
\alpha
is a given contact form. Note that a positive isotopy
P_{0}\rightsquigarrow P_{1}
induces an element
c\in CF(P_{1},P_{0})
, called the continuation element, cf.
[
GPS20
, Section 3.3]
. The relationship between continuation elements and continuation maps is as follows. If
P_{0}\rightsquigarrow P_{1}
and
K\subset T^{*}S
is a Lagrangian with
K,P_{0},P_{1}
pairwise transverse, then the continuation map

c(P_{t})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}CF(P_{0},K)\to CF(P_{1},K)

associated to the continuation element
c\in CF^{0}(P_{1},P_{0})
is chain homotopic to the evaluation
\mu_{2}(c,\cdot)
of the map

\mu_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}CF(P_{1},P_{0})\otimes CF(P_{0},K)\to CF(P_{1},K)

of the
A_{\infty}
-structure at the continuation element
c
. In fact, this even holds if we fix
P
and move
K
by a negative isotopy. Indeed,
[
GPS20
, Lemma 3.26]
proves this for
K,P
having compact horizontal support; for
K
an exact Betti Lagrangian, the same argument works because of the existence of uniform diameter bounds, as discussed in the proof of
Proposition

6.1
. Thus we can and do implicitly switch between continuation elements and continuation maps.

In general, the wrapped Floer cohomology groups defined in
[
GPS20
, Section 3.4]
are given as the direct limit

\displaystyle HW^{\ast}(P,K)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\varinjlim_{(P\rightsquigarrow P^{w})^{+}}HF^{\ast}(P^{w},K)

over (cofinal) sequences of positive isotopies. Our task is to choose a suitable such cofinal sequence, which we explicitly present as a sequence
P_{0}\rightsquigarrow P_{1}\rightsquigarrow P_{1}\rightsquigarrow\cdots
of Lagrangians. We often denote
HF^{\ast}(P,K)=HW^{\ast}(P,K)
when it is clear by context that wrapping must be taken: we always consider a positive wrapping of
P
, in the sense above, though one can equivalently negatively wrap
K
. To define our cofinal sequence, it is convenient to introduce the following notation:

Definition 6.5

(Wrapped fibres and fibre wrappings)
.

A cylindrical Lagrangian
F\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
is said to be a wrapped fibre if there exists a point
z\in S
and a positive isotopy
T^{\ast}_{z}S\rightsquigarrow F
supported on the complement of the unit disk bundle
D^{\ast}S
. In this case, the point
z\in S
is said to be the base point of
F
. By definition, a positive isotopy
\{F_{t}\}_{t}
is called a fibre wrapping if
F_{t}
is a wrapped fibre for each
t
, and
\{F_{t}\}
are uniformly cylindrical and have uniform horizontal support. In this case, the path of base points of
\{F_{t}\}
in
S
is said to be its support arc.
\Box

In order to prove
Theorem

6.3
we need a cofinal sequence that relates to minimal geodesics: intuitively, we will consider a cofinal sequence given by fibre wrapping such that the associated continuation elements map to the minimal geodesics. For that, we need to cover
T^{\ast}S
by sectorial covers, as defined in
[
GPS24a
, Section 12.5]
, and we achieve that by modifying the given metric
(S,g)
, as follows. Given the spectral network
\EuScript{W}\subset S
adapted to
(S,g)
, we consider a finite good cover
\{U_{\alpha}\}
of
{\bf S}
such that each
U_{\alpha}
contains at most one vertex of
\EuScript{W}
, and the open sets
U_{\alpha}
have their closures contained in
S
, except for a distinguished open set for each puncture of
{\bf S}
, which we choose to be contained in the region where the metric is conical. We call the open sets of the former type the “internal” open sets. Then, we choose a collar neighborhood of each
\partial U_{\alpha}\subset S
with a diffeomorphism to
S^{1}\times(0,1)
such that:

-

\partial U_{\alpha}
is mapped to
S^{1}\times\{1/2\}
,

-

\partial U_{\eta}
,
\eta\neq\alpha
, and the edges of
\EuScript{W}
intersect the neighborhood
S^{1}\times(0,1)
of
\partial U_{\alpha}
as an arc of the form
\{p\}\times(0,1)
,
p\in S^{1}
a point.

With this cover chosen, we modify the metric
(S,g)
near each such collar of
\partial U_{\alpha}
so it is of the form
g_{S^{1}}+dt\otimes dt
, where
g_{S^{1}}
is the standard round metric on
S^{1}
. This can be achieved by an arbitrarily
C^{0}
-small compactly supported perturbation of
g
in a manner that
\EuScript{W}\subset S
stays invariant, up to smooth isotopies supported away from the boundaries of these disks
U_{\alpha}
. Note that for two
\EuScript{W}
-adapted points
z,w\in\EuScript{W}^{c}
, the length of the second shortest geodesic will be bounded below by the minimal injectivity radius
\mbox{inj}(g)
of this perturbed metric. For the rest of this section, we assume the metric
(S,g)
has been modified as just described, and still denote this perturbed metric by
(S,g)
.

The sequence of fibre wrappings that we use to prove
Theorem

6.3
, whose continuation elements limit to the minimal geodesics, is constructed in (the proof of) the following result:

Proposition 6.6

(Fibre wrappings and minimal geodesics)
.

Let
F_{z},F_{w}\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
be wrapped fibres based at
z,w\in S
, two close enough generic points.Then, there exists a sequence of fibre wrappings

F_{z}\rightsquigarrow F_{1}\rightsquigarrow F_{2}\rightsquigarrow\ldots

with the base point of
F_{1}
equal to
z
such that:

(1)

\displaystyle HW^{\ast}(F_{z},F_{w})\cong\lim_{i\to\infty}HF(F_{i},F_{w}),

(2)

the image in
HW^{0}(F_{z},F_{w})
of the continuation elements in
HF^{0}(F_{i},F_{w})
is the class
[\alpha_{zw}]\in C_{0}(\Omega_{z,w})
represented by the minimal geodesic
\alpha_{zw}
between
z,w
.

Proposition

6.6
.(2) is the part that requires a new type of argument, whereas
Proposition

6.6
.(1) can be reasonably obtained by modified existing arguments in the literature, as the proof will show, cf. Lemma
6.8
and
6.9
below.

6.2.2.
Proof of
Proposition

6.6

First, we need to introduce the following notion of cofinality:

Definition 6.7
.

Let
P_{t}\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
be a positive isotopy of cylindrical Lagrangians,
t\in[0,\infty)
, and
\{\bf\overline{S}_{t}\}
a nested family of truncations of
S
such that
P_{t}\subset\bf\overline{S}_{t}
and
\cup\bf\overline{S}_{t}=S
. By definition, a such a pair
(P_{t},\bf\overline{S}_{t})
is said to be ind-cofinal if:

(1)

For any isotopy
P_{0}\to P^{w}
, there exists some
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and a positive isotopy
P^{w}\rightsquigarrow P_{t}
such that the composition of
P_{0}\to P^{w}
with
P^{w}\rightsquigarrow P_{t}
is homotopic to a positive isotopy
P_{0}\rightsquigarrow P_{t}
. Furthermore, both the isotopy and the homotopy can be taken to be supported in the interior of
T^{\ast}\bf\overline{S}_{t}
.

(2)

Any two positive isotopies
P_{0}\rightsquigarrow P_{t}
coincide, up to homotopy, after composing with
P_{t}\to P_{s}
, for some large
s>t
. Furthermore, the homotopy (of positive isotopies) can be taken to be supported in the interior of
T^{\ast}\bf\overline{S}_{s}
.

A positive isotopy
P_{t}
is said to be ind-cofinal if there exists
\{\bf\overline{S}_{t}\}
such that
(P_{t},\bf\overline{S}_{t})
is ind-cofinal.
\Box

As in the non-limit case, the ind-cofinality in
Definition

6.7
of a positive isotopy
P_{t}
can be guaranteed by forcing the boundaries
\partial_{\infty}P_{t}
to move fast enough in a Reeb direction in the ideal contact boundary of
(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
. In precise terms:

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
P_{t}\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
be a positive isotopy. Then,
P_{t}
is ind-cofinal if there exists a contact form
\alpha
on the unit sphere bundle, coinciding with the restriction of
\lambda_{st}
outside a compact set, such that

(34)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\inf_{\partial_{\infty}P_{t}}\alpha(\partial_{t}(\partial_{\infty}P_{t}))dt=\infty.

In particular, the time-
t
the geodesic flow at infinity applied to a Lagrangian
P\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
gives an ind-cofinal family.

Proof.

It suffices to modify
[
GPS20
, Lemma 3.29]
. Since the metric is conical at infinity, the associated geodesic flow is complete. By the hypothesis
Eq.

34
, we can reparametrize the family
P_{t}
in the
t
-variable such that
\alpha_{st}(\partial_{t}(\partial_{\infty}P_{t}))\geq 2
,
\alpha_{\text{st}}
being the restriction of
\lambda_{\text{st}}
to the ideal contact boundary, and the horizontal support of
P_{t}
is contained in
\bf\overline{S}{}_{t}=U_{r>f(t)}
25
25

25

Here
r
is the one over the distance to the puncture, so puncture corresponds to
r=\infty
.
for some function
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
f(t)>3t
.

Since
\alpha_{st}(\partial_{t}(\partial_{\infty}P_{t}))\geq 2
, then

P^{0}\rightsquigarrow\phi_{-t}(P^{t})

is a positive isotopy because
\alpha_{st}(\partial_{t}(\partial_{\infty}(\phi_{-t}P_{t})))\geq 1
, where
\phi_{t}
is the geodesic flow. Independently, we can use the geodesic flow on the inverse isotopy
P^{w}\to P^{0}
to also make it positive and, since the metric is conical at infinity, we can ensure
\phi_{t}(T^{\ast}\bf\overline{S}_{s})\subset\bf\overline{S}_{s+t}
. Now that
P^{w}\to\phi_{t}(P^{0})
has been made positive, note that

\phi_{t}(P^{0})\rightsquigarrow(\phi_{t}\circ\phi_{-t})(P^{t})=P^{t}

is positive because
P^{0}\rightsquigarrow\phi_{-t}(P^{t})
is positive. This establishes property
Definition

6.7
.(1). For
Definition

6.7
.(2) the argument is the same as GPS, with the truncation being preserved.
∎

Lemma

6.8
gives an ind-cofinal sequence, as in
Definition

6.7
, and the first part
Proposition

6.6
.(1) is then a consequence of the following lemma:

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
P
and
K
be Lagrangians in
T^{\ast}S
with compact horizontal support. Let
\{P_{j}\}
be an ind-cofinal sequence with
P_{0}=P
, then

HW(P,K)=\lim_{j\to\infty}HF(P_{j},K).

Proof.

The sequence
P_{i}
regarded as a decorated poset is cofinal in the sub-poset of the final decorated poset
P_{final}
given by those
P_{p}
that belong to the wrapping category of
P_{0}
, c.f.
[
GPS20
, Section 3.4]
. Therefore, the natural map
HF(P_{j},K)\to HW(P,K)
is an isomorphism in the limit.
∎

Let us now focus on establishing
Proposition

6.6
.(2). The difficulty here is naming the necessary geometric objects in a homotopically coherent manner. To wit, we must be able to describe the geodesic generator
\alpha_{z,w}
within the framework of sectorial coverings of
[
GPS20
]
, which is itself a highly non-explicit setting. For that, consider the finite good cover from
Section

6.2.1
and the correspondingly perturbed metric
(S,g)
. Then the Liouville sectors
\{T^{\ast}\overline{U}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha}
form a sectorial covering of
T^{\ast}\bf\overline{S}
26
26

26

Using the product decomposition near the boundary, we obtain the projection structure

(35)

\displaystyle Nbh^{Z}(\partial T^{\ast}(U_{\alpha}))\simeq T^{\ast}(\partial U_{\alpha})\times T^{\ast}[0.5,1)

where we identify
T^{\ast}[0.5,1)\simeq\mathbb{C}_{0.5\leq Re(z)<1}
. Unlike the setting in
[
GPS20
]
, here we equip
\mathbb{C}
with the Liouville structure obtained from
\mathbb{C}\simeq T^{\ast}\mathbb{R}
. The argument
[
GPS20
, Lemma 2.41]
that prevents pseudo-holomorphic curves from escaping off the boundary applies for almost complex structures on
\mathbb{C}
obtained from the conically deformed Sasaki metric on
T^{\ast}{\mathbb{R}}
coming from the Euclidean metic. Therefore, we may impose the constraint that the almost complex structure on
T^{\ast}U_{\alpha}
coincides with
J_{cyl}
near the boundary.
and we can consider the associated wrapped Fukaya categories
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}\overline{U_{\alpha}})
on each of these sectors. In this particular case, using the argument similar to
[
GPS24
, Section 5.6]
, we can directly compute wrapped chain complexes as follows. For an internal open set
U_{\alpha}
, and each
z\in U_{\alpha}
, we consider a cofinal fibre wrapping
F_{z,t}
of
T^{\ast}_{z}S
with the support arc given by
z
, such that for
t
large enough, the ideal boundary of
F_{z,t}
is given by some inward push-off of the conormal of
\partial U_{\alpha}
. These
F_{z,t}
give an explicit description of
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}\overline{U_{\alpha}})
since

HF^{\ast}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,T^{\ast}_{z^{\prime}}S)=HF^{0}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,T^{\ast}_{z^{\prime}}S)\cong\mathbb{Z}[\gamma_{zz^{\prime}}],\quad\forall z,z^{\prime}\in U_{\alpha}.

In particular, we can identify
HF^{\ast}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,T^{\ast}_{z^{\prime}}S)
with the unique path homotopy class in
\pi_{1}(U_{\alpha};z,z^{\prime})
. Furthermore, if
z,z^{\prime},z^{\prime\prime}\in U_{\alpha}
, then the
A_{\infty}
-structure map is

\mu_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}HF^{\ast}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,T^{\ast}_{z^{\prime}}S)\otimes H^{\ast}(T^{\ast}_{z^{\prime}}S,T^{\ast}_{z^{\prime\prime}}S)\to H^{\ast}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,T^{\ast}_{z^{\prime\prime}}S),\quad\mu_{2}([\gamma_{zz^{\prime}}],[\gamma_{z^{\prime}z^{\prime\prime}}])=[\gamma_{zz^{\prime\prime}}].

Note also that we have inclusion functors
(i_{\alpha})_{\ast}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}\overline{U_{\alpha}})\to\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
and
[
GPS24a
, Theorem 1.35]
implies that the natural functor

(36)

\displaystyle\mbox{hocolim}(i_{\alpha})_{\ast}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{hocolim}_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}}\mathfrak{W}\big(T^{\ast}U_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{k}}\big)\to\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)

is a pre-triangulated equivalence, where
U_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=U_{\alpha_{1}}\cap\ldots\cap U_{\alpha_{k}}
and the homotopy category of the homotopy colimit is naturally identified with the fundamental groupoid of
S
.

Proof of
Proposition

6.6
.

As said above, Part (1) follows from
Lemma

6.8
. For Part (2), let
x,y\in U_{\alpha}
such that
y
is within the distance
\frac{1}{20}\mbox{inj}(g)
from
x
. By the prior discussion, we have a generator
[\gamma_{xy}]
that represents the minimal geodesic in the homotopy colimit
36
. We must now compute the image of
[\gamma_{xy}]
under the functor
\mbox{hocolim}(i_{\alpha})_{\ast}
and show that, for careful choices, the minimal geodesic generator indeed gets preserved. First, we claim that there are fibre wrappings
\{F_{k}\}_{k}
of
T^{\ast}_{x}S
such that

(i)

the generators of
CF(F_{k},T^{\ast}_{y}S)
correspond to geodesics between
T^{\ast}_{x}S
and
T^{\ast}_{y}S
,

(ii)

the wrapping continuation map
CF(F_{k},T^{\ast}_{y}S)\to CF(F_{k+1},T^{\ast}_{y}S)
preserves the minimal geodesic element.

Intuitively, these can be obtained by considering globally positive Hamiltonians
H_{k}
that are linear at infinity but quadratic in a large compact part of the cotangent bundle and using them to flow
T^{\ast}_{x}S
. Part (2) follows from the existence of such fibre wrappings, so let us prove the claim, as follows.

Let
\{\ell_{i}\}\to+\infty
be an increasing sequence of positive real numbers, with
\ell_{1}>2
, such that no
\ell_{i}
is in the length spectrum of geodesics between
x
and
y
and the minimal geodesic is the only geodesic with length less than
\ell_{1}
. (This is possible because we normalized so that
\mbox{inj}(g)>2
.) For each
k\geq 0
, consider
\delta_{k}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
[\ell_{k}-\delta_{k},\ell_{k}+\delta_{k}]
is disjoint from the length spectrum and, for each
\delta_{k}
, choose a smooth increasing positive function

H_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[1,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that

-

H^{\prime}_{k}(r)=0
on
[1,1+\frac{1}{80}]
,

-

H_{k}(r)=\frac{1}{2}r^{2}
on
r\in[1+\frac{1}{40},\ell_{k}-\delta_{k}]
,

-

H_{k}(r)=\ell_{k}\cdot r
for
r\in[\ell_{k},\infty)
.

-

With respect to
k
,
H_{k}
is fixed on
[1,\ell_{1}]
and the derivatives of
H_{k}
form an increasing sequence of functions.

These Hamiltonians
H_{k}
are linear at infinity and quadratic on large compact part. We then declare the sequence of fibre wrappings
\{F_{k}\}
to be time-
1
image of
T^{\ast}_{x}S
under the Hamiltonian flow of
H_{k}
. The family
\{F_{k}\}
is cofinal because under a (large) compactly supported deformation, it becomes the linear Hamiltonian
\ell_{k}r
and thus the cofinality criterion
Lemma

6.8
applies. Let us argue that it satisfies
(i)
and
(ii)
.

By the observation in
[
Sei08
, Section 3(c)]
, the Hamiltonian chords of
H_{k}
between
T^{\ast}_{x}S
and
T^{\ast}_{y}S
are of the form
(r,x(\ell s))
, where
x
is the sphere bundle lift of a unit-speed geodesic,
\ell
is its length, and
r>0
is such that
H_{k}^{\prime}(r)=\ell
. By our condition
\mbox{inj}(g)>2
, given a non-minimal geodesic of length
\ell
we must have
\ell>1
. Since the interval
[\ell_{k}-\delta_{k},\ell_{k}+\delta_{k}]
is disjoint to the length spectrum, the Hamiltonian chords lie on the interval where
H_{k}
is quadratic and are thus in one-to-one correspondence with non-minimal geodesics of length bounded above by
\ell_{k}
. Furthermore, there is a unique Hamiltonian chord
[\gamma_{xy}]
representing the minimal geodesic in the region
[1,\ell_{1}]
. This concludes
(i)
. For
(ii)
, consider the continuation map
CF(F_{k},T^{\ast}_{y}S)\to CF(F_{k+1},T^{\ast}_{y}S)
. Since
H_{k}
is fixed on
[1,\ell_{1}]
, the minimal geodesic element gives a constant continuation strip: let us argue that this is the only continuation strip. Indeed, let
u
be a homogeneous continuation strip for
CF(F_{k},T^{\ast}_{y}S)\to CF(F_{k+1},T^{\ast}_{y}S)
mapping a geodesic
x^{-}
of length
\ell
to the minimal geodesic
\gamma
. By the geometric energy formula and global positivity of the Hamiltonians, we must have
A(x^{-})\geq A(\gamma)
. In contrast,
[
Sei08
, Section 3(c), Eq.(3.12)]
implies that the action of the first non-minimal geodesic is
-\frac{1}{2}\ell^{2}
, so
A(x^{-})=-L^{2}/2
, and we had
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert A(\gamma)\right\rvert}<L^{2}/2
. This is a contradiction and thus the only strip must be constant, hence the minimal geodesic
[\gamma_{xy}]
is preserved under wrappings.
∎

6.2.3.
Continuation maps for fibre wrappings

In order to continue our proof of
Theorem

6.3
, we need to compute
\mu_{1|1}(c,\cdot)
, where
c
is a continuation element. To employ the family Floer techniques from
Section

5.2
, we use the following facts to relate the maps
\mu_{1|1}(c,\cdot)
with continuation maps (c.f
[
GPS20
, Lemma 3.26]
). First, an analogue of
Theorem

4.2
for fibre wrappings:

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
F
be a wrapped fibre whose base-point is contained in the complement of
\EuScript{W}
. Then there exists
\varepsilon_{0}(F)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that there are no non-constant
J_{cyl}
-holomorphic strips between
F
and
\varepsilon L
if
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}(F))
.

Proof.

Let
z
be the base-point of
F
, then since
F=T^{\ast}_{z}S
on
D_{1}^{\ast}S
, the action of the intersection points are of size
O(\varepsilon)
. Therefore, monotonicity tells us that the
J_{cyl}
-holomorphic strips cannot escape
D_{1}^{\ast}S
.
∎

The analogue of
Proposition

5.30
for fibre wrappings reads as follows. For brevity, we suppress the discussion on the necessity to make the right choice of the elongation functions.

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
\{F_{t}\}
,
t\in[0,1]
, be a fibre wrapping and
\EuScript{W}\subset S
a spectral network.

(1)

If the support arc of
\{F_{t}\}
lies outside
\EuScript{W}
, then there exists a family
J(s,t)
of almost complex structures such that the moduli space of
(F_{t},L)
-continuation strips is cobordant to the moduli space of trivial strips.

(2)

If the support arc of
\{F_{t}\}
intersects
\EuScript{W}
at an
ij
-wall, then there exists a family
J(s,t)
of almost complex structures such that the moduli space of
(F_{t},L)
-continuation strips that do not travel from the
j
th sheet to the
i
th sheet is cobordant to that of trivial strips.

Proof.

Proposition

5.30
implies that there exists
\varepsilon_{0}(\{F_{t}\})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}(\{F_{t}\}))
there are no non-constant
J_{cyl}
-holomorhic strips between
\varepsilon L
and
F_{t}
. We can then apply the Gromov compactness argument as in the proof of
Proposition

5.26
to conclude that the moduli space of continuation strips is cobordant to the moduli of trivial strips.
∎

Since the scaling parameter
\varepsilon
in
Lemma

6.10
only depends on the base point, a consequence of
Proposition

6.11
is that wrappings at infinity do not affect
L
. That is,
if
F\rightsquigarrow F^{\prime}
is a fibre wrapping with the same base point, then the continuation map
CF(F,L)\to CF(F^{\prime},L)
is the identity map. This is an analogue of the invariance statement in
Proposition

5.12
.

6.2.4.
Concluding
Theorem

6.3
and
Corollary

6.4

For
Theorem

6.3
, it suffices to show that
\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}
is the the non-abelianization module, as a module over the cohomology subcategory generated by the wrapped fibres, which we identify with the fundamental groupoid. For that, note that given any
\EuScript{W}
-adapted minimal geodesic,
Proposition

6.6
and
Proposition

6.11
guarantee the upper-triangular form of
\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}([\alpha_{zw}])
. Then the same argument as in the proof of
Theorem

5.24
and the wall-crossing formula imply that the parallel transport map must be equal to that of
\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}(V)
.

For
Corollary

6.4
,
Lemma

5.13
implies that
L
is Maslov
0
and thus the chain complex
\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}(F)
for a wrapping fibre
F
must be cohomologically concentrated in degree zero. By homological perturbation, comparing to the non-abelianization modules, we can perturb
\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}
to be defined over the subcategory of the wrapping fibres. Since the latter subcategory generate the wrapped Fukaya category, the statement follows from
Theorem

6.3
.
\Box

6.3.
Cylindrized Betti Lagrangians and spectral networks

As discussed in
Section

6.1
, a Betti Lagrangian
L\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
does
not
give an element of the wrapped Fukaya category
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
, nor its partially wrapped modification
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
. The reason is the asymptotic behavior of
L
at infinity, which is not compatible with the definitions of these categories. In
Section

6.1
, we showed that a Betti Lagrangian, endowed with
V\in\mbox{Loc}(L)
, does nevertheless give an
A_{\infty}
-module
\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}
over
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
which behaves as if it was the Yoneda module of an object in
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
, and in turn we related the
\mu_{1|1}
-maps of such
A_{\infty}
-modules to the non-abelianization framework of spectral networks, cf.
Theorem

6.3
. An alternative approach to comparing Betti Lagrangians and Fukaya categories to spectral networks is to allow ourselves to modify the Betti Lagrangian
L\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
at infinity so that in fact it defines an object in a Fukaya category. The present section develops this approach, defining such cylindrizations and showing that the non-abelianized parallel transport from spectral networks is captured by
\mu_{2}
-map of the
A_{\infty}
-structure associated to such cylindrization.

Specifically,
Section

6.3.1
explains how to deform
L
to a cylindrical Lagrangian
L_{\circ}\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
with boundary on the Legendrian links associated to the Betti surface
(S,\boldsymbol{m},{\bf\Lambda})
. Denote by
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
the partially wrapped Fukaya category of
(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
partially stopped at
\Lambda
, as constructed in
[
GPS24a
]
. By construction, the cylindrization
L_{\circ}
defines an object in
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
once endowed with a local system
V\in\mbox{Loc}(L)
. We denote by

\EuScript{Y}(L_{\circ},V)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)\to\mbox{mod}_{k}

its associated Yoneda
A_{\infty}
-module and consider its component

\mu_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}HW(T^{\ast}_{z}S,T^{\ast}_{w}S)\otimes HW(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L_{\circ})\to HW(T^{\ast}_{w}S,L_{\circ}),

where
HW(T^{\ast}_{z}S,T^{\ast}_{w}S)=\mbox{Hom}_{\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,T^{\ast}_{w}S)
are now morphisms in
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
, not
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
. That said, given a
\EuScript{W}
-adapted pair
z,w\in S
, we had the minimal geodesic
\alpha_{zw}
which defined a class in
\mbox{Hom}_{\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,T^{\ast}_{w}S)
. In order to transfer it to a class in
\mbox{Hom}_{\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)}(T^{\ast}_{z}S,T^{\ast}_{w}S)
, we consider a subset
S_{int}\subset S
such that the front projections of the Legendrian links in
{\bf\Lambda}
are all contained in
S\setminus S_{int}
and
S
retracts to
S_{int}
: we obtain
S_{int}
by removing a sufficiently large disk neighborhood of each marked point. Then there a natural inclusion functor
i_{\ast}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathfrak{W}(T^{*}S_{int})\to\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
, and we can consider the image
i_{\ast}[\alpha_{zw}]
of (the class defined by)
\alpha_{zw}
under this functor.

For the cylindrized Betti Lagrangian
L_{\circ}
, now in the context of the partially wrapped Fukaya category
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
, the relation between the
A_{\infty}
-structure and non-abelianized parallel transport for spectral networks is as follows:

Theorem 6.12
.

Let
(S,g)
be a Betti surface,
L\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
a Betti Lagrangian,
V\in\mbox{Loc}(L)
,
\EuScript{W}\subset S
an adapted spectral network and
z,w\in S
an
\EuScript{W}
-adapted pair. Then the minimal geodesic
\alpha_{zw}\in\Omega_{z,w}
satisfies

(37)

\mu_{2}(i_{\ast}[\alpha_{zw}],\cdot)=\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}(V)(\alpha_{zw})

Theorem

6.12
is momentarily proven in
Section

6.3.2
, after describing
L_{\circ}
in
Section

6.3.1
. A consequence of
Corollary

6.4
and
Theorem

6.12
is the relationship between
\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}
and
\EuScript{Y}_{(L_{\circ},V)}
, which reads as follows:

Corollary 6.13
.

There is a homotopy

\EuScript{Y}_{(L_{\circ},V)}\circ i_{\ast}\simeq\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}

of
A_{\infty}
-modules over
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
.

6.3.1.
Cylindrization of Betti Lagrangians

Let
L\subset T^{*}S
be a Betti Lagrangian on a Betti surface
(S,\boldsymbol{m},{\bf\Lambda})
, we want to modify
L
near infinity (around the punctures of
S
) to obtain a Lagrangian
L_{\circ}\subset T^{*}S
which is a Lagrangian filling of the Legendrian link in
(T^{\infty}S,\xi_{\text{st}})
defined by
\bf\Lambda
and defines an object in
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
. The cylindrization procedure is in line with the modification for weave fillings in
[
CL22
]
.

From a symplectic topological viewpoint, without taking
\EuScript{W}
into account, the cylindrization process is as follows. Choose a collection
\EuScript{S}
of circles in
S
, one circle per puncture chosen so that it encloses the puncture and is sufficiently close to it. The positive conormal lift of such collection
\EuScript{S}
is a Lagrangian submanifold that cleanly intersects the zero section of
T^{*}S
. Let
C\subset T^{*}S
be the Lagrangian surgery resolving that clean intersection, which is an exact Lagrangian that coincides with the zero section away from the punctures, and with the positive conormal lift of the circles near the punctures. Since
C\subset T^{*}S
is Lagrangian and cylindrical at infinity, there exists a Weinstein neighborhood
i_{cyl}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}D^{*}C\to T^{*}S
for a small enough disk bundle
D^{*}C
, which coincides with the disk bundle of
T^{*}S
away from the punctures. Since
C
is diffeomorphic to
S
, the Betti Lagrangian
L\subset T^{*}S
can be identified with a homonymous Lagrangian
L\subset T^{*}C
which can be assumed to belong to
D^{*}C
.

Definition 6.14
.

The cylindrization
L_{\circ}
of
L
is defined to be
i_{cyl}(L)\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
.
\Box

To incorporate compatibility with a given spectral network
\EuScript{W}
for
L
, we require that the circles in
\EuScript{S}
are chosen enclosing each puncture close enough such that:

(1)

\EuScript{W}
is transverse to the circles,

(2)

each circle is contained in the trapping neighborhood, cf.
Lemma

2.26
, for the corresponding puncture,

(3)

no vertex of
\EuScript{W}
gets mapped in the cylindrical region between each circle and its corresponding puncture.

This can be ensured by choosing the circles in
\EuScript{S}
close enough to the punctures and generically. Note that
Lemma

A.2
implies that
L
is uniformly bounded with respect to
g
, and so any
(F_{z},L)
-disk for
z
away from the circles must have its image contained in a similar region, truncated away from the punctures.

Remark 6.15
.

Note that we had to scale
L
down so that
L\subset D^{*}C
lied inside the Weinstein neighborhood. This is possible while being compatible with
\EuScript{W}
because the metric is of the form
r^{2}d\theta^{2}+dr^{2}
near the punctures.
\Box

The cylindrization
L_{\circ}
in
Definition

6.14
defines an object in
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
after applying a small positive push-off at infinity. We always implicitly understand a Lagrangian filling of a Legendrian
\Lambda
in
T^{\infty}S
as giving an object in
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
in this manner.

6.3.2.
Proof of
Theorem

6.12

(
Floer
\mu_{2}
is spectral transport
)

The argument is in line with the family Floer techniques developed in
Section

5
and follows the same steps as
Section

6.2
, now suitably modified in the partially wrapped setting. Let
\bf\overline{S}(\EuScript{S})
denote the unique compact connected component of the complement in
S
of the circles in
\EuScript{S}
. First, by geometric boundedness, the following locality lemma holds:

Lemma 6.16
.

Let
F_{z}
be a wrapped fibre, with basepoint
z\in\bf\overline{S}(\EuScript{S})
, whose boundary does not intersect
\Lambda
. Then, any
(L_{\circ},F_{z})
-disk belongs to a region of the form
T^{\ast}\bf\overline{S}(\EuScript{S^{\prime}})
, for
\EuScript{S^{\prime}}
another compatible collection of circles. Similarly, the same statement holds for any small enough negative push-offs of
L_{\circ}
whose boundary stays disjoint to
\partial F_{z}
.
\Box

Lemma

6.16
implies that the remainder of the proof of
Theorem

6.12
can be the same as the proof of
Theorem

6.3
, except for the fact that the cohomology module is now defined as the direct limit of
CF(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L_{\circ}^{i})
as
i\to\infty
. We thus need to take into consideration of wrapping morphisms

CF(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L_{\circ}^{i})\to CF(T^{\ast}_{z}S^{w},L_{\circ}^{i}),\quad CF(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L_{\circ}^{i})\to CF(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L_{\circ}^{i+1}).

This does not affect the computations overall, as a combination of the argument in
Lemma

6.16
with the Gromov compactness used in
Proposition

6.11
implies the following fact:

Lemma 6.17
.

There exists a sequence of positive push-offs
\{L_{\circ}^{i}\}_{i}
,
\{F_{z}^{j}\}_{j}
such that:

(1)

For
z\in\bf\overline{S}(\EuScript{S})
and for
i-1>j
, the continuation maps

CF_{\Lambda}(F^{j}_{z},L_{\circ}^{i})\to CF_{\Lambda}(F^{j+1}_{z},L_{\circ}^{i}),\quad CF(F^{j}_{z},L_{\circ}^{i})\to CF_{\Lambda}(F^{j}_{z},\,L_{\circ}^{i+1})

are the identity map.

(2)

\displaystyle HF_{\Lambda}(F_{z},L)=\lim_{j\to\infty}HF(F^{j}_{z},L)=\lim_{i\to\infty}HF(F_{z},L^{i})

Here we denoted
CF_{\Lambda}
when morphisms are in
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
, i.e. partially stopped at
\Lambda
.
\Box

The remaining ingredient for the proof of
Theorem

6.12
is a description of the image morphisms
i_{\ast}(HW(T^{\ast}_{x}S,T^{\ast}_{y}S))
under the inclusion
i_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)\to\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
. It is provided by the following proposition:

Proposition 6.18
.

Let
x,y\in\bf\overline{S}(\EuScript{S})
be two generic points at distance less than
\frac{1}{20}\mbox{inj}(g)
. Then the inclusion map

i_{\ast}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}HF^{\ast}(T^{\ast}_{x}S,T^{\ast}_{y}S)\to HF^{\ast}_{\Lambda}(T^{\ast}_{x}S,T^{\ast}_{y}S)

is an isomorphism. In fact, for
[\alpha_{xy}]
the representative of the minimal geodesic,
i_{\ast}[\alpha_{xy}]=[\alpha_{xy}]
.

Proof.

In brief the core of the proof is using an action filtration argument combined with the fact that, since the metric is conical near the punctures, geodesics in the truncated region
\bf\overline{S}(\EuScript{S})
that enter the neighborhood of the punctures cannot leave.
27
27

27

Also, there is no conceptual importance to the factor
1/20
: it is just there for action estimates.
Let us provide the details of the argument.

First, we must construct a contact form
\alpha
for
(T^{\infty}S,\xi_{\text{st}})
whose Reeb chords correspond to geodesics between
x
and
y
while being stopped at
\Lambda
, so as to be consistent with the partial wrapping in
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
. In precise terms, we claim that there exists a contact 1-form
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(T^{\infty}S)
for
\xi_{\text{st}}=\ker\alpha
such that:

(i)

The Reeb flow of
\alpha
is complete,

(ii)

\alpha=\lambda_{st}|_{T^{\infty}S}
outside a small standard neighborhood of
\Lambda
,

(iii)

\alpha
-Reeb chords between
\partial T^{\ast}_{x}S
and
\partial T^{\ast}_{y}S
are in bijection with
\lambda_{st}
-chords from
\partial T^{\ast}_{x}S
to
\partial T^{\ast}_{y}S
.

The neighborhood in
(ii)
is referred to as the stop region of
\alpha
. Let us argue that such
\alpha
exists, as follows. By construction,
\Lambda
lies in a small neighborhood of the positive conormal of the circles in
\EuScript{S}
. In such a neighborhood, the geodesic flow for
(S,g)
points outward, transverse to the boundary of
\bf\overline{S}(\EuScript{S})
. By
[
GPS20
, Lemma 3.29]
, there exists a contact form satisfying
(i)
,
(ii)
with the stop region lying in this neighborhood. For this choice of neighborhood,
(iii)
also holds. Indeed, an
\alpha
-Reeb chord from
x
to
y
is a geodesic chord away from the stop region. Now, on the one hand, if it entered such neighborhood of
\Lambda
, the geodesic must enter the boundary in the transverse direction and then the conical metric structure implies that the original geodesic could not have returned to
y
. On the other hand, for the
\alpha
-Reeb chord to return to
y
, it must leave the stopping region but the only way the chord can leave it is by exiting the neighborhood of the positive conormal of
\EuScript{S}
. In that case the geodesic flow would further send the chord off to infinity. Therefore, geodesics between
x
and
y
could not have entered such neighborhood of the stop boundary and no
\alpha
-Reeb chord could have done the same. Thus
(iii)
holds and we can choose a contact form
\alpha
with the properties above.

Second, we proceed with an action filtration argument, in line with
Proposition

6.6
. By our choice of contact form above, the
\alpha
-Reeb chords are in bijection with geodesics between
x
and
y
. By genericity of
x,y
, we can assume that the length of the geodesics are all distinct, the energy functional on the path space is Morse, and all the geodesics stay outside the stop collar region. We now consider two variations on the family of Hamiltonians from the proof of
Proposition

6.6
, one for
CF^{\ast}
and one for
CF^{\ast}_{\Lambda}
.

(1) For
CF^{\ast}
, the wrapped case with no stop at
\Lambda
, let
h_{\bf\overline{S}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}T^{\infty}\bf\overline{S}(\EuScript{S})\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth, non-negative function on the unit sphere bundle of
\bf\overline{S}(\EuScript{S})
with
\nabla h_{st}
supported on the stop region, such that
h_{\bf\overline{S}}\lambda_{\text{st}}|_{T^{\infty}S}
has complete Reeb flow. Let
H_{n},\ell_{n}
be as in the proof of
Proposition

6.6
, consider the linear-at-infinity Hamiltonians
h_{\bf\overline{S}}\cdot H_{n}
and let
F_{n}
denote the time
1
-image of
T^{\ast}_{y}S
under the corresponding Hamiltonian flows. The Hamiltonian chords of
h_{\bf\overline{S}}\cdot H_{n}
all lie outside the stop region, and so the argument in
Proposition

6.6
implies that the Hamiltonian chords correspond to non-minimal geodesic of length
\ell
less than
\ell_{n}
, and their actions are given by
-\ell^{2}/2
.

(2) For
CF^{\ast}_{\Lambda}
, write the contact form
\alpha
above as
\alpha=h_{stop}\lambda_{\text{st}}
and let
F^{stop}_{n}
be the time-
1
image of
T^{\ast}_{x}S
using the quadratic Hamiltonians
h^{stop}\cdot H_{n}
. By construction, the inequality
h_{\bf\overline{S}}\leq h_{stop}
holds and so there is a positive isotopy
F_{n}\rightsquigarrow F_{n}^{stop}
and an induced continuation map
CF^{\ast}(F_{n},T^{\ast}_{y}S)\to CF^{\ast}(F_{n}^{stop},T^{\ast}_{y}S)
. By Property
(iii)
above, these two groups coincide as vector spaces. Since the actions are given by
-\ell^{2}/2
, and the length spectrum is discrete, we can consider a basis
[\gamma_{1}],[\gamma_{2}],\ldots,[\gamma_{k}]
, by ordering by length the geodesics of length bounded by
\ell_{n}
.

The last step is to argue that the continuation map is upper-triangular with respect to the basis
[\gamma_{1}],[\gamma_{2}],\ldots,[\gamma_{k}]
. Indeed, let
[\gamma_{i}]
be a generator of
CF^{\ast}(F_{n},T^{\ast}_{y}S)
and consider the image
\sum n_{j}[\gamma_{j}]
under the continuation map. By the geometric energy formula, we have

A(\gamma_{i})\leq A(\gamma_{j})
if
i\leq j
and a continuation strip from
\gamma_{i}
to itself must be constant. Therefore, the action formula implies that this image must be of the form
\displaystyle[\gamma_{i}]+\sum_{j<i}n_{ij}[\gamma_{j}]
. Thus the continuation map
CF(F_{n},T^{\ast}_{y}S)\to CF(F_{n}^{stop},T^{\ast}_{y}S)
is upper-triangular, with identity on the diagonal, and so it must be a quasi-isomorphism. Furthermore, the following diagram commutes, up to chain homotopy:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{CF(F_{n+1},T^{\ast}_{y}S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{CF(F^{stop}_{n+1},T^{\ast}_{y}S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{CF(F_{n},T^{\ast}_{y}S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{CF(F^{stop}_{n},T^{\ast}_{y}S)}.}

In consequence, the induced map
WF(T^{\ast}_{x}S,T^{\ast}_{y}S)\to WF_{\Lambda}(T^{\ast}_{x}S,T^{\ast}_{y}S)
must also be a quasi-isomorphism. Finally, the minimal geodesic generator is preserved under these maps by arguing as in the proof of
Proposition

6.6
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

6.12
.

By
Proposition

6.18
,
i_{\ast}
is cohomologically fully faithful, and the minimal geodesic elements are preserved under the inclusion functor. Consider the cohomology module of
\EuScript{Y}(L_{\circ},V)
over
i_{\ast}
restricted to the subcategory given by the cotangent fibres which is istelf identified with the fundamental groupoid. The cohomology module
\EuScript{Y}(L_{\circ},V)
with the parallel transport morphisms is defined by the direct limit

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}CF(T^{\ast}_{z}S,L^{i}).

By
Lemma

6.17
, such wrappings induce identity maps on the Floer chain complexes
CF_{\Lambda}(F_{z}^{j},L^{i})
along arcs away from the spectral network
\EuScript{W}
. So the local system given by (the cohomology module of)
\EuScript{Y}(L_{\circ},V)
has trivial parallel transport over arcs that do not cross
\EuScript{W}
. The same argument as in
Proposition

6.11
.(2) implies that the associated parallel along an arc crossing
\EuScript{W}
must be as in the wall-crossing formula for non-abelianized local systems. The statement then follows by applying the argument used for
Theorem

6.3
.
∎

Proof of
Corollary

6.13
.

By
Theorem

6.12
,
\EuScript{Y}(L_{\circ},V)\circ i_{\ast}\simeq\EuScript{Y}(L)
over the cohomology subcategory of cotangent fibres. Since the inclusion functor
i_{\ast}
is cohomologically fully faithful, and the cotangent fibres generate
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
, the statement follows from the same argument as the proofs of
Theorem

6.3
and
Corollary

6.4
.
∎

6.4.
Wrapping up the description of
\EuScript{Y}_{(L_{\circ},V)}

In
Section

6.3
we introduced the Yoneda
A_{\infty}
-module
\EuScript{Y}_{(L_{\circ},V)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)\longrightarrow\mbox{mod}_{k}
associated to the cylindrization
L_{\circ}\subset(T^{*}S,\lambda_{\text{st}})
of a Betti Lagrangian
L
. In a nutshell,
Theorem

6.12
and
Corollary

6.13
give a description of this
A_{\infty}
-module on the
A_{\infty}
-subcategory given by the image of the functor
i_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathfrak{W}(T^{*}S_{int})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
, which is generated by the internal cotangent fiber. The goal of this section is to complete the description of the
A_{\infty}
-module
\EuScript{Y}_{(L_{\circ},V)}
on
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
. In order to describe the whole of
\EuScript{Y}_{(L_{\circ},V)}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)\longrightarrow\mbox{mod}_{k}
, we construct in
Proposition

6.19
a set of generators of
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
, containing the internal cotangent fiber, where we can compute the values of
\EuScript{Y}_{(L_{\circ},V)}
.

6.4.1.
Generation by interior and infinity fibers

Let
(S,\boldsymbol{m},{\bf\Lambda})
be a Betti surface, and denote by
\Lambda_{i}\subset(T^{\infty}S,\xi_{\text{st}})
the Legendrian link associated to the marked point
m_{i}\in\boldsymbol{m}
. For simplicity, we shall henceforth assume that
\Lambda_{i}
has finitely many Reeb chords, each at a different angle, and that it is Reeb-positive (for instance, in the case of Stokes Legendrians without multiplicities
Proposition

2.25
, or the positive braid links described in
Remark

2.2
). This includes many of the most important classes, including the case where each Legendrian in
{\bf\Lambda}
is isotopic to the cylindrical closure of a positive braid. We consider the following Lagrangians in
T^{*}S
, all of which yield objects in
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
once appropriately decorated and partially wrapped:

(1)

An internal cotangent fiber
T^{\ast}_{z}S
, for some point
z\in S
,

(2)

The cotangent fiber
F_{m_{i}}
near the puncture corresponding to
m_{i}
. It is given by the cotangent fiber at any point in
S
arbitrarily close to the marked point
m_{i}\in{\bf S}
such that the geodesic between that point and
m_{i}
does not intersect
\Lambda_{i}
, i.e. it is the cotangent fiber at infinity of
m_{i}
,
past
the Legendrian link
\Lambda_{i}
. We denote
F_{\boldsymbol{m}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{F_{1},\ldots,F_{|{\boldsymbol{m}}|}\}
the set of such fibers at the punctured infinity of
S
.

(3)

The collection of linking disks
\mathbb{D}_{i}
associated to the Legendrian
\Lambda_{i}
. That is, each
\mathbb{D}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{D_{i,1},\ldots,D_{i,c_{i}}\}
is the disjoint union of the linking disks of
\Lambda_{i}
, as in
[
GPS24a
, Section 5.3]
,
c_{i}=|\pi_{0}(\Lambda_{i})|
, one for each component of the Legendrian link
\Lambda_{i}
. See Figure
14
.

Figure 14
.
A front for the Legendrian
\Lambda_{i}
: in this picture we assume
\Lambda_{i}
has three components, one per strand. The three linking disks
D_{i,1},D_{i,2}
and
D_{i,3}
for
\Lambda_{i}
are depicted, with the short Reeb chords between them highlighted in purple. The large linking disk
\EuScript{D}
, homologically obtained from coning the small linking disks along the short Reeb chords, is depicted in orange to their right.

By
[
GPS24a
, Theorem 1.14]
, the set of Lagrangians
\{T^{\ast}_{z}S,\mathbb{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{D}_{|\boldsymbol{m}|}\}
generates
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
. By
Section

6.3
, the spectral network
\EuScript{W}
allows us to describe
\EuScript{Y}_{(L_{\circ},V)}
at the generator
T^{\ast}_{z}S
, by choosing
z\in\EuScript{W}^{c}
and using that the inclusion induces a fully faithful functor
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)\to\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
. The value of
\EuScript{Y}_{(L_{\circ},V)}
at the linking disks in
\mathbb{D}_{i}
is

\EuScript{Y}_{(L_{\circ},V)}(D_{i})=\mathbb{Z}.

Indeed, since
\partial^{\infty}L_{\circ}=\Lambda
,
D_{i}
intersects
L_{\circ}
once. Since the same holds for a small enough positive push-off
L_{\circ}^{+}
at the puncture
m_{i}
,
CF^{\ast}(D_{i},L_{\circ}^{+})=\mathbb{Z}
. Nevertheless, the value of
\EuScript{Y}_{(L,V)}
on morphisms
\mbox{Hom}_{\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)}(D_{i,j},D_{i,k})
between such linking disks, even for
j=k
, contains subtler information, encoding augmentations of Reeb chords at infinity which, in terms of
\EuScript{W}
, correspond to augmented
D_{4}^{-}
-trees.
28
28

28

These morphism groups between linking disks can themselves be difficult to describe.
In contrast,
\EuScript{Y}_{(L_{\circ},V)}
vanishes at the fibers
F_{\boldsymbol{m}}
at infinity. Therefore, a description of
\EuScript{Y}_{(L_{\circ},V)}
will follow if we can prove that
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
is generated by
\{T^{\ast}_{z}S,F_{\boldsymbol{m}}\}
. This is the content of the following Floer-theoretical result, which is independent of spectral networks:

Proposition 6.19

(Generation by internal and infinity cotangent fibers)
.

Let
(S,\boldsymbol{m},{\bf\Lambda})
be a Betti surface,
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
the associated partially wrapped Fukaya category,
L\subset(T^{*}S,\omega_{\text{st}})
a Betti Lagrangian and
V\in\mbox{Loc}(L)
. Then
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
is generated by
\{T^{\ast}_{z}S,F_{\boldsymbol{m}}\}
.

Proof.

Let
T^{\ast}_{z}S\subset T^{*}S
be an internal cotangent fiber and choose a puncture
m\in\boldsymbol{m}
with its associated fiber
F_{m}
and Legendrian link
\Lambda_{m}
. Denote by
\mathbb{D}_{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{D_{p_{1}},\ldots,D_{p_{n}}\}
a collection of linking disks chosen such that
D_{p_{i}}
locally links
\Lambda_{m}
at the point
p_{i}\in\Lambda_{m}
and the ordered points
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\in\Lambda_{m}
are located one per each strand of
\Lambda_{m}
and all in the same angle.
29
29

29

We can and do assume that there is no Reeb chord at that angle.
Consider the large linking disk
\EuScript{D}_{m}
associated to
\Lambda_{m}
, which is defined as in Figure
14
. The wrapping exact triangle in
[
GPS24a
, Theorem 1.10]
gives the exact triangle

(38)

T^{\ast}_{z}S\to F_{m}\to\EuScript{D}_{m}

in
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
.
Eq.

38
implies that
\{T^{\ast}_{z}S,F_{\boldsymbol{m}}\}
generate the same category as
\{T^{\ast}_{z}S,\EuScript{D}_{m_{1}},\ldots,\EuScript{D}_{m_{|{\boldsymbol{m}}|}}\}
.
30
30

30

It also follows from
Eq.

38
that
\EuScript{Y}_{(L_{\circ},V)}(\EuScript{D}_{m})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.

The next step is to show that this latter collection
\{T^{\ast}_{z}S,\EuScript{D}_{m_{1}},\ldots,\EuScript{D}_{m_{|{\boldsymbol{m}}|}}\}
generates
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
, which we do by comparing the large linking disk
\EuScript{D}_{m}
to the linking disks
D_{p_{1}},\ldots,D_{p_{n}}
at
\Lambda_{m}
. This comparison is obtained by iterating the wrapping exact triangle for short Reeb chords, which gives the homological description of
\EuScript{D}_{m}
as

\EuScript{D}_{m}\cong\cone(\ldots\cone(\cone(D_{p_{1}}\to D_{p_{2}})[-1]\to D_{p_{3}})[-1])\ldots\to D_{p_{n}})[-1].

as objects in the dg-category
\mbox{Tw}(\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda))
of twisted complexes of
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
. Here the morphisms
D_{p_{i}}\to D_{p_{i+1}}
being coned are given by the short Reeb chords, see e.g. Figure
14
. In fact, by Lemma
6.21
.(1) and (2), we actually have a quasi-isomorphism

(39)

\EuScript{D}_{m}\cong D_{p_{1}}\oplus D_{p_{2}}\oplus\ldots\oplus D_{p_{n}},

again in
\mbox{Tw}(\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda))
. Indeed, this is a consequence of functoriality of mapping cones and homological perturbation, as follows. By
[
Sei08a
, Lemma 3.30]
, exact triangles are sent to exact triangles and in Section 3(e) of ibid., given a different cocycle representative with the same cohomology class, a non-canonical isomorphism is constructed between the abstract mapping cones, that are identified with Yoneda modules of mapping cones. Thus given an
A_{\infty}
-equivalence
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}CF(\EuScript{D}_{m},\EuScript{D}_{m})\to H^{0}(\EuScript{D}_{m},\EuScript{D}_{m})
which induces an isomorphism
\mbox{Tw}(f)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{Tw}(CF(\EuScript{D}_{m},\EuScript{D}_{m}))\to\mbox{Tw}(H^{0}(\EuScript{D}_{m},\EuScript{D}_{m}))
, we can directly use the functoriality of mapping cones to conclude that

\mbox{Tw}(f)(cone(\mathfrak{r}))=cone(\mbox{Tw}(f)^{1}(\mathfrak{r})),

where
\mathfrak{r}
is the short Reeb chord and use that the morphism
{\mbox{Tw}(f)}^{1}(\mathfrak{r})
vanishes.

By
[
GPS24a
, Theorem 1.14]
,
\mbox{Tw}(\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda))
is generated by the interior fiber
T^{\ast}_{z}S
and the objects
D_{p_{1}}\oplus D_{p_{2}}\oplus\ldots\oplus D_{p_{n}}
, taken over all
\Lambda_{m}
ranging over the marked points
m\in\boldsymbol{m}
. Therefore
Eq.

39
implies that the large linking disks
\EuScript{D}_{m}
, ranging over the marked points
m\in\boldsymbol{m}
, along with the interior fiber
T^{\ast}_{z}S
, also generate
\mbox{Tw}(\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda))
.
∎

Remark 6.20
.

In
Proposition

6.19
, generation is defined as having quasi-equivalent categories of modules over twisted complexes, as in e.g.
[
GPS20
,
GPS24
]
. Given our use of cones, we must work in a triangulated framework, and we use the enhancement of twisted complexes; see also
[
Sei08a
, Chapter 1]
.
\Box

Note that there is no canonical choice of grading for the linking disks
D_{p_{i}}
. By definition, we refer to the grading on
D_{p_{i}}
as the
geometric
grading if
CF^{\ast}(D_{p},L_{\circ})
is concentrated in degree
0
. For a Legendrian knot and a linking disk, this geometric grading is the grading that is invariant under contact isotopies of (the boundary of) the linking disk around the Legendrian.

Let us summarize useful properties of the linking disks. Intuitively, these follow from the fact that the surgery models in
[
BEE12
, Section 6.1]
and
[
ENS18
, Lemma 6.4]
show that the dynamics around a (punctured) handle can be taken to be the geodesic flow inside of the handle and the given Reeb flow outside. In more detail:

Lemma 6.21

(Properties of linking disks)
.

In the notation above, the following properties hold:

(1)

(Locality) Let
m_{1},m_{2}
be two distinct punctures with Legendrian links
\Lambda_{m_{1}},\Lambda_{m_{2}}
and
D_{1},D_{2}
corresponding linking disks. Then
HW^{\ast}(D_{1},D_{2})\cong 0
.

(2)

Let
\mathbb{D}
be a collection of linking disks for the Legendrian links
{\bf\Lambda}
, each equipped with the geometric grading. Then its endomorphism algebra
HW^{\ast}(D,D)
is concentrated in non-negative degree and, if links are Reeb-positive, then it is concentrated in the zero degree.

(3)

Let
D_{p_{i}},D_{p_{i+1}}
be two linking disks at the same puncture, as above. Then the short Reeb chords
D_{p_{i}}\to D_{p_{i+1}}
have cohomological grading
1
.

Proof.

Part (1) Locality follows from the fact that the geodesic flow near the positive unit conormal cannot escape the neighborhood of the punctures. For Part (2), we translate the surgery partial wrapping introduced in
[
EL23
]
to the partial wrapping in
[
GPS20
]
. That is, we consider an open Liouville sector
W_{\Lambda}
obtained by attaching the disk cotangent bundle of
\Lambda\times[1,\infty)
, the sectorial analogue of a punctured handle attachment. The Reeb chords of cotangent fibres in
W_{\Lambda}
are computed using
[
EL23
, Lemma 88]
, up to some action cut-off, from which it follows that the Reeb chords all have non-negative degrees.
31
31

31

Strictly speaking, the form of the metric used in ibid. is
f(r)d\theta^{2}+dr^{2}
for some smooth, positive function
f(r)
, with the properties as in the discussion in Section B.3 in ibid., in order to ensure that the geodesics do not travel arbitrarily deep into infinity. In our case, the boundary is a circle and so using the conical metric is sufficient.
This open Liouville sector
W_{\Lambda}
can be truncated to give a closed Liouville sector
W_{\Lambda}(T)
given by attaching the disk cotangent bundle of
\Lambda\times[1,T]
instead, for a large enough
T\gg 1
. The inclusion functor induced from the inclusion of Liouville sectors
W_{\Lambda}(T)\to W_{\Lambda}(T^{\prime})
,
T<T^{\prime}
, is cohomologically fully faithful for
T
sufficiently large enough. Therefore, it suffices to compute the wrapped Floer cohomology in
W_{\Lambda}(T)
. Now,
W_{\Lambda}(T)
is deformation equivalent to the closed Liouville sector obtained by removing a standard 1-jet neighborhood of
\Lambda
in the boundary, c.f.
[
GPS20
, Example 2.15]
, the wrapped Floer cohomology of which is isomorphic to the partially wrapped Floer cohomology in
[
GPS20
]
, by
[
GPS24a
, Corollary 3.9]
. Therefore, partially wrapped Floer cohomology must also be concentrated in non-negative degrees.

For Part (3), we first present the full argument for the rank
2
case. Define the grading on
D_{p_{2}}
so that the short Reeb chord lies in
\hom^{0}(D_{p_{i}},D_{p_{2}})
. For this grading

\hom(\cone(D_{p_{1}}\to D_{p_{2}})[-1],L)=\hom(\mathcal{D}_{l},L)

with the large linking disk
\mathcal{D}_{l}
, connecting sum
D_{p_{1}}
and
D_{p_{2}}
, being equipped with the geometric grading. In particular,
D_{p_{2}}[-1]
is the graded Lagrangian object equivalent to the linking disk at
p_{2}
equipped with the geometric grading. Therefore, since

\hom^{\ast}(D_{p_{1}},D_{p_{2}}[-1])=\hom^{\ast-1}(D_{p_{1}},D_{p_{2}})

the short Reeb chord is a degree
+1
element in
\hom^{\ast}(D_{p_{1}},D_{p_{2}}[-1])
, as claimed. The higher rank case follows from the exactly same argument.
∎

6.4.2.
A concluding remark

There are more topological descriptions of
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
and
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
, not involving pseudo-holomorphic curves. As stated above,
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
-
\mbox{mod}_{k}
is quasi-equivalent to the category of local systems
\mbox{Loc}(S)
and thus perfect modules
\mbox{Perf}(\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S))
is equivalent to its compact objects
\mbox{Loc}^{c}(S)
. More generally,
[
GPS24
, Theorem 1.1]
shows that the category of perfect modules
\mbox{Perf}(\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda))
is equivalent to the category
\Sh_{\Lambda}^{c}(S)
of compact objects in
\Sh_{\Lambda}(S)
, the category of sheaves with singular support contained in
\Lambda
. Independently, since
S
is a surface in the study of Floer theory and spectral networks, one can also approach the generalization from
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S)
to
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
as the generalization from local systems to Stokes local systems. We refer to
[
Boa21
]
for a detailed description of the latter.

We conjecture that the relation between partially wrapped Fukaya categories and spectral networks, as discussed above, is compatible with Stokes local systems. In an admittedly unimpressive degree of detail, we briefly sketch what we expect, as follows. Given a Betti Lagrangian
L\subset T^{*}S
and a compatible spectral network
\EuScript{W}\subset S
, we expect the existence of the following commutative diagram:

(40)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mbox{Loc}^{c}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mbox{St}(S,\Lambda)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\EuScript{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}^{St}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\EuScript{M}}

where the notions in the diagram are:

(1)

\mbox{St}(S,\Lambda)
is the category of Stokes local systems, as described in
[
Boa21
, Section 8]
, with the Stokes diagrams given by
\Lambda
.
32
32

32

The category might be needed to be dg-enhanced, derived and consider compact objects to fit the diagram above, but all meaningful content is in ibid.
Technically, ibid. defines Stokes local systems given irregular data – which is naturally algebraic – but the same definitions allow for the notion of a Stokes local system associated to any Legendrian link isotopic to (the lift of) a front given by the cylindrical closure of a positive braid.

(2)

The Yoneda functor
\EuScript{Y}
is given by
V\mapsto\EuScript{Y}_{(L_{\circ},V)}
, as discussed in
Section

6.3
and
Section

6.4.1
above.

(3)

The functor
\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}^{St}
is essentially the functor
\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}
constructed and studied in
Section

5
. The only difference is that
\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}(V)
was described as a local system, and
\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}^{St}(V)
should be a Stokes local system. As discussed in
[
GMN12
,
GMN13
]
, the non-abelianization process actually produces a Stokes local system, so this adjustment on the codomain is obtained by the same methods.

(4)

The functor
\EuScript{M}
can be described explicitly as follows. Given a Stokes local system
V_{St}\in\mbox{St}(S,\Lambda)
, the
A_{\infty}
-module
\EuScript{M}(V_{St})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)\longrightarrow\mbox{mod}_{k}
is determined by

\EuScript{M}(V_{St})(T^{\ast}_{z}S)=V_{z},\quad\EuScript{M}(V_{St})(F_{\infty})=0,

where
T^{\ast}_{z}S
is an internal fiber,
F_{\infty}
any fiber at infinity and
V
is the underlying local system of
V_{St}
. By
Proposition

6.19
, this determines
\EuScript{M}
on objects. Note that for any
z,w\in S
two
\EuScript{W}
-adapted points, we will also have
\EuScript{M}(V_{St})(i_{*}[\alpha_{zw}])=V([\alpha_{zw}])
.

The commutativity of Diagram (
40
) is a categorical analogue of the results in
Section

5
, comparing the non-abelianiation functor
\Phi{W}
to the Family Floer functor
\mathbb{F}
.

Remark 6.22
.

(1) Note that
[
Boa21
]
establishes equivalent descriptions of
\mbox{St}(S,\Lambda)
, comparing to Stokes graded and Stokes filtered local systems. The category of Stokes filtered local systems is much closer to
\Sh_{\Lambda}(S)
. In contrast, the Floer-theoretic context has generators that directly give a grading, provided by the Lagrangian intersections, and not just the filtration more naturally associated to
\Sh_{\Lambda}(S)
. Therefore, the passage from
\Sh_{\Lambda}(S)
to
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
provides, in a sense, a symplectic topological viewpoint on the splitting result
[
Boa21
, Theorem 1.1]
, which produces a canonical Stokes grading from the filtration.

(2) It would be desirable to also have a conceptual description of
\EuScript{M}
, not given in terms of explicit generators of
\mathfrak{W}(T^{\ast}S,\Lambda)
. Specifically, we expect
\EuScript{M}
to be an equivalence and its inverse should be given by an enhancement of the Family Floer methods in
Section

5.2
.
\Box

By
Theorem

6.12
, Diagram (
40
) commutes if the Legendrian
\Lambda
at infinity is Reeb-positive. This is the case, for instance, if the Legendrian links at infinity are all cylindrical closures of positive braids, which includes the case of any irregular data with no multiplicities. In this Reeb-positive case, it also follows that the non-abelianization functor is injective on objects: it injectively sends local systems on
L
to Stokes local systems on
S
, which was conjectured by the original works
[
GMN12
,
GMN13
]
. Indeed, given
V,V^{\prime}\in\mbox{Loc}(L)
, wrapped cohomology
HW^{\ast}((L_{\circ},V),(L_{\circ},V^{\prime}))
is isomorphic to the Morse cohomology of
L
twisted by
V^{\ast}\otimes V^{\prime}
coefficients. In particular, its degree-0 group is non-zero if and only if
V
and
V^{\prime}
are isomorphic, from which injectivity follows.

7.
Spectral networks and weaves

Weaves were introduced in
[
CZ22
]
, in the context of contact and symplectic topology. Since then, they have also been productively used in the study of cluster structures, see e.g.
[
Cas+23
,
CW24
,
Cas+25
]
.The object of this section is to present a first relation between weaves and spectral networks, in particular discussing
Theorem

5
. The necessary ingredients on weaves are presented in
Section

7.1
, and the combinatorial construction of a spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}
from a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
is given in
Section

7.2
. We then conclude with
Section

7.3
presenting a number of explicit examples and computations. Note that, given the rigorously established bridge between weaves and cluster algebras, cf.
[
Cas+25
]
, this construction of
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}
provides a precise account on how cluster coordinate arise in spectral networks, in line with some predictions from supersymmetric computations, see e.g.
[
Nei14
]
. In addition, since
[
Cas+23
,
CW24
,
Cas+25
]
give explicit descriptions of Donaldson-Thomas transformations in terms of weaves, this connection between weaves and spectral networks therefore gives a direct method to compute the BPS spectrum generator.

7.1.
Weaves on Betti surfaces

Let
(S,\boldsymbol{m},{\bf\Lambda})
be a Betti surface of rank
n
and let
\beta_{i}
be a positive (cyclic) braid word in
n
-strands whose cylindrical closure gives the link
\Lambda_{i}
at the puncture
m_{i}\in\boldsymbol{m}
,
i\in[1,|\boldsymbol{m}|]
. The definition of a weave in
[
CZ22
]
is adapted to the context of Betti surfaces as follows:

Definition 7.1

(Weaves)
.

A weave
\mathfrak{w}
in
(S,\boldsymbol{m},{\bf\Lambda})
is a properly embedded graph in
S
with edges decorated by permutations in
W(G)\cong S_{n}
such that:

(1)

There are only three types of vertices for
\mathfrak{w}
, as depicted in Figure
15
:

Figure 15
.
The three types of vertices allowed in a weave
\mathfrak{w}
. (Left) Trivalent vertex, where all edges are decorated with the same permutation
s_{i}\in W(G)
. (Center) Hexavalent vertex, with the edge decorations alternating between
s_{i},s_{j}
with
|i-j|=1
. (Right) Tetravalent vertex, with edge decorations as drawn with
|i-k|>1
.

(2)

The asymptotics of the graph
\mathfrak{w}
at a puncture
m_{i}\in\boldsymbol{m}
are such that the permutations decorating its edges coincide with the corresponding positive cyclic braid word
\beta_{i}
, for all
i\in[1,m]
.
\Box

The asymptotic condition in
Definition

7.1
.(2) is drawn in Figure
16
. In figures, we often adopt the convention that colors
blue
and
red
are adjacent, and
blue
and
green
are not adjacent, see e.g.
Fig.

15
. By convention, when drawing in small rank
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we typically use
blue for
s_{1}
,
red for
s_{2}
and
green for
s_{3}
.

Figure 16
.
A weave
\mathfrak{w}
near a circle
\partial S_{i}
around a puncture
m_{i}\in\boldsymbol{m}
, according to
Definition

7.1
.(2). The decorations on the edges of
\mathfrak{w}
are written as
s_{i_{j}}
, and we use colors to emphasize that they might be different. The cyclic braid word being spelled is
\beta_{i}=s_{i_{1}}s_{i_{2}}s_{i_{3}}\ldots s_{i_{\ell}}
.

In this section, we focus on the case of the Betti surface
(S^{2},\{\infty\},\beta\delta(\beta))
given by a once punctured 2-sphere
S=S^{2}
, with the braid around the unique puncture
\{\infty\}
being the form
\beta\delta(\beta)
.
33
33

33

We can and will assume that
\delta(\beta)=w_{0}
, see e.g.
[
Cas+25
]
.
In higher rank
G=\mbox{SL}_{n}
, these Betti surfaces already lead to many interesting cases, including Bers-Nevins-Roberts
[
BNR82
]
and all braid varieties
[
Cas+25
]
, thus all double Bruhat cells, positroids and double Bott-Samelson cases. To study weaves and spectral networks in
(S^{2},\{\infty\},\beta\delta(\beta))
, we use the class of Demazure weaves introduced in
[
Cas+24
, Section 4]
, cf. also
[
Cas+25
, Section 4]
. Recall that a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\beta\longrightarrow\delta(\beta)
for
\beta=\sigma_{i_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{i_{\ell}}
is a weave in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
drawn vertically top-to-bottom such that

(i)

There are two types of semi-infinite edges: north and south. The northern semi-infinite edges coincide with the vertical upwards semi-rays
\{j\}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
outside of a compact set,
j\in[1,\ell]
, and the souththern semi-infinite edges coincide with the vertical downwards semi-rays
\{k\}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
k\in[1,\ell(\delta(\beta))
.

(ii)

The weave
\mathfrak{w}
is never tangent to the horizontal lines
\mathbb{R}\times\{y\}
,
y\in\mathbb{R}
.

(iii)

The only vertices of
\mathfrak{w}
are as literally as drawn in
Fig.

15
when
\mathfrak{w}
is scanned top-to-bottom. That is, the trivalent vertex cannot go from
s_{i}
on top to
s_{i}s_{i}
at the bottom: it must always go from
s_{i}s_{i}
at the top to
s_{i}
at the bottom.

Here we identify
S^{2}\setminus\{\infty\}\cong\mathbb{R}^{2}
via a diffeomorphism and choose Cartesian coordinates
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Demazure weaves are a diagrammatic description of a sequence of braid words where the only allowed moves are braid moves and the nil-Hecke type move
\sigma_{i}^{2}\to\sigma_{i}
, the sequence starts with
\beta
, corresponding to a horizontal slice in positive large
y
-height, and ends with
\delta(\beta)
, corresponding to a horizontal slice in negative large
y
-height. For the purpose of constructing spectral networks, we use the following modification of a Demazure weave:

Definition 7.2

(Bending of Demazure weaves)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\beta\longrightarrow\delta(\beta)
be a Demazure weave, as depicted in Figure
17
(left). By definition, the right bending of
\mathfrak{w}
is the weave in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
drawn as in Figure
17
(right).
\Box

Figure 17
.
(Left) A general Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\beta\to\delta(\beta)
. (Right) The right bending of
\mathfrak{w}
. The use of the bent weave, as depicted on the right, is that it can be seen as an embedded exact Lagrangian filling of the
(-1)
-closure of
\beta\delta(\beta)
.

The relevance of
Definition

7.2
is that the bent Demazure weave represents a spatial wavefront whose Legendrian lift is such that its Lagrangian projection in
(\mathbb{R}^{4},\omega_{\text{st}})
an exact embedded Lagrangian filling of the
(-1)
-closure
\Lambda_{\beta\delta(\beta)}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{3},\xi_{\text{st}})
. This follows from the original construction of weaves in
[
CZ22
]
and the observation that bending a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
does not introduce any Reeb chords. It is the starting idea behind many recent results connecting symplectic topology and cluster algebras, cf. e.g.
[
CL22
,
Cas+23
,
Cas+25
,
Cas+24
]
. In short, the bending of a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
describe an exact Betti Lagrangian
L_{\mathfrak{w}}
for the Betti surface
(S^{2},\{\infty\},\beta\delta(\beta))
.

Remark 7.3
.

(1) The need for bending is to ensure there are no concave ends. A Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
, without bending, would naturally yield an exact embedded Lagrangian cobordism from
\Lambda_{\delta(\beta)}
