, in the concave end, up to
\Lambda_{\beta}
, in the convex end. The bending effectively fills the concave end, yielding a Lagrangian filling of
\Lambda_{\beta}
. We plan to study non-empty concave ends in future work as well.

(2) The theory of weaves, as developed in
[
CZ22
]
, works in an arbitrary Betti surface. For instance,
[
CZ22
, Section 3.1]
builds weaves from
N
-triangulations of surfaces, whose spectral networks will match (and generalize) the level
N
lift spectral networks from
[
GMN14
]
. Although it is possible to define a generalization of Demazure weaves to any Betti surface, with higher genus and punctures, the wealth of Demazure weaves in
S^{2}\times\{\infty\}
and their associated spectral networks, as momentarily built in
Section

7.2
, already covers many interesting examples and applications, so we focus on those.
\Box

7.2.
Spectral networks for Demazure weaves

By
Section

7.1
, bending a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
gives an exact Betti Lagrangian
L_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset(T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{2},\lambda_{\text{st}})
for the Betti surface
(S^{2},\{\infty\},\beta\delta(\beta))
. The goal is to construct a Morse spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
for
L_{\mathfrak{w}}
from the data of the weave
\mathfrak{w}
.

Definition 7.4

(Augmentation forest of
\mathfrak{w}
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the right bending of a Demazure weave with
\ell
trivalent vertices. By definition, the augmentation forest
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
of
\mathfrak{w}
is the spectral network built as follows:

(1)

Start with an empty prespectral network
\EuScript{F}_{0}=\EuScript{W}_{0}=\emptyset
. Scan the trivalent vertices of
\mathfrak{w}
bottom-to-top, indexing by
i\in[1,\ell]
in this bottom-to-top order for the vertices: at the
i
th trivalent weave vertex
v_{i}
of
\mathfrak{w}
, create three directed flowlines as in Figure
18
.(1).

(2)

Flow each of the three edge flowlines in Figure
18
.(1) from
v_{i}
as follows. Flowlines (a) and (b) continue following the weave edges up until their reach the top of the weave. These flowlines go straight through both 4 and 6-valent vertices and continue up on the left of a trivalent, as depicted in Figure
18
.(2); the permutation labels of the flowline change by conjugation with the labels of the weave lines. The flowline (c) is extended to the right, through weave edges, changing its labeling accordingly, until it reaches a weave edge whose permutation label coincides with that of the flowline (at that stage). Right before that point (to its left), the edge (c) is extended upward following that weave edge until it reaches the top. The result of these extensions is a prespectral network
\EuScript{F}_{i}
, which is obtained by adding these flowlines to the spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{i-1}
.

(3)

Consider the prespectral network
\EuScript{F}_{i}
and add additional flowlines to create a consistent extension of
\EuScript{F}_{i}
. The only new flowlines that need to be created appear at the intersection of the (new) flowlines both at
v_{i}
with the flowlines of
\EuScript{W}_{i-1}
. Then declare
\EuScript{W}_{i}
to be the resulting spectral network.

(4)

Declare
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}
to be
\EuScript{W}_{\ell}
, the result of applying the above process to all trivalent vertices of
\mathfrak{w}
, scanned bottom to top.
\Box

Figure 18
.
(1) The local model of the flowlines in the augmentation forest
\Theta_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
near a trivalent vertex. (2) The local models for the flowline behaviour near trivalent vertices and hexavalent vertices north of their source trivalent vertex.

The spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
in
Section

7.2
is constructed combinatorially from
\mathfrak{w}
, it is just a directed graph with labels, a priori without any analytic meaning or relation to
D_{4}^{-}
-flowtrees. The adiabatic degeneration results in
Section

3
along with the spatial front associated to a weave
\mathfrak{w}
, as described in
[
CZ22
]
, imply that
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}
is in fact obtained by adiabatically degenerating the pseudoholomorphic strips associated to the augmentation induced by the Lagrangian filling
L_{\mathfrak{w}}
of the
(-1)
-closure of
\beta\delta(\beta)
. We record this fact as follows, which implies
Theorem

5
:

Proposition 7.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{w}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\beta\to w_{0}
be a Demazure weave for a braid word
\beta
with
\delta(\beta)=w_{0}
. Consider the associated embedded exact Lagrangian filling
L_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{2}
of
\Lambda_{\beta w_{0}}
and the induced augmentation

\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{A}(\Lambda_{\beta w_{0}})\longrightarrow k[H_{1}(L_{\mathfrak{w}})]

for its Legendrian contact dg-algebra. Then, the spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the union of all the
D_{4}^{-}
-trees obtained by adiabatically degenerating the rigid pseudo-holomorphic strips contributing to
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}
.
\Box

Definition

7.4
and
Proposition

7.5
provide a wealth of interesting spectral networks directly related to Floer theory. In particular, given the rigorously establish existence of and weave calculus for cluster structures, they yield a versatile set of tools for computing BPS states. For instance, the Donaldson-Thomas transformations for Demazure weaves are explicitly described in
[
Cas+25
, Section 8.3]
, cf. also
[
CW24
, Section 5.4]
, which readily yield the BPS spectrum generator, a.k.a. BPS monodromy, for the associated physical system.

Remark 7.6
.

(1) In the meromorphic case, some of the
D_{4}^{-}
-trees in
W_{\mathfrak{w}}
with a positive puncture at a Reeb chord in
\Lambda_{\partial_{\mathfrak{w}}}
align with the BPS solitons described by the (non-degenerate) open finite webs in
[
GMN13a
, Section 3.2]
.

(2) The Lusztig cycles of a Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
are introduced in
[
Cas+25
, Section 4]
, see also
[
CW24
, Section 3]
. Intuitively, in the meromorphic case, the mutable Lusztig cycles relate to
W_{\mathfrak{w}}
in that they should correspond to finite webs representing 4d BPS states, as in
[
GMN13a
, Section 3.1]
, for a degenerate spectral network related to (but in a different phase than) the non-degenerate network
W_{\mathfrak{w}}
. This relation remains to be explored in detail.
\Box

7.3.
Explicit computations of spectral networks from Demazure weaves

In this subsection, we discuss several cases of
Definition

7.4
and
Proposition

7.5
in detail, establishing the comparison between the flowlines of spectral networks and the adiabatic degenerations of pseudoholomorphic strips.

7.3.1.
A 2-stranded example in detail

Consider the braid word
\beta=\sigma_{1}^{6}
in 2-strands and the Legendrian dg-algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\beta}
of the
(-1)
-closure of
\beta\delta(\beta)
. Let us denote its degree-0 Reeb chords by the variables
z_{6},z_{5},z_{4},z_{3},z_{2},z_{1},w_{1}
, read left-to-right, with
w_{1}
corresponding to the crossing of
\delta(\beta)=\sigma_{1}
. This labeling is drawn in Figure
19
. Consider the Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\beta\longrightarrow\delta(\beta)
depicted in Figure
19
(left). In the language of pinching sequences, the Lagrangian filling
L_{\mathfrak{w}}
is obtained by pinching the crossings in the Lagrangian projection associated to
z_{1},z_{3},z_{4},z_{5},z_{2}
, in this order. A computation, as in
[
CN22
]
, shows that
L_{\mathfrak{w}}
gives the augmentation

\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{A}_{\beta}\longrightarrow k[H_{1}(L_{\mathfrak{w}},T)]

uniquely determined by its non-zero values:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(z_{6})&=\frac{1}{s_{2}s_{3}^{2}s_{4}^{2}s_{5}^{2}}-\frac{1}{s_{5}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(z_{5})&=s_{5}-\frac{1}{s_{4}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(z_{4})&=s_{4}-\frac{1}{s_{3}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(z_{3})&=s_{3}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(z_{2})&=s_{2}-\frac{1}{s_{1}}-\frac{1}{s_{3}}+\frac{1}{s_{3}^{2}s_{4}}-\frac{1}{s_{3}^{2}s_{4}^{2}s_{5}}\\
\end{split}\quad\begin{split}\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(z_{1})&=s_{1}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(w_{1})&=\frac{1}{s_{1}^{2}s_{2}}-\frac{1}{s_{1}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(t_{1})&=-\frac{1}{s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}s_{4}s_{5}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(t_{2})&=s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}s_{4}s_{5}.\end{split}

where
t_{1},t_{2}
are marked points
T=\{t_{1},t_{2}\}
at the (right at the) right of
w_{1}
,
t_{i}
in the
i
th strand, counting from the bottom, and
s_{i}\in H_{1}(L_{\mathfrak{w}},T)
is the relative cycle Poincaré dual to the trivalent vertex whose associated top-right edge corresponds to
z_{i}
.

By construction,
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}
counts rigid pseudo-holomorphic strips and records their (relative) homology classes. Therefore, there ought to be a precise correspondence between the non-zero terms in
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(z_{i})
, and
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(w_{1})
, and the rigid flowtrees of the spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}
for
L_{\mathfrak{w}}
which are asymptotic to
z_{i}
, and
w_{1}
. This is indeed the case:
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}
is drawn in Figure
19
(right), and each term of
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}
above exactly corresponds to such flowtrees. For instance, the flowtrees in the spectral network that are coloured not black correspond to the terms of

\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(z_{2})=s_{2}-\frac{1}{s_{1}}-\frac{1}{s_{3}}+\frac{1}{s_{3}^{2}s_{4}}-\frac{1}{s_{3}^{2}s_{4}^{2}s_{5}},

as they are asymptotic to the Reeb chord
z_{2}
. Specifically, the green flowline gives the term
-s_{3}^{-1}
, the yellow gives
s_{3}^{-2}s_{4}
, the orange
-s_{3}^{-2}s_{4}^{-2}s_{5}^{-1}
, the purple gives
s_{2}
and the rightmost cardinal flowline
-s_{1}^{-1}
.

Figure 19
.
(Left) A Demazure weave
\mathfrak{w}
for
\beta=\sigma_{1}^{6}
, with its Reeb chords at the (top) boundary marked with a pink dot and labeled. (Right) The spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}
compatible with
L_{\mathfrak{w}}
, as built above.

7.3.2.
Mutation in 2-strands

Similar to the example above, this correspondence between terms of the augmentation
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}
and the flowlines in
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}
works in the same lin for other 2-stranded braids and their Demazure weaves. The mutation in spectral networks can be analyzed in the local model of
\beta=\sigma_{1}^{3}
. The two sides of a weave mutations are depicted in Figure
20
,
\mathfrak{w}_{1}
on the left and
\mathfrak{w}_{2}
on the right.

Figure 20
.
The two sides of a weave mutation,
\mathfrak{w}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{w}_{2}
, and their associated spectral networks
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}_{1}}
and
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}_{2}}
.

If we label the degree-0 Reeb chords associated to the crossings of
\beta\delta(\beta)=\sigma_{1}^{4}
by
z_{3},z_{2},z_{1},w_{1}
, reading left-to-right, and use
T=\{t_{1},t_{2}\}
as above, the two augmentations

\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}_{i}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{1}^{3}}\longrightarrow k[H_{1}(L_{\mathfrak{w}_{i}},T)],\quad i=1,2,

are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}_{1}}(z_{3})&=\frac{1}{s_{1}s_{2}^{2}}-\frac{1}{s_{2}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}_{1}}(z_{2})&=s_{2}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}_{1}}(z_{1})&=s_{1}-\frac{1}{s_{2}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}_{1}}(w_{1})&=-\frac{1}{s_{1}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}_{1}}(t_{1})&=-\frac{1}{s_{1}s_{2}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}_{1}}(t_{2})&=-s_{1}s_{2}.\end{split}\quad\begin{split}\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}_{2}}(z_{3})&=-\frac{1}{s_{2}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}_{2}}(z_{2})&=s_{2}-\frac{1}{s_{1}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}_{2}}(z_{1})&=s_{1}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}_{2}}(w_{1})&=\frac{1}{s_{1}^{2}s_{2}}-\frac{1}{s_{1}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}_{2}}(t_{1})&=-\frac{1}{s_{1}s_{2}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}_{2}}(t_{2})&=-s_{1}s_{2}.\end{split}

Note that in the
z
-coordinates of the braid variety
X(\sigma_{1}^{3})
, the mutable cluster variable
A_{1}
for the torus chart
T_{\mathfrak{w}_{1}}\subset X(\sigma_{1}^{3})
associated to
L_{\mathfrak{w}_{1}}
is
A_{1}=z_{2}
, and the frozen is
z_{2}z_{3}-1
. The quiver
Q_{\mathfrak{w}_{2}}
has a unique arrow from the frozen to the mutable. After mutation, the mutable cluster variable
A_{2}
for the torus chart
T_{\mathfrak{w}_{2}}\subset X(\sigma_{1}^{3})
associated to
L_{\mathfrak{w}_{2}}
is
A_{2}=z_{3}
, and the frozen
z_{3}z_{2}-1
. The quiver
Q_{\mathfrak{w}_{1}}
has a unique arrow from the mutable to the frozen. By the mutation formula, we must have that the mutated variable
A_{1}^{\prime}
of
A_{1}=z_{2}
is such that

A_{1}A_{1}^{\prime}=1+(z_{2}z_{3}-1)

and thus indeed
A_{1}^{\prime}=A_{2}=z_{3}
. The corresponding mutation in the spectral networks
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}_{1}}
and
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}_{2}}
, i.e. its change in the flowtrees, is drawn in Figure
20
. The change in the flowlines, seen as adiabatic limits of pseudo-holomorphic strips, matches the difference in the augmentation
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}_{1}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}_{2}}
and, using
z
-coordinates, it becomes the standard cluster mutation.

Figure 21
.
(Left) The weave
\mathfrak{w}_{bnr}
obtained by considering the front of the Legendrian lift of the real part of the Bers-Nevins-Roberts spectral curve
\Sigma_{bnr}
. (Right) The spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}_{bnr}}
according to
Section

7.2
.

7.3.3.
The Berk-Nevins-Roberts spectral network

The remarkable article
[
BNR82
]
first introduced the
new
Stokes lines, born at certain intersections of two ordinary Stokes lines and studied them in the context of the WKB asymptotics, c.f.
Example

2.12
. These flowlines only appear at higher rank
n\geq 3
, and mark a crucial difference between the rank-2 case and higher ranks. The specific example studied in
[
BNR82
]
corresponds to the spectral curve

\Sigma_{bnr}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\{(z,w)\in T^{*}\mathbb{C}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}w^{3}-3w+x=0\}.

The weave
\mathfrak{w}_{bnr}
associated to its real part
\text{Re}(\Sigma)
is depicted in Figure
21
(left). It is already depicted with the bending, so that it is apparent that can be seen as a bent Demazure weave for the braid word
\beta=\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}
. Note that the associated spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{bnr}
, depicted in
[
BNR82
, Figure 1]
was analyzed in
[
Kat+15
, Section 5]
from the perspective of harmonic maps to buildings.

Figure 22
.
The spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}_{bnr}}
, which indeed coincides with the spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{bnr}
from
[
BNR82
]
. In this figure, the two new Stokes line are highlighted in purple, and the two trivalent
D_{4}^{-}
-vertices are circled in dashed yellow.

From the Floer-theoretic perspective, we label the Reeb chords of
\beta\delta(\beta)=\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1})
as in Figure
21
:
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4},z_{5}
correspond to the crossings of
\beta
, reading right-to-left, and
w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}
to those of
\delta(\beta)
, also reading right-to-left. The spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}_{bnr}}
associated to
\mathfrak{w}_{bnr}
according to
Section

7.2
is depicted in Figure
21
(right). We have also drawn
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}_{bnr}}
on its own in Figure
22
, where the two
new
Stokes lines, born at creation vertices, are depicted in purple. Here we can readily see that the spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}_{bnr}}
obtained by adiabatic degeneration of pseudoholomorphic strips coincides with the original spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{bnr}
from
[
BNR82
]
associated to
\Sigma_{bnr}
.

7.3.4.
An example of a cancellation pair

Let us consider a local model for a cancellation pair coming from applying a Reidemeister III move twice to
\beta=\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}
. This corresponds to the sequence of braid words
\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\to\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\to\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}
. First, we will consider one Reidemeister III move
\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\to\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}
on its own, as realized by the weave
\mathfrak{w}_{R3}
in Figure
23
. This is understood as a local move that can be inserted anywhere in a weave where there is a subword
\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}
.

Figure 23
.
The four degenerations of pseudoholomorphic strips contributing to the dg-algebra morphism induced by a Reidemeister III move on a Legendrian front. In (4), the bifurcation occurs as a creation vertex from the flowlines in (1) and (2), the traces of which are drawn in dashed gray in (4).

The embedded exact Lagrangian cobordism associated to
\mathfrak{w}_{R3}
gives a dg-algebra morphism
\Psi_{R3}
between the Legendrian contact dg-algebra of the convex end, which has the piece
\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}
, to the concave end, which contains
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}
. Denote by
z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
the Reeb chords next to the crossings associated to
\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}
, and keep the same notation for those in
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}
, as depicted in Figure
23
.(1). This morphism
\Psi_{R3}
is computed in
[
CN22
, Section 4.1.2]
and, locally in this model, reads as follows:

\Psi_{R3}(z_{1})=z_{3},\quad\Psi_{R3}(z_{3})=z_{1},\quad\Psi_{R3}(z_{2})=z_{2}\pm z_{1}z_{3},

where we do not specify the sign, cf.
[
CN22
]
for the choices that give precise signs. Each of these four terms exists because of a pseudoholomorphic strip. Their adiabatic degenerations, in the form of flowtrees, are depicted in Figure
23
. Namely, Figure
23
.(1) explains
\Psi_{R3}(z_{3})=z_{1}
,
23
.(2) explains
\Psi_{R3}(z_{1})=z_{3}
,
23
.(3) is the tree corresponding to the term
z_{2}
in
\Psi_{R3}(z_{2})=z_{2}\pm z_{1}z_{3}
and the
D_{4}^{-}
-tree in
23
.(4) corresponds to the term
z_{1}z_{3}
.

Let us now perform two consecutive Reidemeister moves, inverses of each other. The corresponding weave
\mathfrak{w}_{c}
is depicted in Figure
24
.(1). By the candy twist move in
[
CZ22
, Figure 19]
, this weave
\mathfrak{w}_{c}
is equivalent to the trivial weave with three strands labeled
s_{1},s_{2},s_{1}
and no crossings. Therefore, by Hamiltonian invariance of the associated exact embedded Lagrangian cobordisms, it must be that the induced dg-algebra map given by
\mathfrak{w}_{c}
is the identity and there
has to
be a cancelation account for the term
z_{1}z_{3}
above appearing and disappearing.

Figure 24
.
(1) The weave corresponding to two consecutive Reidemeister III moves
\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\to\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\to\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}
. (2) The associated spectral network, which coincides with the adiabatic
D_{4}^{-}
-tree degeneration of the pseudoholomorphic strips corresponding to the induce dg-algebra maps. (3) The spectral network on its own, with the two canceling terms highlighted in purple. (4) The result of deleting the walls of the spectral network in (3) that cancel each other. In this case, the interaction vertex that is not creation would be of
annihilation
type. The actual spectral network in (3) witnesses both canceling flowlines, and their cancelation is seen in the BPS index. More intuitively, the
D_{4}^{-}
-tree corresponding to the upper purple flowline has a self-intersection point, unlike the tree for the purple flowline, hence its sign is opposite.

This cancellation is indeed seen in the spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}_{c}}
, which is depicted in Figure
24
.(2). The two flowlines, with the same asymptotic end, that cancel each other are highlighted in purple in Figure
24
.(3). In the physics literature, this configuration is sometimes depicted as in Figure
24
.(4), where the parts of the walls of the canceling pair that have vanishing BPS index are not drawn: this is, with phases appropriately understood, related to the
setting sun
model in
[
GMN13a
, Section 7.4]
.

7.3.5.
A more elaborate 3-stranded example

Consider the 3-stranded braid word
\beta=(\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1})^{3}\sigma_{2}
, so that
\beta\delta(\beta)=(\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1})^{5}\sigma_{2}
and the (-1)-closure of
\beta\delta(\beta)
is the max-tb
(3,2)
-torus knot, i.e. the max-tb trefoil presented in 3-strands. Consider its right inductive weave
\mathfrak{w}
, as depicted in Figure
25
(left), and its associated embedded exact Lagrangian filling
L_{\mathfrak{w}}
. This example illustrates how the correspondence between the flowlines of the spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}
and adiabatic degenerations of the pseudoholomorphic strips contributing to the augmentation
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}
induced by
L_{\mathfrak{w}}
. It is particularly helpful in understanding how terms can (and do) cancel, locally as in
Section

7.3.4
, in a globally interesting example.

Figure 25
.
(1) The right inductive weave
\mathfrak{w}
for
\beta=(\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1})^{3}\sigma_{2}
. The four trivalent vertices are labeled
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}
, the Reeb chords associated to crossings in
\beta
are denoted
z_{1},\ldots,z_{7}
and those for
\delta(\beta)
are denoted
w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}
. (2) The first prespectral network
\EuScript{F}_{1}
, consisting of the three flowlines starting at the trivalent
D_{4}^{-}
-vertex
v_{1}
. (3) The second prespectral network
\EuScript{F}_{2}
, consisting of the flowlines starting at the two
D_{4}^{-}
-vertices
v_{1},v_{2}
. Notice that it is
not
consistent, as there are 4-valent vertices in
\EuScript{F}_{2}
which should be creation vertices. A consistent extension is drawn in Figure
26
.(4).

First, from the Floer-theoretic side, we label the Reeb chords as in Figure
25
(left). In these coordinates, the Lagrangian filling
L_{\mathfrak{w}}
gives the augmentation

\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{A}_{\beta}\longrightarrow k[H_{1}(L_{\mathfrak{w}},T)]

which determined by its non-zero values:

(41)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(z_{7})&=-\frac{s_{3}}{s_{2}s_{4}^{2}}-\frac{1}{s_{4}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(z_{6})&=\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}s_{3}^{2}}-\frac{1}{s_{3}}+\frac{1}{s_{1}s_{3}s_{4}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(z_{5})&=\frac{s_{2}s_{4}}{s_{1}s_{3}^{2}}-\frac{s_{4}}{s_{3}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(z_{4})&=s_{4}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(z_{3})&=s_{3}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(z_{2})&=s_{2}+\frac{s_{3}}{s_{4}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(z_{1})&=s_{1}-\frac{s_{2}}{s_{3}}-\frac{1}{s_{4}}\end{split}\quad\begin{split}\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(w_{3})&=\frac{s_{1}}{s_{2}}-\frac{1}{s_{3}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(w_{2})&=-\frac{1}{s_{2}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(w_{1})&=-\frac{1}{s_{1}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(t_{1})&=-\frac{1}{s_{1}s_{3}}\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(t_{2})&=-\frac{s_{1}s_{3}}{s_{2}s_{4}}.\\
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}(t_{3})&=-s_{2}s_{4}.\end{split}

where
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}
are marked points
T=\{t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\}
at the (right at the) right of
w_{1}
,
t_{i}
in the
i
th strand, counting from the bottom. As above,
s_{i}\in H_{1}(L_{\mathfrak{w}},T)
denotes the relative cycle Poincaré dual to the trivalent vertex whose associated top-right edge corresponds to
z_{i}
.

Figure 26
.
Prespectral networks in the steps towards building
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}
. In (4) we added flowlines to make the network in Figure
25
.(3) consistent. In (5) the flowlines born from
v_{3}
are added and (6) depicts the associated consistent extension.

Second, independent of the Floer-theoretic computation, we consider the spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}
associated to the weave
\mathfrak{w}
, as built in
Section

7.2
. The final spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}
is drawn in Figure

. The process to obtain it is depicted in several steps and described as follows:

(i)

The first prespectral network
\EuScript{F}_{1}
is depicted in Figure
25
.(2), where the three walls of
\EuScript{F}_{1}
are drawn, being born at the trivalent
D_{4}^{-}
-vertex
v_{1}
.

(ii)

The second prespectral network
\EuScript{F}_{2}
is depicted in Figure
25
.(3), where three additional walls are born at the
D_{4}^{-}
-vertex
v_{2}
, in addition to those for
\EuScript{F}_{1}
are drawn.

(iii)

Since the prespectral network
\EuScript{F}_{2}
not consistent, we proceed by considering a consistent extension
\EuScript{F}_{3}
, as drawn in Figure

.(4). It is obtained by adding four flowlines to
\EuScript{F}_{2}

\EuScript{F}_{3}
is consistent.

(iv)

The next prespectral network
\EuScript{F}_{4}
is depicted in Figure

.(5), where the three flowlines born in the
D_{4}^{-}
-vertex
v_{3}
are added. Such
\EuScript{F}_{4}
is inconsistent, and thus we consider its consistent extension
\EuScript{F}_{5}
, which is drawn in Figure

.(6). The only flowline needed to be added to
\EuScript{F}_{4}
for consistency is depicted in pink, which itself is caused by the flowline born in

highlighted in green interacting with a flowline born in

.

(v)

The final step is to add the three flowlines born at
v_{4}
and the additional flowlines to make the resulting prespectral network consistent. The resulting spectral network is
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}
, which is drawn in Figure

(left), on top of the weave
\mathfrak{w}
. It is drawn on its own in Figure

(right).

Figure 27
.
(Left) The spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}
on the weave
\mathfrak{w}
. (Right) The spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}
drawn on its own. The colors for some of the flowlines indicate that they are creation flowlines: those that share a color are created by (repeated) intersections of the same two flowlines. Some pairs of flowlines of the same color cancel each other, e.g. the two pink flowlines cancel each other.

The spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}
, with all its flowlines, is drawn in Figure
27
(right), but some of these flowlines cancel each other. That is, when considering counts of BPS states or pseudoholomorphic strips, there are pairs of such flowlines where each of the two flowlines contributes the same term, but they do so with opposite signs. Specifically, this occurs in this example in two cases: the two pink flowlines in Figure
27
(right) cancel each other, and two of the yellow flowlines (the two ones born lower than the third one) also cancel each other. We have depicted the spectral network obtained by removing such flowlines in Figure

, where the two canceling yellow lines are drawn dashed and the two pink flowlines have been erased.

.

. The two dashed flowlines, drawn in yellow, cancel each other. The (solid) flowlines in yellow, green and blue are the flowlines born in creation vertices in the last step of the process, after adding the flowlines born in
v_{4}
and making the resulting prespectral network consistent.

We conclude by noting that the flowlines for the spectral network
\EuScript{W}_{\mathfrak{w}}
, as drawn in Figure

, indeed match with the terms of the augmentation
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{w}}
as described in
Eq.

41
.

Appendix A
Aspects of the geometry of pseudo-holomorphic curves

This appendix collects three technical results that are used in the manuscript: geometric boundedness of meromorphic spectral curves, a type of reverse isoperimetric inequality and a brief discussion on spin structures.

A.1.
Geometric boundedness of meromorphic spectral curves

In this section, we show that the meromorphic spectral curves that satisfy the
O(-1)
-end conditions are geometrically bounded, in the sense of
[
GS16
, Definition 2.7]
. This is achieved by showing that the
C^{\ell}
-norm of the second fundamental form is uniformly bounded. We use the Sasaki metric obtained from metrics of the form
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert z\right\rvert}^{-2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert dz\right\rvert}^{2}
near the poles. This metric is geometrically bounded outside
T^{\ast}K
for some compact subset
K\subset C
, since the metric
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert z\right\rvert}^{-2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert dz\right\rvert}^{2}
is flat. This is used in Sections
4
,
5
and
6
. The precise result reads as follows:

Proposition A.1
.

Let
\Sigma\subset T^{*}C
be a meromorphic spectral curve. Then the smooth sheets of the scaled curves
\varepsilon\Sigma\subset T^{*}C
near infinity are uniformly geometrically bounded, uniformly with respect to
\varepsilon
, for any
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Proof.

The argument is essentially finding an appropriate coordinate system where geometric boundedness is apparent. First, by construction, the germ of
\Sigma
at infinity can be written as

z\to(z,c_{1}t^{-k}+c_{2}t^{-k+1}+\ldots+c_{k-n}t^{-n}),\quad\mbox{ for some }n<k,\quad t^{n}=z.

Choose a branch cut for the logarithm and consider the flat conformal coordinate
W\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int z^{-1}dz=\log(z)
; we can and do assume that
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert z\right\rvert}<1
, so that
W
has
negative
real part. Under this coordinate transformation, the smooth sheets of the meromorphic spectral curve transform into

(W,c_{1}\exp((-k/n+1)W)+\ldots+c_{k}).

Set
m=k/n
and note that
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\exp((-m+1)W)\right\rvert}
converges to
+\infty
as
W\to-\infty
because
m>1
. Also, since the metric
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert z\right\rvert}^{-2}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert dz\right\rvert}^{2}
transforms into
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert dW\right\rvert}^{2}
, the metric on
\mathbb{C}^{2}_{W,dW}
is the standard flat metric. By denoting
Q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\exp(c_{1}(-m+1)W)
, and using the branch cut, we can write
\log(Q)=c_{1}(-m+1)W
. This coordinate
Q
is the one we will use to prove geometric boundedness, as follows. Reparametrizing the sheets in the
Q
-coordinate we obtain

(b_{0}\log(Q),b_{1}Q+O(Q^{1/(k-1)}))

for some
0<\alpha<1
and non-zero constants
b_{0},b_{1}
. We now show that curves in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
of the form

(\log(Q),Q)

are geometrically bounded over the entire complex plane, with the more general case with the
O(Q^{1/(k-1)})
term follows similarly. Consider a curve of the form
(\log(Q),Q)
. If
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert Q\right\rvert}\leq 1
then re-write
W=\log(Q)
so that
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert Q\right\rvert}\leq 1
is equivalent to
W
having negative real part. The geometric boundedness of the curve for
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert Q\right\rvert}\leq 1
then follows from the geometric boundedness of the curve
(W,e^{W})
for
\text{Re}{(W)}<0
.

If
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert Q\right\rvert}\geq 1
, note that given a holomorphic curve of the form
(f(z),g(z))
in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, the tangent vectors are given by
(f^{\prime}(z),g^{\prime}(z))
,
(if^{\prime}(z),ig^{\prime}(z))
and the normal vectors are given by
(-\overline{g^{\prime}(z)},\overline{f^{\prime}(z)})
and
(-i\overline{g^{\prime}(z)},i\overline{f^{\prime}(z)})
. Specializing to our case, the tangent vectors are given by
(1/Q,1)
and
(i/Q,i)
, and the normal vectors are given by
(-1,1/\bar{Q})
and
(-i,i/\bar{Q})
. The induced metric on the curve is
(\mathinner{\!\left\lvert Q\right\rvert}^{-2}+1)\mathinner{\!\left\lvert dQ\right\rvert}^{2}
. Therefore, the non-vanishing Christoffel symbols are given by

\displaystyle\Gamma^{Q}_{QQ}=(1+\mathinner{\!\left\lvert Q\right\rvert}^{-2})^{-1}(\bar{Q}^{-1}),

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\bar{Q}}_{\bar{Q}\bar{Q}}=(1+\mathinner{\!\left\lvert Q\right\rvert}^{-2})^{-1}(Q^{-1}),

and the second fundamental form reads

B(\partial_{z},\partial_{z})=[-Q^{-2}/(1+\mathinner{\!\left\lvert Q\right\rvert}^{-1})^{3/2}](-1,1/\bar{Q}).

A direct computation implies that the derivatives of the second fundamental form, the Christofell symbols, and their derivatives are of the form
O(\mathinner{\!\left\lvert Q\right\rvert}^{-1})
. The norm of
\partial_{z}
is uniformly positive, and the derivatives are again
O(\mathinner{\!\left\lvert Q\right\rvert}^{-1})
. Therefore,
C^{\ell}
norm of the second fundamental form must be bounded as well. In addition, these bounds are uniform with respect to
\varepsilon
because we can reparametrize

(\log(Q),\epsilon Q)=(\log(P)-\log(\epsilon),P)
and use the boundedness of
(\log(Q),Q)
.
∎

A consequence of
Proposition

A.1
is the following a priori diameter estimate for pseudoholomorphic disks with boundaries on Betti Lagrangians and conormals:

Lemma A.2
.

Let
S
be a Betti surface and
L\subset T^{*}S
a Betti Lagrangian. If
L
is not exact, suppose that it is meromorphic near the punctures. Let
K\subset S
be a precompact submanifold of
S
containing
\pi(K_{L})
in its interior. Then there exists
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(M,g,L,K)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
and
J_{g}
-holomorphic half-disk
u\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(A_{1},\partial^{+}A_{1})\to(D^{\ast}M,D^{\ast}(K)\cup\varepsilon L)
with energy less than
\varepsilon E
, the image of
u
is contained in the disk bundle
D^{\ast}N_{CE}(K)
, where
C\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
depends only on
(M,g,L,K)
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

A.1
, smooth sheeets of
\varepsilon\Sigma
are uniformly geometrically bounded near infinity. Thus an argument as in
[
AL94
, Chapter V]
implies that that given any enery cut-off
E\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, there is a constant
C_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that pseudo-holomorphic half-disks with boundary on a
single
smooth sheet of
\varepsilon\Sigma
satisfy the estimate. Since
\varepsilon\Sigma
is multi-sheeted, we set
C=\max(\rho^{-1},1)\cdot C_{0}
and iterate. For the monotonicity argument, we need to choose a small neighborhood of points on
L
such that its intersection with
\varepsilon L
is contractible and connected. The size of such a neighborhood will be
\rho\varepsilon
and shrinking the size of the neighborhood affects the diameter given by monotonicity estimate by
\rho^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}
. Since
\rho^{-1}
is fixed, the diameter is bounded by
CE
, as required.
∎

A.2.
Truncated reverse isoperimetric inequality

Reverse isoperimetric inequalities in the context of pseudoholomorphic curves bounded by Lagrangians appeared in
[
GS14
]
, cf. also
[
Duv16
]
. As pointed out in
Section

4
, we believe an inequality of this type is also required in the arguments of
[
Ekh07
]
and, for completeness, we include a proof here.

Let
X
be a compact almost Kähler manifold and
K
a union of finitely many balls in
X
. Let
L\subset X
be a Lagrangian submanifold and
r_{0}
the radial injectivity radius of
L
outside
K
. We consider
r_{0}
to be small enough such that outside of
K
, the squared radial distance function
h\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\rho^{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}D_{r_{0}}^{\ast}L\to\mathbb{R}
is strictly plurisubharmonic with respect to
J
. For
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we write
N_{\delta}(K)
for the
\delta
-neighborhood of
K
. The necessary reverse isoperimetric inequality reads as follows:

Theorem A.3
.

In the notation above, there exist a choice of
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and a constant
T\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that any
J
-holomorphic curve

u\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(S,\partial S)\to(X,L\cup K)\quad\mbox{ with energy}\quad\frac{1}{2}\int\mathinner{\!\left\lvert du\right\rvert}^{2}\leq E

has boundary length outside
N_{2\delta}(K)
bounded above by
TE
.

Proof.

At core, the argument uses the coarea formula to slice in terms of the level sets of the distance function
\rho
and then use the Lagrangian boundary condition to study bounds for the length. We have structured the proof in four steps: summarizing, we will choose an appropriate bump function
\beta
around
K
and define functions
a(r)
which coarsely measures the area outside
K
. Then we show that the inequality

ra^{\prime}(r)\geq a(r)\frac{1-Dr}{1+Cr}

for some constants
C,D\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
that only depend on
(X,K,L,r_{0})
but not on
u
. The required bound on length will follows from that.

First, let us construct the support function
\beta\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to\mathbb{R}
, which typically depends on
u
, though the resulting constants
C,D
will not be affected. Fix a reference constant
\delta\in(0,r_{0}/2)
and
\delta_{u}\in(0,\delta)
. We then choose a non-negative smooth function
\beta\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}X\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that

(1)

\beta
vanishes on
N_{\delta_{0}}(K)
,

(2)

\beta
is strictly positive on
N_{\delta_{0}}(K)^{c}-\partial N_{\delta_{0}}(K)
,

(3)

\beta
is equal to
1
outside
N_{2\delta}(K)
.

Choose
\delta_{u}
above generically enough so that
u
is transverse to
\partial N_{\delta}(K)
, and sufficiently close to
\delta
so that the corresponding bump function
\beta
has its derivative bounded above by
C\delta^{-1}
, for some constant
C\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, independent of
\delta_{u}
. The transversality condition is needed because in Step 3 below we will need
u^{-1}(\partial N_{\delta}(K))
to have
finite
length, although its length does not contribute to our inequality.

Set
N\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=N_{\delta_{0}}(K)
for the
\delta_{0}
-neighborhood of
K
and choose a strictly decreasing sequence
\delta_{m}\to\delta_{u}
so that
\rho/\beta
is Lipshitz on the
\delta_{m}
-neighborhood
N_{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=N_{\delta_{m}}(K)
. The functions
a(r)
and
l^{\beta}(r)
are defined, for almost every real number, as follows.

(42)

\displaystyle a(r)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\{\rho\leq\beta r\}\cap u\cap{N^{c}}}dA

\displaystyle a_{m}(r)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\{\rho\leq\beta r\}\cap u\cap N_{m}^{c}}dA

(43)

\displaystyle l^{\beta}(r)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\{\rho=\beta r\}\cap u\cap N^{c}}\beta dl.

\displaystyle l_{m}^{\beta}(r)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=\int_{\{\rho=\beta r\}\cap u\cap N_{m}^{c}}\beta dl.

Here, the integral is taken with respect to the induced metric on the image of
u
. The reason why
a_{m}(r)
and
l_{m}^{\beta}(r)
are introduced is that we may apply the co-area formula. Since the integrand is always non-negative, by the monotone convergence theorem, we can freely replace the integral and limit.

Step 1.
We show that the function
a_{m}(r)
is absolutely continuous and satisfies

(44)

\displaystyle a_{m}(r)\geq\int_{0}^{r}\frac{l_{m}^{\beta}(\tau)}{1+\tau C}d\tau\quad\text{and}\quad a^{\prime}_{m}(r)\geq\frac{l_{m}^{\beta}(r)}{1+rC}\quad\text{ a.e}.

We deduce
Eq.

44
from the coarea formula, as follows. Since
\beta
is positive on the complement
N^{c}
, it follows that
\rho/\beta
is Lipschitz on
N_{m}^{c}
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Applying the coarea formula, we obtain

(45)

\displaystyle a_{m}(r)=\int_{\{\rho\leq\beta r\}\cap u\cap N_{m}^{c}}dA=\int_{\{\rho\leq\beta r\}\cap u\cap N_{m}^{c}}\frac{1}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\nabla(\rho/\beta)\right\rvert}}\cdot{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\nabla(\rho/\beta)\right\rvert}}dA=\int_{0}^{r}\int_{\{\rho=\beta\tau\}\cap u\cap N_{m}^{c}}\frac{1}{\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\nabla(\rho/\beta)\right\rvert}}dld\tau.

Since
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\nabla\rho\right\rvert}=1
, in the region
\rho=\tau\beta
we have the estimate

(46)

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\nabla(\rho/\beta)\right\rvert}\leq\frac{1}{\beta}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\nabla\rho\right\rvert}+\frac{\rho\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\beta^{\prime}\right\rvert}}{\beta^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{\beta}\left(\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\nabla\rho\right\rvert}+\tau\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\beta^{\prime}\right\rvert}\right)\leq\frac{1+\tau C}{\beta}.

Thus (
44
) follows from from (
45
) and (
46
).
Since
a_{m}(r)
is a absolutely continuous non-negative function with non-negative derivative, it follows that
a(r)=\lim_{m\to\infty}a(r)
is also absolutely continuous. Furthermore, by the monotone convergence theorem applied with respect to
\delta_{m}
,

(47)

\displaystyle a(r)\geq\int_{0}^{r}\frac{l^{\beta}(\tau)}{1+\tau C}d\tau\quad\text{and}\quad a^{\prime}(r)\geq\frac{l^{\beta}(r)}{1+rC}\quad\text{ a.e}.

Step 2.
Recall that we denote
h=\rho^{2}
for the square of the distance function to
L
. Let us show the inequality

rl^{\beta}(r)\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\{\rho\leq\beta r\}\cap u\cap N^{c}}dd^{c}h.

The key observation is that, because
\beta
vanishes near
\partial(N_{\delta}(K))
, we can suppress the contribution to the length coming from
u\cap\partial(N_{\delta}(K))
. For almost every
r
, we have the following:

(48)

\displaystyle rl_{m}^{\beta}(r)

\displaystyle=\int_{\{\rho=\beta r\}\cap u\cap N_{m}^{c}}r\beta dl\geq\int_{\{\rho=\beta r\}\cap u\cap N_{m}^{c}}\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla h,\nu\rangle dl

(49)

\displaystyle=\int_{\{\rho=\beta r\}\cap u\cap N_{m}^{c}}\frac{1}{2}d^{c}h=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\{\rho\leq\beta r\}\cap u\cap N_{m}^{c}}dd^{c}h-\int_{\{\rho\leq\beta r\}\cap u\cap\partial N_{m}}d^{c}h.

Here we have parametrized the oriented smooth curve
\{\rho=\beta r\}\cap u\cap N_{m}^{c}\}
via
l(t)
and took its unit normal
\nu(t)=-J(du\circ l(t))\mathinner{\!\left\lvert du\circ l^{\prime}(t)\right\rvert}^{-1}
. We are also using the fact that the critical points of
u
do not lie on
l(t)
for generic
r
. For the inequality in (
48
), we use that
\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\nabla\rho\right\rvert}=1
and, from
\langle\nabla h,\nu\rangle dl
to
d^{c}h
, we used

\displaystyle\langle\nabla h,\nu\rangle dl=\langle\nabla h,\nu\rangle\mathinner{\!\left\lvert du(l^{\prime}(t))\right\rvert}l^{\prime}(t)dt=-l^{\prime}(t)dh\circ(J\circ du\circ\nu(t))=d^{c}h.

The critical points of
u
do not contribute because they are discrete, which is measure
0
. The first equality in (
49
) is more involved. For that, we first use Stokes’ theorem to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\{\rho\leq\beta r\}\cap u\cap N_{m}^{c}}dd^{c}h

\displaystyle=\int_{\{\rho\leq\beta r\}\cap u\cap\partial N_{m}}d^{c}h+\int_{\{\rho\leq\beta r\}\cap\partial u\cap N_{m}^{c}}d^{c}h

(50)

\displaystyle+\int_{\{\rho=\beta r\}\cap u\cap N_{m}^{c}}d^{c}h.

Here,
d^{c}h=0
on
\{\rho\leq\beta r\}\cap\partial u
since this set is contained in
L
. This explains why the second term in (
50
) vanishes. We now need to show that the term
\int_{\{\rho\leq\beta r\}\cap u\cap\partial N_{m}}d^{c}h
converges to zero as
m\to 0
. For that, note that the set is contained in
u^{-1}(\partial N_{m})=u^{-1}(\partial N_{\delta_{m}}(K))
, whose length is uniformly bounded for sufficiently large
m\in\mathbb{N}
. This is the only place where
u
being transverse to
\partial(N_{\delta}(K))
is used. Therefore, we have

(51)

\displaystyle\int_{\{\rho\leq\beta r\}\cap u\cap\partial N_{m}}d^{c}h\leq\int_{\{\rho\leq\beta r\}\cap u\cap N_{m}^{c}}\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla h,\nu\rangle dl\leq\sup_{\partial{N_{\delta_{m}}(K)}}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}r_{0}\cdot\text{Length}(u^{-1}(\partial N_{m}(K))).

Now, since
\sup_{\partial{(N_{\delta_{m}}(K))}}\mathinner{\!\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert}
converges to zero as
m\to\infty
, this term uniformly converges to zero as
m\to\infty
. The remaining term
\frac{1}{2}\int_{\{\rho\leq\beta r\}\cap u\cap N_{m}^{c}}dd^{c}h
in
(
49
)
converges to
\int_{{\rho\leq\beta r}\cap u\cap K^{c}}dd^{c}h
by monotone convergence theorem, since
dd^{c}h
is
J
-plurisubharmonic that the integrand is necessarily non-negative. In consequence, we obtained the claimed inequality

(52)

\displaystyle rl^{\beta}(r)=\lim_{m\to\infty}rl_{m}^{\beta}(r)\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\{\rho\leq\beta r\}\cap u\cap N^{c}}dd^{c}h.

Step 3.
Let us prove the inequality

\frac{1}{2}dd^{c}h\geq(1-Dr)\omega,\quad\mbox{ for some }D\in\mathbb{R}_{+}.

Indeed, for each point
p\in L
, we can trivialize the metric and bring
(X,L,J)
to the configuration
(\mathbb{C}_{(x,y)}^{n},\mathbb{R}_{x}^{n},J)
such that
h=\sum_{i,j}a_{ij}y^{i}y^{j}+O(\mathinner{\!\left\lvert y\right\rvert}^{3})
,
a_{ij}(x)=\delta_{ij}+O(\mathinner{\!\left\lvert x\right\rvert}^{2})
,
J-i=O(\mathinner{\!\left\lvert y\right\rvert})
and
g=g_{std}+O(\mathinner{\!\left\lvert y\right\rvert}^{2})+O(\mathinner{\!\left\lvert x\right\rvert}^{2})
. In particular,
\frac{1}{2}dd^{c}h
restricted to
x=0
is
\omega_{std}+O(\mathinner{\!\left\lvert y\right\rvert})
and
g
restricted to
x=0
is
g_{std}+O(\mathinner{\!\left\lvert y\right\rvert}^{2})
. It follows that
\omega=\omega_{std}+O(\mathinner{\!\left\lvert y\right\rvert})
and so
\omega=dd^{c}h+O(\mathinner{\!\left\lvert y\right\rvert})
. For sufficiently small
y
, it follows that
dd^{c}h\geq(1-Dr)\omega
.

Step 4.
Let us combine the inequalities thus far to show that

ra^{\prime}(r)\geq a(r)\frac{1-Dr}{1+Cr}

and conclude the proof of the truncated reverse isoperimetric inequality. Step 3 implies that
\lim rl^{\beta}(r)\geq(1-Dr)a(r)
for some
D\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Combining (
47
) and (
48
)–(
49
), we obtain the inequality

(53)

\displaystyle ra^{\prime}(r)\geq a(r)\frac{1-Dr}{1+Cr}.

This implies the inequality

(54)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dr}\log\left(a(r)\cdot\frac{Cr+1}{r(1-Dr)}\right)\geq 0,

which implies that the function

r\to{\frac{a(r)}{r(1-Dr)^{-(C+D)/D}}}

is non-decreasing. For
r\ll\min{(D^{-1},C^{-1})}
small enough, choose a constant
T\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
greater than
(1-Dr)^{-(C+D)/D}
so that

(55)

\displaystyle T\frac{a(r)}{r}\geq\lim_{s\to 0}\frac{\text{Area}(u;\{\rho\leq s\}\cap N_{2\delta}(K)^{c})}{s}\frac{Cs+1}{(1-sD)}.

This implies
a(r)T\geq rl
, as required.
∎

A.3.
Local systems and twisted local systems

In
Section

5
we directly work with twisted local systems. Since
[
GPS20
]
is presented only for (untwisted) local systems,
Section

6
is written in the context of local systems. In the framework of Betti Lagrangians, being spin, there is an equivalence between twisted local systems and local systems. To unify both approaches, we briefly set up the non-abelianization functor
\Phi{W}
in terms of spin structures and local systems, instead of twisted local systems. The notation follows that of the manuscript.
Let
\alpha\subset S
be a path transverse to
\EuScript{W}
. The difference line bundle
\beta=\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}-\pi^{\ast}\mathfrak{s}
can be understood as a
O(1;\mathbb{R})
-local system on the trivial bundle over
L-K_{L}
. Such local systems are almost-flat, in that their monodromy along a small loop encircling a point in
K_{L}
is
-Id
.

As in
Section

5.1
, we will define a functor

\Phi^{\textbf{V},\beta}_{\EuScript{W}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mbox{Loc}_{1}(L)\longrightarrow\mbox{Loc}_{n}(S).

We will adopt the same notational conventions as introduced in
Section

5
, using genuine paths. As before, we will write
{\alpha}^{i}
to denote the cotangent lifts of the paths. The analogue of
Definition

5.6
reads as follows.

Definition A.4
.

Let
\textbf{V}\in\mbox{Loc}_{1}(L)
be a
\mbox{GL}_{1}(\mathbb{C})
-local system on
L
and
\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,1]\to{\bf S}
a path transverse to
\EuScript{W}
. Let
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}},\mathfrak{s}
be spin structures on
L
and
S
, respectively, and
\beta
be their difference line bundle.

(i)

If
\alpha
is represented by a free
\EuScript{W}
-adapted path, then

\Phi^{\textbf{V},\beta}_{\EuScript{W}}(\alpha)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Phi^{\textbf{V},\beta}({\alpha}^{i})[{\alpha}^{i}],

where the sum runs over the
n
lifts
{\alpha}^{i}
to
L
,
i\in[1,n].

(ii)

If
\alpha
is short and intersects
\EuScript{W}
at
z
, and
\rho^{u}
the unit velocity sphere bundle lift, then

\Phi^{\textbf{V}\otimes\beta}_{\EuScript{W}}(\alpha)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Phi^{\textbf{V}\otimes\beta}({\alpha}^{i})[{\alpha}^{i}]+\sum_{{\mathfrak{s}(z;\mathfrak{w})}\in\mathfrak{S}(z;\mathfrak{w})}{\mu(\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(z)})}\Phi^{\textbf{V}\otimes\beta}\big(\pi\big(\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(\rho^{u})}))[\pi\big(\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(\rho^{u})}\big)].

(iii)

If
\alpha
is given as a composition
\alpha=\alpha_{1}\circ\alpha_{2}\circ\ldots\circ\alpha_{n}
, where each
\alpha_{i}
is either short or free, then

\Phi^{\textbf{V},\beta}_{\EuScript{W}}(\alpha)=\Phi^{\textbf{V},\beta}_{\EuScript{W}}(\alpha_{1})\circ\Phi^{\textbf{V},\beta}_{\EuScript{W}}(\alpha_{2})\circ\cdots\circ\Phi^{\textbf{V},\beta}_{\EuScript{W}}(\alpha_{k}).

\Box

The spin structure
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}
can be seen as a fibrewise double cover
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathring{L}\to\mathring{L}
. Then the pull-back
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}^{\ast}\textbf{V}
has monodromy
Id
along
H
, and so using
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}
to choose a trivialization
\mathring{L}\simeq\mathring{L}\times U(1)_{\theta}
, and pulling back
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}^{\ast}V
by the
U(1)
-section
\Sigma\to\mathring{L}
, we can regard
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}^{\ast}V
as a local system on
L
. Conversely, given a rank-
1
flat connection
\nabla
on the trivial bundle of
L
, we can use
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}
to trivialize
\mathring{L}\simeq L\times U(1)_{\theta}
and consider the twisted connection
\nabla+\pi id\theta
. The pull-back under the fibrewise square map gives
\nabla+2\pi id\theta
, which has trivial monodromy along the
U(1)
component. The same construction recovers
\nabla
.

Similarly, the spin structure
\mathfrak{s}
trivializes the sphere bundle on
S
. Let
\alpha
be a
\EuScript{W}
-transverse path, and let
\rho^{\mathfrak{s}}(\alpha)
be the lift of
\alpha
via the trivialization
\mathfrak{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathring{S}\simeq S\times U(1)
. By construction,
\rho^{\mathfrak{s}}(\alpha)
is a
\EuScript{W}
-adapted path. We identify the stalk of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}^{\ast}V
, regarded as a local system on
L
, with that of
V
. The relation with
\Phi{W}
in
Section

5
reads:

Proposition A.5
.

\Phi_{\EuScript{W}}(V)(\rho^{\mathfrak{s}}(\alpha))=\Phi^{\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}^{\ast}V,\beta}_{\EuScript{W}}(\alpha)

Proof.

It suffices to verify the formula for free paths and short paths. For free paths,

\displaystyle\Phi^{\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}^{\ast}V\otimes\beta}_{\EuScript{W}}(\alpha)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Phi^{\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}^{\ast}V\otimes\beta}(\alpha^{i})[{\alpha}^{i}]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Phi^{V}(\rho^{\mathfrak{s}}(\alpha)^{i})[{\alpha}^{i}],

since
\beta=\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}-\pi^{\ast}\mathfrak{s}
. For short paths,

\displaystyle{\mu(\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(z)})}\Phi^{\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}^{\ast}\textbf{V}\otimes\beta}\big(\pi\big(\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(\rho^{u})})\big)[\pi\big(\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(\rho^{u})}\big)]={\mu(\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(z)})}\Phi^{\textbf{V}}\big(\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(\rho^{\mathfrak{s}}(\alpha)})\big)[\pi\big(\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}{(\rho(\alpha)}\big)],

since
\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}(\rho^{u})
and
\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}(\rho^{\mathfrak{s}}(\alpha))
differ by
\langle\beta,\partial\mathring{\mathfrak{s}}(\rho^{u})\rangle H
modulo
2H
.
∎

Finally, given a local system
\textbf{V}\in\mbox{Loc}(L)
and
V\in\mbox{Loc}^{\dagger}(L)
a twisted local system on
\mathring{L}
such that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}^{\ast}V=\textbf{V}
, we can define the non-abelianization of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}}^{\ast}V
to be
\mathfrak{s}^{\ast}\Phi{W}(V)
. This is consistent with the main body of the manuscript by
Proposition

A.5
.
